Preface

These notes have been written for a series of lectures to be given at the 44th
Finnish Summer School on Probability and Statistics in Lammi, Finland, from 25th to
29th May, 2026. They contain an introduction to Wiener chaos decomposition in finite
dimension, a construction of Gaussian fields on the torus, including white noise
and the Gaussian free field, and applications to the
\Phi^{4}
model. They do

not
cover other important aspects of the topic, such as stochastic
integration, stochastic PDEs and Malliavin calculus.
Sections with a
*
are more technical, and can safely be skipped in a
first reading.

The material included in these notes is mostly based on the
monograph
[
32
]
, the lecture
notes
[
29
]
, and the monograph
[
7
]
.
Other useful resources on the topic
include
[
33
,
31
,
36
]
.
This is a preliminary version of the notes, that may contain mistakes
and typos. Feel free to let me know if you find any.

Thanks are due to Dario Gasbarra for organising the Summer School and
inviting me to give these lectures, thereby providing the motivation
to compile these notes, as well as supporting
institutions.

Chapter 1
The one-dimensional case

We start this exposition with the very simple situation of a one-dimensional
Gaussian random variables, since this allows us to introduce many objects that will
become important in higher dimension in a relatively simple setting.

1.1
Gaussian random variables

Our fundamental objects are Gaussian random variables, whose definition we recall
here.

{definition}

[Gaussian random variable]
Let
\mathbb{R}
be the real line, equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathscr{B}}

of Borel sets and Lebesgue measure
\dd\!x
. A random variable
X:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
(one-dimensional) Gaussian random variable with mean
m
and variance
\sigma^{2}
if
its law is

\mu(\dd\!x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\e^{-(x-m)^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}\dd\!x\;.

(1.1.1)

In that case, we write
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(m,\sigma^{2})
.

We summarise some fundamental properties of Gaussian random variables as follows.

{proposition}

[Basic properties of normal random variables]

1.

One has
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(m,\sigma^{2})
if, and only if,
X=m+\sigma Y

with
Y\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
.

2.

Assume
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(m_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})
and
Y\sim{\mathscr{N}}(m_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2})

are defined on a common probability space, and let
Z=X+Y
. Then

Z
is Gaussian, with parameters

Z\sim{\mathscr{N}}\bigl(m_{1}+m_{2},\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}+2\cov(X,Y)\bigr)\;.

(1.1.2)

3.

Two Gaussian variables
X
and
Y
are independent if, and only if,
they are uncorrelated, that is,
\cov(X,Y)=\mathbb{E}[XY]-\mathbb{E}[X]\mathbb{E}[Y]=0
.

The first property states that all one-dimensional Gaussian random variables are
equivalent by an affine transformation. The second one states that Gaussian random
variables are stable, and is at the core of the central limit theorem. The third
property is only true for vary special random variables: while independence
always implies non-correlation, the converse is false in general.

Because of the first property, we will almost always focus on the case
\mu=0
,
that is, when
X
is
centered
. Whenever possible, we will assume
\sigma^{2}=1
,
although it will sometimes be useful to allow for different variances.

Our main interest will be expectations of functions of Gaussian random variables.
Assume
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
, and let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then

\mathbb{E}[f(X)]=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)\mu(\dd\!x)\;,

(1.1.3)

provided the integral is absolutely convergent.

{example}

[Laplace transform]

Let
f(x)=\e^{tx}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, using completion of squares
to write

tx-\frac{x^{2}}{2}=-\frac{1}{2}(x-t)^{2}+\frac{t^{2}}{2}\;,

(1.1.4)

we find

\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{tx-x^{2}/2}\frac{\dd\!x}{\sqrt{2\pi}}=\e^{t^{2}/2}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{-(x-t)^{2}/2}\frac{\dd\!x}{\sqrt{2\pi}}=\e^{t^{2}/2}\;.

(1.1.5)

For general functions
f
, explicit expressions of
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]
are not available.
One possible strategy to compute expectations efficiently is to compute expectations
of a set of appropriate basis functions. One choice of basis functions is given by
monomials.

{proposition}

[Moments of Gaussian random variables]

Let
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, one has

\mathbb{E}[X^{n}]=\begin{cases}(n-1)!!&\text{if $n$ is even\;,}\\
0&\text{if $n$ is odd\;,}\end{cases}

(1.1.6)

where

(n-1)!!=\prodop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n/2-1}(2k+1)=1\cdot 3\cdot 5\dots(n-3)(n-1)

(1.1.7)

is the double factorial.

{exercise}

Prove Proposition
1.1
in two different ways:

1.

by using (
1.1.5
);

2.

by showing, using integration by parts, that

\mathbb{E}[X^{n+1}]=n\mathbb{E}[X^{n-1}]\qquad\text{for all $n\mathbin{\geqslant}1$\;.}

(1.1.8)

If
f
admits an entire series expansion

f(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}x^{n}

(1.1.9)

with positive radius of convergnce
R
, then Proposition
1.1

allows to compute

\mathbb{E}[f(X)]=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}a_{n}\mathbb{E}[X^{n}]\;.

(1.1.10)

1.2
Hermite polynomials

Our main workhorse will be Hermite polynomials. In this section, we review
several of their definitions, and how they are related to geometry/liner
algebra, probability theory, analysis, algebra, and combinatorics.

1.2.1
Gram–Schmidt orthogonalisation

One drawback of using moments to compute expectations is that the basis
(X^{n})_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}

is not orthogonal. Here orthogonality is defined with respect to the inner product

\langle f,g\rangle=\mathbb{E}[fg]\;,

(1.2.1)

meaning that random variables are orthogonal if, and only if, they are independent.
Indeed, Propsition
1.1
shows that
X^{n}
and
X^{m}
are orthogonal, according to this definition, if and only if
n+m
is odd.

However, a basis can always be turned into an orthogonal basis by the

Gram–Schmidt procedure
. Let
(v_{n})_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
be an arbitrary basis of a vector
space. Then the Gram–Schmidt procedure provides an orthogonal basis
(u_{n})_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}

defined inductively by
u_{0}=v_{0}
and

u_{n}=v_{n}-\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{\langle v_{n},u_{k}\rangle}{\langle u_{k},u_{k}\rangle}u_{k}\;,\qquad n\mathbin{\geqslant}1\;.

(1.2.2)

This means that
u_{n}
is obtained by subtracting from
v_{n}
its projection
on the plane spanned by the
n-1
first
u_{k}
. It is easy to show by
induction that
u_{n}
is orthogonal to all vectors
u_{0},\dots,u_{n-1}
.
Indeed, the base case
n=0
is trivially true, while for
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
,
one has, for any
\ell\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
,

\langle u_{n},u_{\ell}\rangle=\langle v_{n},v_{\ell}\rangle-\frac{\langle v_{n},u_{\ell}\rangle}{\langle u_{\ell},u_{\ell}\rangle}\langle u_{\ell},u_{\ell}\rangle=0\;.

(1.2.3)

Let us apply this procedure to the basis
(X^{n})_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
, denoting the
resulting orthogonal basis by
(H_{n}(X))_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
. The first steps are

\displaystyle H_{0}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{0}=1\;,

(1.2.4)

\displaystyle H_{1}(X)

\displaystyle=X-\frac{\langle X,1\rangle}{\langle 1,1\rangle}1=X\;,

(1.2.5)

\displaystyle H_{2}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{2}-\frac{\langle X^{2},X\rangle}{\langle X,X\rangle}X-\frac{\langle X^{2},1\rangle}{\langle 1,1\rangle}1=X^{2}-0-\frac{\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]}{\mathbb{E}[1]}1=X^{2}-1\;,

(1.2.6)

\displaystyle H_{3}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{3}-\frac{\langle X^{3},H_{2}(X)\rangle}{\langle H_{2}(X),H_{2}(X)\rangle}H_{2}(X)-\frac{\langle X^{3},X\rangle}{\langle X,X\rangle}X-\frac{\langle X^{3},1\rangle}{\langle 1,1\rangle}1=X^{3}-0-\frac{\mathbb{E}[X^{4}]}{\mathbb{E}[1]}X=X^{3}-3X\;.

(1.2.7)

{exercise}

Show by this method that

H_{4}(X)=X^{4}-6X^{2}+3\;.

(1.2.8)

Check that the random variables
(H_{n}(X))_{0\mathbin{\leqslant}n\mathbin{\leqslant}4}
are mutually
independent.

Table
1.1
shows the first
11
Hermite polynomials.
Clearly, while the Gram–Schmidt procedure does produce an orthogonal basis,
the computations are not efficient. We are thus going to look for more
efficient methods. One of them, which we discuss in the next section, uses the notion of
cumulant expansion
.

n

H_{n}(x)

0

1

1

x

2

x^{2}-1

3

x^{3}-3x

4

x^{4}-6x^{2}+3

5

x^{5}-10x^{3}+15x

6

x^{6}-15x^{4}+45x^{2}-15

7

x^{7}-21x^{5}+105x^{3}-105x

8

x^{8}-28x^{6}+210x^{4}-420x^{2}+105

x^{9}-36x^{7}+378x^{5}-1260x^{3}+945

x^{10}-45x^{8}+630x^{6}-3150x^{4}+472x^{2}-945

Table 1.1:
List of the first Hermite polynomials.

{remark}

[Scaling conventions]

One finds several conventions for Hermite polynomials in the literature.
What we use here are the “probabilists’ Hermite polynomials”.
Another convention, called the “physicists’ Hermite polynomials”,
uses the scaled version

\widetilde{H}_{n}(x)=2^{n/2}H_{n}(\sqrt{2}x)\;.

(1.2.9)

Yet another convention, used in
[
32
]
,
is to multiply
H_{n}(x)
by
1/n!
.

1.2.2
Hermite polynomials and cumulants

{definition}

[Cumulant expansion]
Let
X
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]
exists for all
t

in an open interval
(-\delta,\delta)
, and write

\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\mu_{n}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\;,\qquad\mu_{n}=\mathbb{E}[X^{n}]\;.

(1.2.10)

Then the
cumulant expansion
of
X
is given by

K_{X}(t)=\log\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\kappa_{n}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\;.

(1.2.11)

The coefficients
\mu_{n}
are called
moments
of
X
, while the

\kappa_{n}
are called
cumulants
.

In the case of a Gaussian
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
, the situation is particularly simple.
Indeed, we have by (
1.1.5
)

K_{X}(t)=\log(\e^{t^{2}/2})=\frac{t^{2}}{2}\;,

(1.2.12)

so that the cumulants are given by

\kappa_{n}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $n=2$\;,}\\
0&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(1.2.13)

Consider now the function

G(t,x)=\frac{\e^{tx}}{\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]}=\e^{tx-t^{2}/2}\;.

(1.2.14)

where
t\mathbin{\geqslant}0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

{proposition}

[Generating function]

G
is the generating function of Hermite polynomials, that is

G(t,x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}H_{n}(x)\;.

(1.2.15)

We will proceed in several steps to prove this result. First, we
make an easy observation: if
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
we have

1=\mathbb{E}[G(t,X)]=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X)]\;.

(1.2.16)

Since this is valid for all
t
in
(-\delta,\delta)
, uniqueness of coefficients
of power series shows that

\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X)]=\delta_{n0}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $n=0$\;,}\\
0&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(1.2.17)

In other terms, all
H_{n}(X)
with
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
are centered, and therefore
orthogonal to
H_{0}(X)=1
. The following result is a generalisation of this
observation.

{proposition}

[Orthogonality of the
H_{n}(X)
]

For any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, one has

\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X)H_{m}(X)]=n!\delta_{nm}=\begin{cases}n!&\text{if $n=m$\;,}\\
0&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(1.2.18)

Proof:
.

We compute the expectation of
G(t,X)G(s,X)
in two different ways.
The first way is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[G(t,X)G(s,X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX-t^{2}/2}\e^{sX-s^{2}/2}]

(1.2.19)

\displaystyle=\e^{-(t^{2}+s^{2})/2}\mathbb{E}[\e^{(t+s)X}]

(1.2.20)

\displaystyle=\e^{-(t^{2}+s^{2})/2}\e^{(t+s)^{2}/2}

(1.2.21)

\displaystyle=\e^{ts}

(1.2.22)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{t^{n}s^{n}}{n!}\;.

(1.2.23)

The second way to perform the computation is, using (
1.2.15
),

\mathbb{E}[G(t,X)G(s,X)]=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\sumop\displaylimits_{m\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\frac{s^{m}}{m!}\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X)H_{m}(X)]\;.

(1.2.24)

Comparing the two obtained power series yields the result, by uniqueness
of the coefficients of a series.
∎

This result shows that the
H_{n}(X)
defined via (
1.2.15
)
form
an
orthogonal basis. It remains to show that this basis
is identical with the one obtained by the Gram–Schmidt procedure.
We do this with the following result.

{lemma}

[Recursive relation between Hermite polynomials]

For any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
, one has

H_{n+1}(x)=xH_{n}(x)-H_{n}^{\prime}(x)\;.

(1.2.25)

Proof:
.

This follows from the relation

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}G(t,x)=(x-t)G(t,x)=xG(t,x)-\frac{\partial}{\partial x}G(t,x)\;.

(1.2.26)

Indeed, we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}G(t,x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}1}\frac{t^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}H_{n}(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}H_{n+1}(x)\;,

(1.2.27)

while

xG(t,x)-\frac{\partial}{\partial x}G(t,x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\bigl(xH_{n}(x)-H_{n}^{\prime}(x)\bigr)\;.

(1.2.28)

Comparing the coefficients of the last two power series yields the
result.
∎

The recursive relation (
1.2.25
) provides a quicker
way to compute Hermite polynomials than the Gram–Schmidt procedure,
starting with
H_{0}(x)=1
. It also allows us to complete the proof
of Proposition
1.2.2
.

Proof of Proposition
1.2.2
.

We have already shown that the
(H_{n})_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
form an orthogonal
family. It remains to show that they coïncide with the polynomials
constructed by the Gram–Schmidt procedure.
Evaluating (
1.2.15
) at
t=0
yields

1=G(0,x)=H_{0}(x)
. From (
1.2.25
), it follows
by induction on
n
that
H_{n}(x)
has degree
n
, with
x^{n}
having
coefficient
1
, which is also the case for the
H_{n}
obtained
via the Gram–Schmidt procedure.
∎

Another useful consequence of the expression (
1.2.15
) of
the generating function is the following generalisation of Proposition
1.2.2
, which allows to
transform products of Hermite functions into sums of such functions.
One can think of it as an analogue of product-sum formulas in trigonometry,
which are useful to compute Fourier series.

{proposition}

[Product–sum formula]

For any
n,m\mathbin{\geqslant}0
, one has

H_{n}(x)H_{m}(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{n\wedge m}p!\binom{n}{p}\binom{m}{p}H_{n+m-2p}(x)\;,

(1.2.29)

where
n\wedge m
denotes the minimum of
n
and
m
.

Proof:
.

We start by observing that

\displaystyle G(t,x)G(s,x)

\displaystyle=\e^{(t+s)x-(t^{2}+s^{2})/2}

(1.2.30)

\displaystyle=\e^{ts}\e^{(t+s)x-(t+s)^{2}/2}

(1.2.31)

\displaystyle=\e^{ts}G(t+s,x)\;.

(1.2.32)

Expanding the exponential and
G(t+s,x)
, and using Newton’s binomial
formula yields

\displaystyle G(t,x)G(s,x)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(ts)^{p}}{p!}\sumop\displaylimits_{q=0}^{\infty}\frac{(t+s)^{q}}{q!}H_{q}(x)

(1.2.33)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{\infty}\sumop\displaylimits_{q=0}^{\infty}\sumop\displaylimits_{r=0}^{q}\frac{t^{p+r}s^{p+q-r}}{p!r!(q-r)!}H_{q}(x)\;.

(1.2.34)

On the other hand, we have

G(t,x)G(s,x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\sumop\displaylimits_{m\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\frac{s^{m}}{m!}H_{n}(x)H_{m}(x)\;.

(1.2.35)

The result follows by determining the coefficient of
t^{n}s^{m}

in (
1.2.34
), which is obtained by summing over
all triples
(p,q,r)
such that
p+r=n
and
p+q-r=m
.
∎

Note that when taking the expectation on both sides
of (
1.2.29
) when
x=X
, we
recover the orthogonalilty relation (
1.2.18
).

{exercise}

Use (
1.2.29
) to write
H_{4}(X)^{2}
as a sum
of Hermite polynomials, and compute
\mathbb{E}[H_{4}(X)^{3}]
.

1.2.3
Hermite polynomials and differential operators

Lemma
1.2.2
has revealed a link between Hermite
polynomials and differential operators. To make this connection more
precise, we indroduce the linear operators

a=\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\;,\qquad a^{\dagger}=x-\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\;,\qquad{\mathscr{L}}=-a^{\dagger}a=\frac{\dd\!^{2}}{\dd\!x^{2}}-x\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}

(1.2.36)

acting on
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
functions in the Hilbert
space
{\mathscr{H}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu(\dd\!x))
, where
\mu(\dd\!x)
is the Gaussian
measure

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\e^{-x^{2}/2}\dd\!x\;.

(1.2.37)

The notation
a^{\dagger}
is motivated by the following result.

{lemma}

The operators
a
and
a^{\dagger}
are mutually adjoint, while
{\mathscr{L}}

is self-adjoint in
{\mathscr{H}}
. Furthermore,

aa^{\dagger}-a^{\dagger}a=\id\;.

(1.2.38)

Proof:
.

An elegant proof of the first claim consists in rewriting
a
and
a^{\dagger}
as

(af)(x)=\e^{x^{2}/2}\biggl(x+\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\biggr)\bigl(\e^{-x^{2}/2}f(x)\bigr)\;,\qquad(a^{\dagger}f)(x)=-\e^{x^{2}/2}\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\bigl(\e^{-x^{2}/2}f(x)\bigr)\;.

(1.2.39)

One indeed checks that this is equivalent to (
1.2.36
) by applying
Leibniz’ rule. Now for any
f,g\in{\mathscr{H}}
, integration by parts gives

\displaystyle\langle a^{\dagger}f,g\rangle

\displaystyle=-\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\bigl(\e^{-x^{2}/2}f(x)\bigr)g(x)\frac{\dd\!x}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

(1.2.40)

\displaystyle=\e^{-x^{2}/2}f(x)g(x)\biggr|_{-\infty}^{\infty}+\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\e^{-x^{2}/2}f(x)g^{\prime}(x)\frac{\dd\!x}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

(1.2.41)

\displaystyle=\langle f,ag\rangle\;,

(1.2.42)

since the boundary term vanishes because
\langle f,g\rangle<\infty
by
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. As a consequence, we also have

-\langle f,{\mathscr{L}}g\rangle=\langle f,a^{\dagger}ag\rangle=\langle af,ag\rangle=\langle a^{\dagger}af,g\rangle=-\langle{\mathscr{L}}f,g\rangle\;,

(1.2.43)

which implies that
{\mathscr{L}}
is self-adjoint. Finally,

(aa^{\dagger}f)(x)=\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!x}\bigl(xf(x)-f^{\prime}(x)\bigr)=f(x)+xf^{\prime}(x)-f^{\prime\prime}(x)=(a^{\dagger}af)(x)+f(x)\;,

(1.2.44)

which proves (
1.2.38
).
∎

The link with Hermite polynomials is as follows.

{corollary}

[Hermite polynomials and differential operators]

The Hermite polynomials are eigenfunctions of
{\mathscr{L}}
. More precisely,

({\mathscr{L}}H_{n})(x)=-nH_{n}(x)\qquad\forall n\mathbin{\geqslant}0\;.

(1.2.45)

Furthermore, one has

a^{\dagger}H_{n-1}=H_{n}\;,\qquad aH_{n}=nH_{n-1}\qquad\forall n\mathbin{\geqslant}1\;.

(1.2.46)

Proof:
.

First note that the first relation in (
1.2.46
) is
just a rewriting of (
1.2.25
).
This allows us to prove (
1.2.45
) by induction on
n
.
For
n=0
, we clearly have
{\mathscr{L}}H_{0}(x)=0
, proving the base case.
Assuming (
1.2.45
) holds for some
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
,
(
1.2.38
) yields

-{\mathscr{L}}H_{n+1}=a^{\dagger}aa^{\dagger}H_{n}=a^{\dagger}(a^{\dagger}a+\id)H_{n}=(n+1)a^{\dagger}H_{n}=(n+1)H_{n+1}\;.

(1.2.47)

Finally, we also have

aH_{n+1}=aa^{\dagger}H_{n}=(a^{\dagger}a+\id)H_{n}=(n+1)H_{n}\;,

(1.2.48)

which proves the second relation in (
1.2.46
).
∎

Note that this result provides another proof of orthogonality of Hermite
polynomials, since eigenfunctions of a self-adjoint operator
corresponding to different eigenvalues are known to be orthogonal.
The second relation in (
1.2.46
) can also be written

H_{n}^{\prime}(x)=nH_{n-1}(x)\;.

(1.2.49)

Together with the recursive relation (
1.2.25
), this yields

H_{n+1}(x)=xH_{n}(x)-nH_{n-1}(x)\qquad\forall n\geq 1\;.

(1.2.50)

Yet another relation, following from the
representation (
1.2.39
) of
a^{\dagger}
is

H_{n}(x)=((a^{\dagger})^{n}H_{0})(x)=(-1)^{n}\e^{x^{2}/2}\frac{\dd\!^{n}}{\dd\!x^{n}}(\e^{-x^{2}/2})\;.

(1.2.51)

The operator
{\mathscr{L}}
defined in (
1.2.36
) occurs in several applications.
In particular, define the
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process
as the solution of the
stochastic differential equation

\dd\!x_{t}=-x_{t}\dd\!t+\sqrt{2}\dd\!W_{t}\;,

(1.2.52)

which can be written in terms of an Itô integral as

x_{t}=x_{0}\e^{-t}+\sqrt{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{-(t-s)}\dd\!W_{s}\;,

(1.2.53)

where
(W_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
is a standard Brownian motion.
Then
{\mathscr{L}}
is the infinitesimal generator of the process, meaning that
for any sufficiently regular test function
f
, one has

\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!t}\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mux_{0}}\!\left[f(x_{t})\right]\Bigr|_{t=0}=({\mathscr{L}}f)(x_{0})\;.

(1.2.54)

This is a consequence of Itô’s formula.

There also is a connection with quantum physics. Indeed,
one finds that the conjugated operator

H=\e^{-x^{2}/4}{\mathscr{L}}\e^{x^{2}/4}

(1.2.55)

has the expression

(Hf)(x)=\biggl(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{x^{2}}{4}\biggr)f(x)+f^{\prime\prime}(x)\;,

(1.2.56)

which is equivalent, up to a scaling, to the Hamiltonian of the
quantum harmonic oscillator. The operator
H
is self-adjoint in

L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\dd\!x)
, and its eigenfunctions are conjugated to the Hermite
polynomials. As the operators
a^{\dagger}
and
a
allow to move
between eigenfunctions, and these eigenfunctions are interpreted
as
n
-particle states in quantum field theory, they as known as

creation operator
and
annihilation operator
.

1.2.4
Convolution algebra*

Some of the above computations required to perform operations on
power series, such as multiplication, division, and taking the logarithm.
There exists an algebraic framework that makes these computations
particularly easy.

Let
\mathbb{R}[x]
denote the vector space of polynomials in one variable
x
,
with the canonical basis
\{x^{n}\}_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
. This is also an algebra
for the usual product

x^{n}\cdot x^{m}=x^{n+m}\;.

(1.2.57)

Let
\mathbb{R}[[t]]
be the space of formal power series, that is, expressions
of the form

\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\varphi_{n}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\;,\qquad\varphi_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\;,

(1.2.58)

endowed with pointwise multiplication.
By formal we mean that at this point, we are not concerned about
convergence of the series.

We can view the coefficients
\varphi_{n}
as the images of the
x^{n}
by
a linear map
\varphi:\mathbb{R}[x]\to\mathbb{R}
. Denoting by
{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})
the
space of all these maps, we can define a linear map

\displaystyle\Lambda\colon{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}[[t]]

(1.2.59)

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\varphi(x^{n})\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\;.

(1.2.60)

This map associates a power series with the map
\varphi
defining its coefficients.
The interest of this construction is that due to the Cauchy product formula,
multiplication of power series is equivalent to a convolution operation
of maps. More precisely, for two maps
\varphi,\psi\in{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})
,
define a map
\varphi*\psi\in{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})
by

(\varphi*\psi)(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\varphi(x^{k})\psi(x^{n-k})\;.

(1.2.61)

We then have the following result.

{theorem}

[Isomorphism between convolution algebra and algebra of
power series]

The map
\Lambda
is an isomorpism between
{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})
and
\mathbb{R}[[t]]
.

Proof:
.

By the Cauchy product formula, for any
\varphi,\psi\in{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\Lambda(\varphi)(t)\Lambda(\psi)(t)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\varphi(x^{n})\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\biggr)\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\psi(x^{n})\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\biggr)

(1.2.62)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{p}\frac{\varphi(x^{k})}{k!}\frac{\psi(x^{p-k})}{(p-k)!}\biggr)t^{p}

(1.2.63)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p\mathbin{\geqslant}0}(\varphi*\psi)(x^{p})\frac{t^{p}}{p!}

(1.2.64)

\displaystyle=\Lambda(\varphi*\psi)(t)\;.

(1.2.65)

This shows that
\Lambda
is indeed an algebra morphism. Bijectivity follows
from uniqueness of the coefficients of power series.
∎

This result allows us to work with convolution of linear maps instead
of multiplication of power series.
More generally, we define the
p
-fold convolution by

\varphi^{\ast p}(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\mathbin{\geqslant}0\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{p}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{p}!}\varphi(x^{n_{1}})\dots\varphi(x^{n_{p}})\;.

(1.2.66)

It till turn out to be useful to work with linear instead of multilinear maps.
This is achieved by introducing a linear map
\Delta:\mathbb{R}[x]\to\mathbb{R}[x]\otimes\mathbb{R}[x]
,
given by

\Delta(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}x^{k}\otimes x^{n-k}\;.

(1.2.67)

Here the tensor product
\mathbb{R}[x]\otimes\mathbb{R}[x]
is the vector space spanned
by all
x^{n}\otimes x^{m}
with
n,m\mathbin{\geqslant}0
. Indeed (
1.2.67
) allows us
to rewrite the convolution product (
1.2.61
) as

\varphi\ast\psi={\mathscr{M}}(\varphi\otimes\psi)\Delta\;,

(1.2.68)

where
{\mathscr{M}}
denotes the multiplication map,
{\mathscr{M}}(a\otimes b)=ab
.
More generally, for
p\mathbin{\geqslant}3
we set

\Delta^{(p-1)}(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\mathbin{\geqslant}0\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{p}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{p}!}x^{n_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes x^{n_{p}}\;.

(1.2.69)

Writing
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{p})=a_{1}\dots a_{p}
,
the
n
-fold convolution product becomes

\varphi^{\ast p}={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\varphi^{\otimes p})\Delta^{(p-1)}\;.

(1.2.70)

{remark}

[Hopf algebra]

The space
\mathbb{R}[x]
endowed with the maps
\cdot
,
\Delta
, and a

counit

\mathbf{1}^{\star}:\mathbb{R}[x]\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
\mathbf{1}^{\star}(x^{n})=\delta_{n0}

is a so-called
bi-algebra
. When adding a linear map
{\mathscr{A}}

defined by
{\mathscr{A}}(x^{n})=(-1)^{n}x^{n}
and called
antipode
, it
becomes a
Hopf algebra
. The map
\Delta
is called a

co-product
, because it enjoys a property called

co-associativity
, saying that applying

\Delta
to the left or to the right of the tensor product
in
\Delta(x^{n})
yields the same result, namely

\Delta^{(2)}(x^{n})=(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x^{n})=(\id\otimes\Delta)\Delta(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\mathbin{\geqslant}0\\
n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!n_{2}!n_{3}!}x^{n_{1}}\otimes x^{n_{2}}\otimes x^{n_{3}}

(1.2.71)

and similarly for higher powers.

Let us now introduce two special subsets of
{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})
, given by

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\varphi\in{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})\colon\varphi(1)=1\bigr\}\;,

(1.2.72)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\varphi\in{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R})\colon\varphi(1)=0\bigr\}\;.

(1.2.73)

Elements of
{\mathscr{L}}_{1}
can be inverted, via the Neumann series

\varphi^{-1}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\infty}(\mathbf{1}^{\star}-\varphi)^{\ast k}\;.

(1.2.74)

One has explicitly

\varphi^{-1}(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\mathbin{\geqslant}1\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{k}!}\varphi(x^{n_{1}})\dots\varphi(x^{n_{k}})\;.

(1.2.75)

{exercise}

Check that one has indeed

(\varphi\ast\varphi^{-1})(x^{n})=\delta_{n0}
for all
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
.

Hints:
Recall that
\varphi(1)=1
, and therefore
\varphi^{-1}(1)=1
.
Check the cases
n\in\{0,1,2\}
first.
For the general case, the term
k=0
in (
1.2.61
)
should be treated separately.

We can now define an exponential map
\exp_{\ast}:{\mathscr{L}}_{0}\to{\mathscr{L}}_{1}
and its inverse
\log_{\ast}:{\mathscr{L}}_{1}\to{\mathscr{L}}_{0}
by

\exp_{\ast}(\varphi)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{1}{k!}\varphi^{\ast k}\;,\qquad\log_{\ast}(\varphi)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}(\varphi-\mathbf{1}^{*})^{\ast k}\;.

(1.2.76)

There is no issue of convergence, since the sums are always finite when evaluated on a basis element. In fact,

\displaystyle\exp_{\ast}(\varphi)(x^{n})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{k!}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\mathbin{\geqslant}1\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{k}!}\varphi(x^{n_{1}})\dots\varphi(x^{n_{k}})\;,

(1.2.77)

\displaystyle\log_{\ast}(\varphi)(x^{n})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=1}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\mathbin{\geqslant}1\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{k}!}\varphi(x^{n_{1}})\dots\varphi(x^{n_{k}})\;.

(1.2.78)

The following corollary of Theorem
1.2.4

clarifies the link between these objects and the usual inverse,
exponential and logarithm.

{proposition}

For any
\varphi\in{\mathscr{L}}_{0}
and
\psi\in{\mathscr{L}}_{1}
, one has the relations

\displaystyle\Lambda(\psi^{-1})(t)

\displaystyle=\bigl[\Lambda(\psi)(t)\bigr]^{-1}\;,

(1.2.79)

\displaystyle\Lambda(\exp_{\ast}\varphi)(t)

\displaystyle=\exp(\Lambda(\varphi)(t))\;,

(1.2.80)

\displaystyle\Lambda(\log_{\ast}\psi)(t)

\displaystyle=\log(\Lambda(\psi)(t))\;.

(1.2.81)

Proof:
.

We prove the second relation. Setting
\psi=\exp_{\ast}\varphi
, we have

\Lambda(\psi)(t)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{1}{k!}\Lambda(\varphi^{\ast k})(t)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{1}{k!}\Lambda(\varphi)(t)^{k}=\exp(\Lambda(\varphi)(t))\;.

(1.2.82)

The other relations are proved in a similar way.
∎

Returning to the topic of Hermite polynomials, we consider the
special case where
X
is a real-valued random variable, admitting
exponential moments, and associate with it the linear map

\mu_{X}:\mathbb{R}[x]\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\mu_{X}(x^{n})=\mathbb{E}[X^{n}]\;.

(1.2.83)

Note that
\mu_{X}\in{\mathscr{L}}_{1}
, since
\mathbb{E}[1]=1
. The associated power series

\Lambda(\mu_{X})(t)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\mathbb{E}[X^{n}]=\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]

(1.2.84)

is the
moment generating function
of
X
introduced in (
1.2.10
).
Proposition
1.2.4
implies that the cumulant generating function
(cf. (
1.2.11
)) can be written as

K_{X}(t)=\log\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]=\Lambda(\log_{\ast}\mu_{X})(t)=\Lambda(\kappa_{X})(t)\;,

(1.2.85)

where

\kappa_{X}=\log_{\ast}\mu_{X}\;,\qquad\mu_{X}=\exp_{\ast}\kappa_{X}\;.

(1.2.86)

Applying (
1.2.77
) and (
1.2.78
) implies

\displaystyle\mu_{X}(x^{n})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{k!}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\mathbin{\geqslant}1\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{k}!}\kappa_{X}(x^{n_{1}})\dots\kappa_{X}(x^{n_{k}})\;,

(1.2.87)

\displaystyle\kappa_{X}(x^{n})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=1}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\mathbin{\geqslant}1\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{k}!}\mu_{X}(x^{n_{1}})\dots\mu_{X}(x^{n_{k}})\;.

(1.2.88)

These are the so-called
Leonov–Shiraev moment-cumulant relations
.
We now define the
Wick exponential
associated to
X
as the
linear map
\W:\mathbb{R}[x]\to\mathbb{R}[x]
given by

\W=(\mu_{X}^{-1}\otimes\id)\Delta=(\exp_{\ast}(-\kappa_{X})\otimes\id)\Delta\;.

(1.2.89)

One easily checks that
\W(1)=1
, while for
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
,
(
1.2.75
) and (
1.2.77
) imply

\W(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{j}\mathbin{\geqslant}1\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{(n-k)!n_{1}!\dots n_{j}!}\kappa_{X}(x^{n_{1}})\dots\kappa_{X}(x^{n_{j}})x^{n-k}\;.

(1.2.90)

Moreover, we have an explicit expression for the inverse of
\W
.

{lemma}

The inverse of
\W
is the map

\W^{-1}=(\mu_{X}\otimes\id)\Delta\;.

(1.2.91)

Proof:
.

For a linear map
g:\mathbb{R}[x]\to\mathbb{R}
, write

M^{g}(x^{n})=(g\otimes\id)\Delta(x^{n})\;.

(1.2.92)

Note that for any linear form
f:\mathbb{R}[x]\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\langle f,M^{g}(x^{n})\rangle=(g\otimes f)\Delta(x^{n})=:\langle g\circ f,x^{n}\rangle\;,

(1.2.93)

where we use an “inner product” notation for the action of

f
for clarity.
It follows that if
h:\mathbb{R}[x]\to\mathbb{R}
is yet another linear form, then

\langle f,M^{h}M^{g}(x^{n})\rangle=\langle h\circ f,M^{g}(x^{n})\rangle=\langle g\circ h\circ f,x^{n}\rangle=\langle f,M^{g\circ h}(x^{n})\rangle\;.

(1.2.94)

In short,
M^{h}M^{g}=M^{g\circ h}
. Now we observe that

\W=M^{\mu_{X}^{-1}}
and
\W^{-1}=M^{\mu_{X}}
. Therefore

\W\W^{-1}(x^{n})=M^{\mu_{X}\circ\mu_{X}^{-1}}(x^{n})=\bigl((\mu_{X}\circ\mu_{X}^{-1})\otimes\id\bigr)\Delta(x^{n})\;,

(1.2.95)

where

\displaystyle(\mu_{X}\circ\mu_{X}^{-1})(x^{k})

\displaystyle=(\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{X}^{-1})\Delta(x^{k})

(1.2.96)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}\mu_{X}(x^{\ell})\mu_{X}^{-1}(x^{k-\ell})

(1.2.97)

\displaystyle=(\mu_{X}\ast\mu_{X}^{-1})(x^{k})

(1.2.98)

\displaystyle=\delta_{k0}\;.

(1.2.99)

This implies
(\mu_{X}\circ\mu_{X}^{-1}\otimes\id)\Delta(x^{n})=x^{n}

for all
n
, proving that
\W^{-1}
is indeed the inverse of
\W
.
∎

The following result shows that
\W(t,x):=\Lambda(\W)(t)
is nothing but the
generating function that we encountered in (
1.2.14
).

{proposition}

One has the relation

\W(t,x)=\frac{\e^{tx}}{\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]}=\e^{tx-K_{X}(t)}\;.

(1.2.100)

Proof:
.

Observe that

\W(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\mu_{X}^{-1}(x^{k})x^{n-k}=(\mu_{X}^{-1}\ast\id)(x^{n})\;.

(1.2.101)

Therefore, Theorem (
1.2.4
) implies

\Lambda(\W)(t)=\Lambda(\mu_{X}^{-1}\ast\id)(t)=\Lambda(\mu_{X}^{-1})(t)\Lambda(\id)(t)=\frac{\Lambda(\id)(t)}{\Lambda(\mu_{X})(t)}=\frac{\e^{tx}}{\mathbb{E}[\e^{tX}]}\;,

(1.2.102)

where we have used Proposition
1.2.4
and (
1.2.84
).
∎

So far, the construction works for any random variable
X
with exponential
moments. Let us now particularise to the case
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
. Since

\kappa_{X}(x^{n})=\delta_{n2}
, cf. (
1.2.13
), all
n_{i}

in the first Leonov–Shiraev relation (
1.2.87
) must have value
2
.
This is only possible if
n
is even and
k=n/2
. Therefore, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[X^{2k}]=\mu_{X}(x^{2k})=\frac{(2k)!}{k!2^{k}}\;.

(1.2.103)

This is equal to
(k-1)!!
, as one checks by separating even and odd factors
in
(2k)!
. We thus recover Proposition
1.1
.
In addition, we obtain the following explicit expressions for
Hermite polynomials, as well as the inverse relations between monomials
and Hermite polynomials.

{proposition}

[Explicit expression of Hermite polynomials]

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, one has

H_{n}(x)=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{k}k!(n-2k)!}x^{n-2k}\;.

(1.2.104)

The inverse relation is given by

x^{n}=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{1}{2^{k}k!(n-2k)!}H_{n-2k}(x)\;.

(1.2.105)

Proof:
.

Since

\W(t,x)=\Lambda(\W)(t)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\W(x^{n})\frac{t^{n}}{n!}

(1.2.106)

by the definition (
1.2.59
) of
\Lambda
, we have
H_{n}(x)=\W(x^{n})
. We can thus apply (
1.2.90
) when all
n_{i}
are
equal to
2
. This is only possible if
2j=k
, and the result
follows by the index shift
k\mapsto k/2
.
The inverse relation (
1.2.105
) follows in the same way
from the expression (
1.2.91
) for
\W^{-1}
.
∎

1.2.5
Hermite polynomials and combinatorics

The coefficients of Hermite polynomials also have a nice combinatorial
interpretation. Given a finite set
E_{n}
of cardinal
n
, say

E_{n}=[\![1,n]\!]:=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, we will call
pairwise matching
,
or
pairing
, a partition of
E_{n}
into sets of cardinality
1
or
2
,
the former being called
singletons
and
the latter being called
pairs
.

{proposition}

[Combinatorial interpretation of Hermite polynomials]
Let
0\mathbin{\leqslant}2k\mathbin{\leqslant}n
.
The coefficient of
x^{n-2k}
of
H_{n}(x)
is equal to the number of
pairwise matchings of
E_{n}
with
k
pairs.

Proof:
.

Given a pairwise matching of
E_{n}
with
k
pairs, we associate to each
singleton the label
x
, and to each pair the label
-1
. The value
of this matching is defined as the product of all labels, namely

(-1)^{k}x^{n-2k}\;,

(1.2.107)

see Figure
1.1
for an example.
We claim that
H_{n}(x)
is equal to the sum of the values of all pairwise
matchings of
E_{n}
.

We proceed by induction, taking
n=1
as base case. Then the only
pairwise matching is
\{\{1\}\}
, which has value
x=H_{1}(x)
.

Assume now that
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
. The pairwise matchings of
E_{n+1}
are of two
types. The first type are those in which
\{n+1\}
is a singleton.
These have value
xH_{n}(x)
by induction hypothesis. The second type of
pairings are those in which
n+1
belongs to a pair. There are
n
choices
for the partner of
n+1
, and for each of these choices, the value of
the matching of the remaining elements is
H_{n-1}(x)
. This shows that

H_{n+1}(x)=xH_{n}(x)-nH_{n-1}(x)\;,

(1.2.108)

which is exactly the recurrence relation (
1.2.50
).
∎

x
x
x
x
x
x
-1
x
x
-1
x
x
-1
x
x
-1
x
x
-1
x
x
-1
-1
-1
-1
-1
-1
-1

Figure 1.1:
Pairwise matchings of
E_{4}=\{1,2,3,4\}
.
There is one matching with no pairs, of value
x^{4}
.
There are
6
matchings with one pair, of total value
-6x^{2}
,
and
3
matchings with two pairs, of total value
3
.
The sum of all values is
x^{4}-6x^{2}+3=H_{4}(x)
.

The number of pairwise matchings can also be computed exactly,
which yields the following alternative proof of the first relation in
Proposition
1.2.4
.

{proposition}

[Explicit expression of Hermite polynomials]
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, one has

H_{n}(x)=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{k}k!(n-2k)!}x^{n-2k}\;.

(1.2.109)

Proof:
.

It is sufficient to count the number of matchings with
k
pairs.
There are
\binom{n}{n-2k}
choices for the
n-2k
singletons.
The number of pairings of the remaining
2k
elements is

(2k-1)!!=\frac{(2k)!}{k!2^{k}}\;.

(1.2.110)

This is because the first element has
2k-1
possible partners. Having
chosen the first part, it remains to pair
2k-2
elements, so that the
claim follows by induction. Multiplying the two numbers gives the
claimed coefficient of
x^{n-2k}
.
∎

Pairings appear at other places in computations with Hermite polynomials.
For instance, Proposition
1.2.2
yields

H_{4}(x)^{2}=H_{8}(x)+4^{2}H_{6}(x)+2!6^{2}H_{4}(x)+3!4^{2}H_{2}(x)+4!H_{0}(x)\;.

(1.2.111)

The right-hand side can be represented graphically by

\,\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox to23.62pt{\vbox to17.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-8.5359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

(1.2.112)

where the numerical coefficients count the number of ways to pair
2p
legs
from the two different diagrams having
4
legs each.

1.3
Wiener chaos expansion

Recall that we have introduced the Hilbert space

{\mathscr{H}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu(\dd\!x))\;,

(1.3.1)

which consists in random variables of the form
f(X)
which have a finite
variance, with
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
. We begin with a preparatory lemma,
which is a special case of
[
32
, Lemma 1.1.1]
.

{lemma}

The random variables
\{\e^{tX}\colon t\in\mathbb{R}\}
form a total subset of
{\mathscr{H}}
.

Proof:
.

Let
Z\in{\mathscr{H}}
be such that
\mathbb{E}[Z\e^{tX}]=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We have
to show that
Z=0
. Define a signed measure
\nu
by

\nu(B)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z\mathbbm{1}_{B}(X)\bigr]

(1.3.2)

for any Borel set
B
of
\mathbb{R}
. The fact that
\mathbb{E}[Z\e^{tX}]=0

means that the Laplace transform of
\nu

is identically zero on
\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, this measure
is zero, so that
\mathbb{E}[Z\mathbbm{1}_{B}]=0
for any Borel set
B
.
This implies that
Z
is indeed equal to
0
.
∎

{definition}

[Wiener chaos]
For any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
, we denote by
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
the one-dimensional subspace of
{\mathscr{H}}

spanned by the random variable
H_{n}(X)
. For
n=0
,
{\mathscr{H}}_{0}
is the set
of constants, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Then
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
is called the

homogeneous Wiener chaos of order
n
.
The
inhomogeneous Wiener chaos of order
n
is defined as

{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathbin{\leqslant}n}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}{\mathscr{H}}_{k}\;.

(1.3.3)

Note that Proposition
1.2.2
implies that the subspaces

{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
are mutually orthogonal.

The main theorem is then as follows (this is a particular case of
[
32
, Theorem 1.1.1]
:

{theorem}

[Wiener chaos decomposition]

The Hilbert space
{\mathscr{H}}
can be decomposed into the infinite orthogonal sum
of the subspaces
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
:

{\mathscr{H}}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathscr{H}}_{n}\;.

(1.3.4)

Proof:
.

Let
Z\in{\mathscr{H}}
be orthogonal to
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
for all
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
. This means that

\mathbb{E}[ZH_{n}(X)]=0\qquad\text{for all $n\mathbin{\geqslant}0$\;.}

(1.3.5)

By (
1.2.105
),
X^{n}
can be expressed as a linear
combination of
H_{k}(X)
, with
0\mathbin{\leqslant}k\mathbin{\leqslant}n
. Therefore, we also have

\mathbb{E}[ZX^{n}]=0\qquad\text{for all $n\mathbin{\geqslant}0$\;.}

(1.3.6)

This implies that
\mathbb{E}[Z\e^{tX}]=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. By
Lemma
1.3
, this means that
Z=0
, which completes
the proof.
∎

In a sense, this theorem is quite remarkable, because
f
being in

L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu(\dd\!x))
is a rather weak requirement. In particular,
f

needs not be continuous, nor does it need to be bounded. We started this
chapter by remarking that
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]
can be computed if
f
admits
a power series expansion, with strictly positive radius of convergence,
see (
1.1.10
). Theorem
1.3
shows that
this assumption of
f
is not at all necessary.

{remark}

If
f\in{\mathscr{H}}
, we have

f(X)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(X)H_{n}(X)]H_{n}(X)\;.

(1.3.7)

Taking expectations shows that

\mathbb{E}[f(X)]=\mathbb{E}[f(X)H_{0}(X)]\;,

(1.3.8)

which does not yield any new information. However, in applications
f(X)
often
admits a more or less explicit decomposition of the
form (
1.3.7
), which makes it possible to compute
expectations.

{exercise}

Consider the function

f(X)=\e^{-\lambda H_{4}(X)}

(1.3.9)

where
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
. Our aim is to obtain an asymptotic expansion

\mathbb{E}[f(X)]\asymp\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}a_{n}\lambda^{n}\;,

(1.3.10)

which means that

\mathbb{E}[f(X)]=\sumop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{N}a_{n}\lambda^{n}+{\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{N+1})

(1.3.11)

for any
N\mathbin{\geqslant}1
.

•

Compute explicitly
a_{0}
,
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
and
a_{3}
.

•

Give a combinatorial interpretation of
a_{n}
for any
n

(in terms of certain types of graphs).

•

Find an upper bound
b_{n}
for
\lvert a_{n}\rvert
. What is the
radius of convergence of the series
\sumop\displaylimits_{n}b_{n}\lambda^{n}
?

{exercise}

Consider the function

f(X)=\e^{-\lambda H_{2}(X)}

(1.3.12)

where
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
.

•

Compute
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]
explicitly.

•

What is the radius of convergence of the expansion of
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]

into powers of
\lambda
?

•

Give a combinatorial interpretation of the
n
th coefficient
of this series. Check this for the first values of
n
.

Chapter 2
The multi-dimensional case

In this chapter, we extend results from the previous chapter to
n
-dimensional
Gaussian random variables.

2.1
Wick calculus

2.1.1
Multivariate Gaussian random variables

We start by a quick recapitulation of basic properties of
N
-dimensional
Gaussian random variables.

{definition}

[Multivariate Gaussian]
For
N\mathbin{\geqslant}1
, let
\mathbb{R}^{N}
be equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathscr{B}}
of Borel
sets and Lebesgue measure
\dd\!x
. Let
m\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and let
C\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
be
a symmetric, positive definite matrix.
A random variable
X:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a (multivariate) Gaussian random variable
with mean
m
and covariance matrix
C
if its law is

\mu(\dd\!x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}\det(C)^{1/2}}\e^{-\langle(x-m),C^{-1}(x-m)\rangle/2}\dd\!x\;.

(2.1.1)

In that case, we write
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(m,C)
.

The following result generalises (
1.1.5
)
to the multivariate case.

{proposition}

[Laplace transform]

Let
C
be symmetric, positive definite.
Then
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,C)
if, and only if, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one has

\mathbb{E}[\e^{\langle t,X\rangle}]=\e^{\langle t,Ct\rangle/2}\;.

(2.1.2)

Proof:
.

We proceed by diagonalisation.
Since
C
is symmetric, positive definite, there exists an orthogonal
matrix
U
such that
U^{\top}CU=\Lambda
is diagonal, with real, strictly
positive diagonal elements
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}
. Since
\det(\Lambda)=\det(C)\det(UU^{\top})=\det(C)
, the fact that
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,C)
is equivalent, by
the change of variables formula (or transfer theorem), to
Y
having law

\hat{\mu}(\dd\!y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}\det(\Lambda)^{1/2}}\e^{-\langle U^{\top}y,C^{-1}U^{\top}Y\rangle/2}\dd\!y=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}\det(\Lambda)^{1/2}}\e^{-\langle y,\Lambda^{-1}y\rangle/2}\dd\!y=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\e^{-\lambda_{i}y_{i}^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi\lambda_{i}}}\;.

(2.1.3)

This means that the components
Y_{i}
of
Y
are independent, with

Y_{i}\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,\lambda_{i})
. By (
1.1.5
), this implies that
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\mathbb{E}[\e^{\langle s,Y\rangle}]=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[\e^{\langle s_{i},Y_{i}\rangle}]=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}\e^{\lambda_{i}s_{i}^{2}/2}=\mathbb{E}[\e^{\langle s,\Lambda s\rangle}]\;.

(2.1.4)

The converse is actually true, because
\mathbb{E}[\e^{\langle s_{i},Y_{i}\rangle}]=1

if
s_{i}=0
. Setting
s=tU^{\top}
, this is equivalent to

\mathbb{E}[\e^{\langle t,X\rangle}]=\mathbb{E}[\e^{\langle t,UY\rangle}]=\mathbb{E}[\e^{\langle U^{\top}t,Y\rangle}]=\e^{\langle U^{\top}t,\Lambda U^{\top}t\rangle/2}=\e^{\langle t,U\Lambda U^{\top}t\rangle/2}=\e^{\langle t,Ct\rangle/2}\;,

(2.1.5)

which completes the proof.
∎

One consequence of this result is that one can easily justify the name
“covariance matrix” (though there are other ways to verify this).

{corollary}

If
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,C)
, then
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]=C_{ij}
for all

i,j\in[\![1,N]\!]
.

Proof:
.

This follows by taking the derivative of (
2.1.2
)
with respect to
t_{i}
and
t_{j}
, and evaluating at
t=0
.
∎

2.1.2
Isserlis’ theorem

Isserlis’ theorem (also known as Wick’s theorem in physics) is a generalisation
of the expression (
1.1.6
) for the moments of a Gaussian
random variable to the multivariate case. We first show a preparatory lemma,
which is a simple instance of the Schwinger–Dyson equations in
quantum field theory.

{lemma}

[Integation by parts]

Assume
X\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,C)
.
For any
i\in[\![1,N]\!]
, the equality

\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{i}f(X)\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}C_{ij}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\partial_{j}f(X)\bigr]

(2.1.6)

holds for all differentiable
f:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
such that both sides of the equality are
well-defined.

Proof:
.

Leibniz’ rule yields

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\Bigl(f(x)\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\Bigr)=\biggl[\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{j}}(x)-\frac{1}{2}f(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle\biggr]\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\;.

(2.1.7)

Since we have

\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}C_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle=\sumop\displaylimits_{j,k=1}^{N}C_{ij}C^{-1}_{jk}x_{k}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{ik}x_{k}=x_{i}\;,

(2.1.8)

it follows that

x_{i}f(x)\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}C_{ij}\biggl[-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\Bigl(f(x)\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\Bigr)+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{j}}\e^{-\langle x,C^{-1}x\rangle/2}\biggr]\;.

(2.1.9)

Integrating over the whole space, we see that the boundary terms vanish, proving (
2.1.6
).
∎

An immediate consequence of this result is the following theorem, due to
Leon Isserlis.

{theorem}

[Isserlis]

For any
1\mathbin{\leqslant}k\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{N}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}\dots X_{2k}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{{\mathscr{P}}}\;\prodop\displaylimits_{\{i,j\}\in{\mathscr{P}}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{i}X_{j}\bigr]\;,

(2.1.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}\dots X_{2k-1}\bigr]

\displaystyle=0\;,

(2.1.11)

where the sum runs over all
perfect matchings

{\mathscr{P}}

of
[\![1,2k]\!]
, meaning that each
{\mathscr{P}}
is a partition

{\mathscr{P}}=\{\{i_{1},j_{1}\},\dots,\{i_{k},j_{k}\}\}
of this set into disjoint subsets
of two elements.

Proof:
.

The proof proceeds by induction on the number of factors,
applying (
2.1.6
) with
i=1
and
f(X)
of the form
X_{2}\dots X_{m}
.
∎

{example}

In the case
2k=4
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}X_{4}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{2}\bigr]\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{3}X_{4}\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{3}\bigr]\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{2}X_{4}\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{4}\bigr]\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{2}X_{3}\bigr]\;.

(2.1.12)

A convenient graphical way of representing this relation is the following:

\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}X_{4}\bigr]=\raisebox{-22.0pt}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to42.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower-35.56595pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(2.1.13)

{remark}

Theorem
2.1.2
remains true if the covariance matrix
C

is only semi-definite positive. We may thus allow for the case

X=(X_{1},X_{1},\dots,X_{1})
, in which case it yields

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{2k}]=(2k-1)!!

(2.1.14)

since this is the number of perfect matchings of
[\![1,2k]\!]
.
We thus recover Proposition
1.1
.

{exercise}

Provide an alternative proof of Isserlis’ theorem, using the expression (
2.1.2
) of the Laplace transform
of
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
.

2.2
Hermite polynomials for multivariate Gaussians

In this section, we derive some additional properties of Hermite polynomials,
when they are evaluated in linear combinations of Gaussian random variables.

2.2.1
Scaling

In Chapter
1
, we defined Hermite polynomials for centred
Gaussian random variables of variance
1
. While centering is never
a problem, it will often be useful to allow for general variances.
This can be achieved by a simple scaling.

{definition}

[Scaled Hermite polynomials]
The
Hermite polynomial of degree
n
with variance
\sigma^{2}

is defined as

H_{n}(x;\sigma^{2})=\sigma^{n}H_{n}(x/\sigma)\;.

(2.2.1)

Table
2.1
shows the expressions of the first six
scaled Hermite polynomials.

n

H_{n}(x;\sigma^{2})

0

1

1

x

2

x^{2}-\sigma^{2}

3

x^{3}-3\sigma^{2}x

4

x^{4}-6\sigma^{2}x^{2}+3\sigma^{4}

5

x^{5}-10\sigma^{2}x^{3}+15\sigma^{4}x

Table 2.1:
List of the first Hermite polynomials of variance
\sigma^{2}
.

One easily checks the following properties:

•

scaled Hermite polynomials admit the generating function

G(t,x)=\e^{tx-\sigma^{2}t^{2}/2}\;;

(2.2.2)

•

scaled Hermite polynomials satisfy the same
orthogonality (
1.2.18
) as unscaled ones;

•

scaled Hermite polynomials satisfy the recursive relations

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(x;\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=xH_{n}(x;\sigma^{2})-\sigma^{2}\partial_{x}H_{n}(x;\sigma^{2})\;,

(2.2.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}H_{n}(x;\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=nH_{n-1}(x;\sigma^{2})\;;

(2.2.4)

•

scaled Hermite polynomials admit the explicit expression

H_{n}(x;\sigma)=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{k}k!(n-2k)!}\sigma^{2k}x^{n-2k}\;.

(2.2.5)

2.2.2
Binomial formula

The following binomial formula allows to relate Hermite polynomials with
different variances.

{lemma}

[Binomial formula for Hermite polynomials]

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, any
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}

and any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, one has

H_{n}(x+y;\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})=\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{n}\binom{n}{m}H_{m}(x;\sigma_{1}^{2})H_{n-m}(y;\sigma_{2}^{2})\;.

(2.2.6)

Proof:
.

Expanding the identity

\e^{t(x+y)-(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})t^{2}/2}=\e^{tx-\sigma_{1}t^{2}/2}\e^{ty-\sigma_{2}^{2}t^{2}/2}

(2.2.7)

yields

\sumop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}H_{n}(x+y;\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})=\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{m}}{m!}H_{m}(x;\sigma_{1}^{2})\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}H_{k}(y;\sigma_{2}^{2})\;.

(2.2.8)

Comparing coefficients of
t^{n}
gives the result.
∎

A very useful generalisation of this property, spelled out here for
Hermite polynomials of unit variance, is the following result.

{lemma}

[Multinomial formula for Hermite polynomials]

Let
a\in\ell^{2}
be a sequence of real numbers such that

\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}a_{i}^{2}=1
. Then for any sequence
(x_{i})_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
such that

\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}a_{i}x_{i}
converges, one has

H_{n}\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}a_{i}x_{i}\biggr)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\lvert k\rvert=n}\frac{n!}{k!}a^{k}\prodop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}H_{k_{i}}(x_{i})\;,

(2.2.9)

where the sum runs over all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
such that
\lvert k\rvert=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}k_{i}=n
,
and

k!:=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}k_{i}!\;,\quad a^{k}:=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}a_{i}^{k_{i}}\;.

(2.2.10)

Proof:
.

First note that the condition
\lvert k\rvert=n
implies that
k
has only finitely
many nonzero indices, so that all products are well-defined. Consider now
x,y,a,b\in\mathbb{R}
such that
a^{2}+b^{2}=1
. Then Lemma
2.2.2

and the scaling property (
2.2.1
) imply

\displaystyle H_{n}(ax+by)

\displaystyle=H_{n}(ax+by;a^{2}+b^{2})

(2.2.11)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{n}\binom{n}{m}H_{m}(ax;a^{2})H_{n-m}(by;b^{2})

(2.2.12)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{n}\binom{n}{m}a^{n}b^{n-m}H_{m}(x)H_{n-m}(y)\;.

(2.2.13)

The result then follows by applying this identity repeatedly.
∎

{exercise}

Use these results to compute
H_{2}\Bigl(\frac{x+y}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigr)
and

H_{4}\Bigl(\frac{x+y}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigr)
in terms of
H_{n}(x)
and
H_{n}(y)
.

2.3
Wiener chaos expansion

2.3.1
Main result

Consider now the case where
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
are independent Gaussian random
variables, of zero expectation and unit variance, defined on a common probability
space
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
. We are interested in the Hilbert space

{\mathscr{H}}=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})

(2.3.1)

of random variables
F=f(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
admitting a finite variance.

In order to deal with linear combinations of the
X_{i}
, it turns out to be
useful to introduce the Hilbert space
\mathbf{H}=\mathbb{R}^{N}
endowed with the canonical
inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}
. Define a linear map
W:\mathbf{H}\to{\mathscr{H}}
by

W(h)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}h_{i}X_{i}\;.

(2.3.2)

Then we have, for any
h,g\in\mathbf{H}
,

\mathbb{E}[W(h)W(g)]=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}h_{i}g_{j}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}h_{i}h_{j}=\langle h,g\rangle_{H}\;.

(2.3.3)

This means that the map
W
is an isometry from
\mathbf{H}
onto
{\mathscr{H}}
.

{definition}

[Wiener chaos,
N
-dimensional version]
For any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
, we denote by
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
the subspace of
{\mathscr{H}}

spanned by the random variables

\{H_{n}(W(h))\colon h\in\mathbf{H},\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}=1\}\;.

(2.3.4)

For
n=0
,
{\mathscr{H}}_{0}
is the set
of constants, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Then
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
is called the

homogeneous Wiener chaos of order
n
.
The
inhomogeneous Wiener chaos of order
n
is defined as

{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathbin{\leqslant}n}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}{\mathscr{H}}_{k}\;.

(2.3.5)

We can now extend Lemma
1.3
and Theorem
1.3

to the situation at hand. These are again particular cases of
[
32
, Lemma 1.1.2 and Theorem 1.1.1]
.

{lemma}

The random variables
\{\e^{W(h)}\colon h\in\mathbf{H}\}
form a total subset of
{\mathscr{H}}
.

Proof:
.

Let
Z\in{\mathscr{H}}
be such that
\mathbb{E}[Z\e^{W(h)}]=0
for all
h\in\mathbf{H}
.
The linearity of the map
h\mapsto W(h)
implies that

\mathbb{E}\biggl[Z\exp\biggl\{\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}t_{i}W(h_{i})\biggr\}\biggr]=0

(2.3.6)

for any choice of
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
, any
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in\mathbf{H}
and
m\mathbin{\geqslant}1
.
We now fix
m
and
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}
, and define a signed measure
\nu
by

\nu(B)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z\mathbbm{1}_{B}(W(h_{1}),\dots,W(h_{m}))\bigr]

(2.3.7)

for any Borel set
B
of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then (
2.3.6
)
means that the Laplace transform of
\nu

is identically zero on
\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, this measure
is zero, so that
\mathbb{E}[Z\mathbbm{1}_{B}]=0
for any Borel set
B
.
This implies that
Z
is equal to
0
, and therefore the completeness
if the system.
∎

{theorem}

[Wiener chaos decomposition]

The Hilbert space
{\mathscr{H}}
can be decomposed into the infinite orthogonal sum

{\mathscr{H}}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathscr{H}}_{n}\;.

(2.3.8)

Proof:
.

Let
Z\in{\mathscr{H}}
be orthogonal to
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
for all
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
. This means that

\mathbb{E}[ZH_{n}(W(h))]=0

(2.3.9)

for all
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
and all
h\in\mathbf{H}
with
\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}=1
.
By (
1.2.105
), we also have
\mathbb{E}[ZW(h)^{n}]=0
for all
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
,
and therefore
\mathbb{E}[Z\e^{tW(h)}]=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and all

h\in\mathbf{H}
such that
\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}=1
. By Lemma
2.3.1
,
this means that
Z=0
, which completes the proof.
∎

2.3.2
Wiener isometry and Fock space

We now construct an orthogonal basis of each Wiener chaos
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{N}
, we define

\Phi_{k}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}H_{k_{i}}(X_{i})\;.

(2.3.10)

Then Proposition
1.2.2
(orthogonality of the
H_{n}
)
implies that

\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{k}\Phi_{\ell}]=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[H_{k_{i}}(X_{i})H_{\ell_{i}}(X_{i})]=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}k_{i}!\delta_{k_{i}\ell_{i}}=k!\delta_{k\ell}

(2.3.11)

with
k!
as in (
2.2.10
).

We denote by
\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
the
n
-fold tensor product of
\mathbf{H}
, and
by
\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}
the subspace of symmetric tensors. Any element of

\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}}
can be canonically projected on
\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}
via

\Pi\bigl(h_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes h_{n}\bigr):=\frac{1}{n!}\sumop\displaylimits_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}h_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes h_{\sigma(n)}\;,

(2.3.12)

where
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
denotes the set of all permutations of
\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Let
(e_{1},\dots,e_{N})
denote any orthonormal basis of
\mathbf{H}
(for instance the
canonical basis). Then for any
k\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

e_{k}:=\Pi\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}^{\otimes k_{i}}

(2.3.13)

is an element of
\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}\lvert k\rvert}}
, where
\lvert k\rvert=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}\lvert k_{i}\rvert
,
with the convention that only strictly positive
k_{i}
count in the product (
2.3.13
).
Moreover, the set
\{e_{k}\colon\lvert k\rvert=n\}
forms an orthogonal basis of
\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}
,
with

\langle e_{k},e_{\ell}\rangle=\frac{k!}{n!}\delta_{k\ell}\;.

(2.3.14)

This is because among the
n!
permutations defining
e_{k}
, there are

k!
permutations that yield the same term.

{example}

Assume
N=3
, and let
k=(2,1,0)
. Then
k!=2
,
n=\lvert k\rvert=3
, and

\displaystyle e_{k}

\displaystyle=\Pi(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{2})

(2.3.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\bigl(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{2}+e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{1}+e_{2}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{1}\bigr)\;,

(2.3.16)

because the
3!=6
permutations of
\{1,2,3\}
have pairwise the same image.
It follows that

\langle e_{k},e_{k}\rangle=\frac{1}{3}=\frac{k!}{n!}\;.

(2.3.17)

{definition}

[Wiener isometry]
For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{N}
, let
n=\lvert k\rvert
. The map

I_{n}:e_{k}\longmapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\Phi_{k}

(2.3.18)

is called the
n
th
Wiener isometry
.

{exercise}

1.

Show that
I_{n}
is indeed an isometry between
\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}
and
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
.

2.

If
N=3
, what are the dimensions of
{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
,
{\mathscr{H}}_{2}
and
{\mathscr{H}}_{3}
?
Provide orthogonal bases of these spaces.

3.

What is the dimension of
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
for general
N
?

Hint:
Use the method of stars and bars.

The space

{\mathscr{F}}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}

(2.3.19)

is called
Fock space
.
The Wiener isometry thus provides an isometry between Fock space and
{\mathscr{H}}
.
Fock space is known from quantum physics, where it describes interacting
bosons. The space
\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}
describes the set of states with given number

n
of particles. It is identified with the space of symmetric functions
of
n
variables.

Two important particular cases of Wiener isometries are

I_{0}=1\;,\qquad I_{1}(h)=W(h)\;,

(2.3.20)

where the second relation follows from
I_{1}(e_{i})=X_{i}
, see (
2.3.2
).
The following lemma generalises these relations.

{lemma}

For any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
and
h\in\mathbf{H}
with
\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}=1
, one has

I_{n}(h^{\otimes n})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}H_{n}\bigl(W(h)\bigr)\;,

(2.3.21)

independently of the basis
\{e_{i}\}_{1\mathbin{\leqslant}i\mathbin{\leqslant}N}
of
\mathbf{H}
.

Proof:
.

If
h=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}h_{i}e_{i}
, then

h^{\otimes n}=\Pi(h^{\otimes n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{1\mathbin{\leqslant}i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\mathbin{\leqslant}N}h_{i_{1}}\dots h_{i_{n}}\Pi(e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{n}})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\lvert k\rvert=n}\frac{n!}{k!}h^{k}e_{k}\;,

(2.3.22)

where the combinatorial factor counts the number of ways a tuple

(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
can be mapped to a given
k
, defined by the fact that

k_{j}
is the number of indices equal to
j
. The result then follows from the
definitions (
2.3.18
) of
I_{n}
and (
2.3.10
) of
\Phi_{k}
and
the multinomial formula (
2.2.9
) (see
Lemma
2.2.2
).
∎

{remark}

If
h
is not normalised, one has

I_{n}(h^{\otimes n})=\frac{\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}H_{n}\biggl(W\biggl(\frac{h}{\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}}\biggr)\biggr)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}H_{n}\bigl(W(h);\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\bigr)

(2.3.23)

by the scaling property (
2.2.1
) of Hermite polynomials.

In general, we can compute
I_{n}
on an element of
\mathbf{H}^{n}
by projecting it
on
\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}
, decomposing it in the basis of
e_{k}
, and applying the
definition (
2.3.18
) of
I_{n}
.

{exercise}

Assume
h=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}h_{i}e_{i}
and
g=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}g_{i}e_{i}

belong to
\mathbf{H}
. Show that

I_{2}(h\otimes g)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2!}}\biggl[\sumop\displaylimits_{i\neq j=1}^{N}h_{i}g_{j}H_{1}(X_{i})H_{1}(X_{j})+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}h_{i}g_{i}H_{2}(X_{i})\biggr]\;.

(2.3.24)

Check that one recovers (
2.3.21
) when
h=g
and

\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}=\lVert g\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}=1
.

2.3.3
Multiplication and Wick product

Now that we have defined
I_{n}(f)
for general
f\in\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}

(or
\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
, using symmetrisation), it is of interest to compute
products of such quantities, in the same spirit as for the product-sum
formulas seen in Proposition
1.2.2
.
Here it will be more convenient to use the normalisation

\hat{I}_{n}(f)=\sqrt{n!}I_{n}(f)\;.

(2.3.25)

In order not to confuse components of tensor products in
\mathbf{H}^{n}
and components
of elements of
\mathbf{H}
, we will write the latter as

f=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}f(i)e_{i}\in\mathbf{H}\;.

(2.3.26)

In this way, elements of
\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
can be written as

f_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes f_{n}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}=1}^{N}f_{1}(i_{1})\dots f_{n}(i_{n})e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{n}}\ =:\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}=1}^{N}f(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{n}}\;.

(2.3.27)

We can thus view
f
as a map from
[\![1,N]\!]
to
\mathbb{R}
.

Let us start by computing
\hat{I}_{1}(f)\hat{I}_{1}(g)
.
This is given by

\hat{I}_{1}(f)\hat{I}_{1}(g)=W(f)W(g)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j=1}^{N}f(i)g(j)X_{i}X_{j}\;.

(2.3.28)

Comparing with (
2.3.24
), we see that

\hat{I}_{1}(f)\hat{I}_{1}(g)-\hat{I}_{2}(f\otimes g)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}f(i)g(i)\bigl[X_{i}^{2}-H_{2}(X_{i})\bigr]=\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{0}\;.

(2.3.29)

We have thus obtained

\hat{I}_{1}(f)\hat{I}_{1}(g)=\hat{I}_{2}(f\otimes g)+\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}\;.

(2.3.30)

Let
{\mathfrak{S}}(p,n)\subset{\mathfrak{S}}(n)
denote the set of permutations
of
[\![1,n]\!]
preserving the order of the first
p
and the last
n-p
elements, also called
shuffles
. A first generalisation of (
2.3.30
)
is as follows.

{lemma}

[Multiplication between the
n
th and first chaos]

Assume
f\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
and
g\in\mathbf{H}
. Then

\hat{I}_{n}(f)\hat{I}_{1}(g)=\hat{I}_{n+1}(f\otimes g)+\hat{I}_{n-1}(f\star_{1}g)\;,

(2.3.31)

where
\star_{1}
denotes the
contraction operation

(f\star_{1}g)(i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\Sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}(1,n)}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}f(\Sigma(j,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}))g(j)\;.

(2.3.32)

Proof:
.

By linearity, it suffices to check the relation for basis vectors, that is,
when one has
f=e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{n}}
and
g=e_{j}
. We
distinguish between two cases.

1.

If
j\notin\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\}
, then
f\star_{1}g=0
, because
all components of
f
containing one index
j
are zero.
One easily sees that

\hat{I}_{n+1}(f\otimes e_{j})=\hat{I}_{n}(f)X_{j}\;.

(2.3.33)

2.

If
j\in\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\}
, say
j=i_{1}
,
let
m
be the number of indices equal to
i_{1}
. We have

\hat{I}_{n}(f)=H_{m}(X_{i_{1}})P_{n-m}\;,\qquad\hat{I}_{1}(g)=H_{1}(X_{i_{1}})\;,

(2.3.34)

where
P_{n-m}
is a polynomial of degree
n-m
that does not contain
X_{i_{1}}
.
By the product-sum formula (
1.2.34
), we obtain

H_{m}(X_{i_{1}})H_{1}(X_{i_{1}})=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{1}\binom{m}{p}\binom{1}{p}H_{m+1-p}(X_{i_{1}})=H_{m+1}(X_{i_{1}})+mH_{m-1}(X_{i_{1}})\;,

(2.3.35)

so that

\hat{I}_{n}(f)\hat{I}_{1}(g)=\bigl[H_{m+1}(X_{i_{1}})+mH_{m-1}(X_{i_{1}})\bigr]P_{n-m}\;.

(2.3.36)

On the other hand, we have

\hat{I}_{n+1}(f\otimes g)=H_{m+1}(X_{i})P_{n-m}\;,

(2.3.37)

while, using
g(\ell)=\delta_{\ell j}
,

(f\star_{1}g)(i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\Sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}(1,m)}f(\Sigma(i_{1},i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}))\;.

(2.3.38)

There are
m
ways to insert the first
i_{1}
among the other indices,
which all yield the same value upon applying
\hat{I}_{n-1}
. Therefore,

\hat{I}_{n-1}(f\star_{1}g)=mH_{m-1}(X_{i_{1}})P_{n-m}\;.

(2.3.39)

The sum of (
2.3.37
) and (
2.3.39
) is indeed
equal to (
2.3.36
).
∎

{example}

Consider the case
n=3
. Then

\hat{I}_{3}(f)\hat{I}_{1}(g)=\hat{I}_{4}(f\otimes g)+\hat{I}_{2}(f\star_{1}g)\;,

(2.3.40)

where

(f\otimes g)(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},j)=f(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})g(j)\;.

(2.3.41)

Denoting a permutation
\Sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}(1,3)
by the image

(\Sigma(1),\Sigma(2),\Sigma(3))
of
(1,2,3)
, we have

{\mathfrak{S}}(1,3)=\{(1,2,3),(2,1,3),(2,3,1)\}

(2.3.42)

and therefore

(f\star_{1}g)(i_{1},i_{2})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N}\bigl[f(j,i_{1},i_{2})+f(i_{1},j,i_{2})+f(i_{1},i_{2},j)\bigr]g(j)\;.

(2.3.43)

The generalisation of this to arbitrary
f
and
g
is as follows.

{proposition}

[Multiplication between
n
th and
m
th chaos]

Assume
f\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
and
g\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes m}
. Then

\hat{I}_{n}(f)\hat{I}_{m}(g)=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{n\wedge m}\hat{I}_{n+m-2p}(f\star_{p}g)

(2.3.44)

where
\star_{0}=\otimes
and
the contraction
\star_{p}
is defined
for
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n-p})

and
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{m-p})

by

(f\star_{p}g)(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}(p,n)\\
\bar{\Sigma}\in{\mathfrak{S}}(p,m)\end{subarray}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}(p)}\sumop\displaylimits_{\mathbf{k}\in[\![1,N]\!]^{p}}f(\Sigma(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{i}))g(\bar{\Sigma}(\mathbf{k},\sigma(\mathbf{j})))\;.

(2.3.45)

Before giving a proof of this result, we provide some intuition for the
meaning of contractions, see Figure
2.1
.
We think of
f
as a vertex with
n
legs, representing its
components, while
g
is a vertex with
m
legs.
The contraction
\star_{p}
represents all ways of
pairing
p
legs of
f
with
p
legs of
g
, where
\Sigma

represents the choice of legs of
f
,
\bar{\Sigma}
represents the
choice of legs of
g
, and
\sigma
counts all ways of pairing
the chosen legs. As for the sum over
\ell
, it can be viewed as
an inner product in
\mathbf{H}
. With this picture in mind, we can show
the following lemma.

{lemma}

Assume
f\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
, and
g=g_{1}\otimes g_{2}\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes m}

with
g_{1}\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes(m-1)}
and
g_{2}\in\mathbf{H}
. Then
one has

f\star_{p}g=\mathbbm{1}_{p\neq 0}(f\star_{p-1}g_{1})\star_{1}g_{2}+\mathbbm{1}_{p\neq m}(f\star_{p}g_{1})\otimes g_{2}\qquad\text{for $0\mathbin{\leqslant}p\mathbin{\leqslant}m$\;.}

(2.3.46)

Proof:
.

We give a graphical proof.
The first term on the right-hand side represents pairing
p-1

legs of
f
with
p-1
legs of
g_{1}
, and one leg of
f
with one
leg of
g_{2}
, see Figure
2.1
(a).
This is only possible if
p\mathbin{\geqslant}1
. The second term
on the right-hand side represents pairing
p
legs of
f
with
p

legs of
g_{1}
, and leaving
g_{2}
alone, see
Figure
2.1
(b). This is only possible
if
p\mathbin{\leqslant}m-1
.
∎

f
g_{1}
g_{2}
(a)
f
g_{1}
g_{2}
(b)

Figure 2.1:
Examples of pairings corresponding to

(f\star_{p-1}g_{1})\star_{1}g_{2}
(a)
and to
(f\star_{p}g_{1})\otimes g_{2}
(b), when
n=7
,
m=5

and
p=3
.

We can now give the proof of Proposition
2.3.3
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3.3
.

We may assume
n\mathbin{\geqslant}m
.
The proof is by induction on
m
. The base case
m=1
is Lemma
2.3.3
.
For the induction step, we can restrict by linearity to the case
where
g=g_{1}\otimes g_{2}
with
g_{1}\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes(m-1)}
and
g_{2}\in\mathbf{H}
.
Assume that
g_{2}
is orthogonal to all components of
g_{1}
. Then we claim that

g_{1}\star_{1}g_{2}=0\;.

(2.3.47)

Indeed, taking a
g_{2}
as a basis vector of
\mathbf{H}
,
g_{1}
will be a linear
combination of basis vectors different from
g_{2}
, so that the
inner product in (
2.3.32
) vanishes. It follows that

\hat{I}_{m-1}(g_{1})\hat{I}_{1}(g_{2})=\hat{I}_{m}(g_{1}\otimes g_{2})\;.

(2.3.48)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\hat{I}_{n}(f)\hat{I}_{m}(g)

\displaystyle=\hat{I}_{n}(f)\hat{I}_{m-1}(g_{1})\hat{I}_{1}(g_{2})

(2.3.49)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{m-1}\hat{I}_{n+m-1-2p}(f\star_{p}g_{1})\hat{I}_{1}(g_{2})

(2.3.50)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{m-1}\hat{I}_{n+m-2p}\bigl((f\star_{p}g_{1})\otimes g_{2}\bigr)+\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{m-1}\hat{I}_{n+m-1-2p}\bigl((f\star_{p}g_{1})\star_{1}g_{2}\bigr)\;,

(2.3.51)

where we have used the induction hypothesis in the second line,
and Lemma
2.3.3
in the third one. Making the index
shift
p\mapsto p+1
in the second sum and using Lemma
2.3.3

yields the result.

In case it is not possible to find an orthogonal decomposition

g=g_{1}\otimes g_{2}
, by linearity it suffices to consider
the case where
g=g_{2}^{\otimes m}
. This case can be reduced
to an explicit computation, see Exercise
2.3.3
below.
∎

{remark}

If
f
and
g
are symmetric under permutations of their arguments,
since
{\mathfrak{S}}(p,n)
has cardinality
\binom{n}{p}
, we have

(f\star_{p}g)(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=p!\binom{n}{p}\binom{m}{p}\sumop\displaylimits_{\mathbf{k}\in[\![1,N]\!]^{p}}f(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{i})g(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{j})\;.

(2.3.52)

{exercise}

Compute
\hat{I}_{n}(f)\hat{I}_{m}(g)
when
f=h_{1}^{\otimes n}

and
g=h_{2}^{\otimes m}
with
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbf{H}
.
Argue that for the case left out in the proof of Proposition
2.3.3
,
is suffices to consider the case
h_{1}=h_{2}
. Why is the result true
in that case?

The leading term in the decomposition (
2.3.44
)
plays a special role, and is therefore given a name.

{definition}

[Wick product]
The
Wick product
of
f\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
and
g\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes m}

is defined by

\hat{I}_{n}(f)\diamond\hat{I}_{m}(g)=\hat{I}_{n+m}(f\otimes g)\;.

(2.3.53)

Relation (
2.3.44
) can thus be written

\hat{I}_{n}(f)\hat{I}_{m}(g)=\hat{I}_{n}(f)\diamond\hat{I}_{m}(g)+\sumop\displaylimits_{p=1}^{n\wedge m}\hat{I}_{n+m-2p}(f\star_{p}g)\;.

(2.3.54)

2.4
Equivalence of moments

The purpose of this section is to give a proof of the following, very
useful result.

{theorem}

[Equivalence of moments]

Assume
F
belongs to the
n
th Wiener chaos
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
. Then for any

p>1
, one has

\mathbb{E}[F^{2p}]^{1/2p}\mathbin{\leqslant}(2p-1)^{n/2}\mathbb{E}[F^{2}]^{1/2}\;.

(2.4.1)

This result states that the variance of a random variable

F=f(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
controls all
L^{p}
norms of
F
for

p>1
.

We will follow a proof given in
[
32
, Section 1.4]
, based
on hypercontractivity of the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup. Other proofs can be found
in
[
19
, Section 4]
and in the lecture
notes
[
29
, Section 7]
.

2.4.1
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup

{definition}

[Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup]
The Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup is the one-parameter semigroup

\{T_{t}\colon t\mathbin{\geqslant}0\}
of contraction operators on
{\mathscr{H}}
defined by

T_{t}(F)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}\e^{-nt}P_{n}F

(2.4.2)

for any
F\in{\mathscr{H}}
, where
P_{n}:{\mathscr{H}}\to{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
denotes the orthogonal
projection on the
n
th Wiener chaos.

We have already encountered the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process in
Section
1.2.3
. Consider first the one-dimensional
case (
N=1
). Then this process is defined as the solution of the
stochastic differential equation in
\mathbb{R}

\dd\!X_{t}=-X_{t}\dd\!t+\sqrt{2}\dd\!W_{t}\;,\qquad X_{0}=x\;,

(2.4.3)

where
(W_{t})_{t\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
denotes standard Brownian motion. By Ito’s formula,
for any
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
of class
{\mathscr{C}}^{2}
, the process
Y_{t}=f(x_{t})
satisfies

\dd\!Y_{t}=\bigl[-X_{t}f^{\prime}(X_{t})+f^{\prime\prime}(X_{t})\bigr]\dd\!t+\sqrt{2}f^{\prime}(X_{t})\dd\!W_{t}\;.

(2.4.4)

In particular, if
f(x)=H_{n}(x)
, then the term in brackets is

-nH_{n}(X_{t})
(see (
1.2.45
) in
Corollary
1.2.3
), so that

\dd\!Y_{t}=-nY_{t}\dd\!t+\sqrt{2}f^{\prime}(X_{t})\dd\!W_{t}\;.

(2.4.5)

Integrating from
0
to
t
, we find

H_{n}(X_{t})=H_{n}(x)-n\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}H_{n}(X_{s})\dd\!s+\sqrt{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}f^{\prime}(X_{s})\dd\!W_{s}\;.

(2.4.6)

Since the expectation of the stochastic integral is zero, it follows that

\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X_{t})]=H_{n}(x)-n\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X_{s})]\dd\!s\;,

(2.4.7)

which implies

\frac{\dd\!}{\dd\!t}\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X_{t})]=-n\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X_{t})]\;.

(2.4.8)

The solution of this ordinary differential equation is simply

\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(X_{t})]=H_{n}(x)\e^{-nt}\;.

(2.4.9)

Consider now a general
f
of the form

f(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}c_{n}H_{n}(x)\;.

(2.4.10)

Then by linearity, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t})]=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}c_{n}H_{n}(x)\e^{-nt}=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}(P_{n}f))(x)\e^{-nt}=T_{t}(f)(x)\;,

(2.4.11)

explaining why
T_{t}
in (
2.4.2
) is called Ornstein–Uhlenbeck
semigroup.

Consider now the
N
-dimensional case. The
N
-dimensional Ornstein–Uhlenbeck
is defined as the solution of the stochastic differential equation (
2.4.3
)
when
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
W_{t}
is
N
-dimensional Brownian motion. If
Y_{t}=f(X_{t})

for a twice differentiable
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, Ito’s formula now reads

\dd\!Y_{t}=\bigl[-\langle\nabla f(X_{t}),X_{t}\rangle+\Delta f(X_{t})\bigr]\dd\!t+\sqrt{2}\langle\nabla f(X_{t}),\dd\!W_{t}\rangle\;.

(2.4.12)

The fact that the associated semigroup is again of the form (
2.4.2
)
is a consequence of the following observation.

{lemma}

[Eigenfunctions of the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck generator in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
]
For
k\in\mathbf{H}=\mathbb{R}^{N}
let

f(x)=\Phi_{k}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{N}H_{k_{i}}(x_{i})\;.

(2.4.13)

Then

-\langle\nabla f(x),x\rangle+\Delta f(x)=-\lvert k\rvert f(x)\;.

(2.4.14)

Proof:
.

This follows directly from the fact that

\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}=H_{k_{i}}^{\prime}(x_{i})\prodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i}H_{k_{j}}(x_{j})\;,\qquad\frac{\partial f^{2}}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}=H_{k_{i}}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})\prodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i}H_{k_{j}}(x_{j})

(2.4.15)

for any
i\in[\![1,N]\!]
.
∎

{exercise}

Check that (
2.4.14
) implies that the semigroup of the

N
-dimensional Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process is given by (
2.4.2
).

Using variation of constants, the solution of (
2.4.3
) can be
represented as

X_{t}=\e^{-t}x+\sqrt{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{-(t-s)}\dd\!W_{s}\;,

(2.4.16)

as can be verified by applying Ito’s formula. The second
term on the right-hand side is Gaussian, centered, and by Ito’s isometry,
its variance is

2\intop\nolimits_{0}^{t}\e^{-2(t-s)}\dd\!s=1-\e^{-2t}\;.

(2.4.17)

The Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process can thus be written as

X_{t}=\e^{-t}x+\sqrt{1-\e^{-2t}}X_{t}^{\prime}\;,

(2.4.18)

where
X_{t}^{\prime}\sim{\mathscr{N}}(0,1)
for any
t\mathbin{\geqslant}0
.
This observation is the intuition behind the following result.

{proposition}

[Mehler’s formula]

Let
W^{\prime}=\{W^{\prime}(h)\colon h\in\mathbf{H}\}
be an independent copy of

W=\{W(h)\colon h\in\mathbf{H}\}
, where
W
and
W^{\prime}
are
defined on a product probability space

(\Omega\times{}^{\prime},{\mathscr{F}}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{\prime},\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}^{\prime})
.
For
t>0
, consider the process
Z=\{Z(h)\colon h\in\mathbf{H}\}
defined by

Z(h)=\e^{-t}W(h)+\sqrt{1-\e^{-2t}}W^{\prime}(h)\;.

(2.4.19)

Then for any
F\in{\mathscr{H}}
of the form
F=f(W)
, one has

T_{t}(F)=\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\bigl[f(Z)\bigr]

(2.4.20)

where
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
denotes the expectation with respect to the law
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
of
W^{\prime}
.

Proof:
.

The process
Z(h)
is Gaussian, centered, with covariance

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z(h_{1})Z(h_{2})]

\displaystyle=\e^{-2t}\mathbb{E}[W(h_{1})W(h_{2})]+(1-\e^{-2t})\mathbb{E}[W^{\prime}(h_{1})W^{\prime}(h_{2})]

(2.4.21)

\displaystyle=\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}

(2.4.22)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[W(h_{1})W(h_{2})]

(2.4.23)

by (
2.3.3
).
The right-hand side of (
2.4.20
) defines a linear contraction
on any
L^{p}(\Omega)
with
p>1
, because by Jensen’s inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lvert T_{t}(F)\rvert^{p}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl|\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\bigl[f(\e^{-t}W(h)+\sqrt{1-\e^{-2t}}W^{\prime}(h))\bigr]\bigr|^{p}\Bigr]

(2.4.24)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\bigl[\lvert f(\e^{-t}W(h)+\sqrt{1-\e^{-2t}}W^{\prime}(h))\rvert^{p}\bigr]\Bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lvert F\rvert^{p}\bigr]\;.

(2.4.25)

Therefore, it is sufficient to check that (
2.4.20
) holds for
the generating function of Hermite polynomials

F=f(W)=\exp\{W(h)-\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\}
with any
h\in\mathbf{H}
.
On one hand, we have

\displaystyle F=G(1,W(h))

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{1}{n!}H_{n}(W(h);\lVert h\rVert_{H})

(2.4.26)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{1}{n!}\hat{I}_{n}(h^{\otimes n})

(2.4.27)

by Remark
2.3.2
. This implies

T_{t}(F)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{\e^{-nt}}{n!}\hat{I}_{n}(h^{\otimes n})\;.

(2.4.28)

On the other hand, we have

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\bigl[f(Z)\bigr]=\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\bigl[\e^{a+bW^{\prime}(h)}\bigr]\;,\qquad a=\e^{-t}W(h)-\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\;,\qquad b=\sqrt{1-\e^{-2t}}\;.

(2.4.29)

By Proposition
2.1.1
(Laplace transform) and the
expression (
2.4.22
) for the covariance of
Z
, this yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\bigl[f(Z)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\e^{a}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\bigl[\e^{\langle b,W(h)\rangle}\bigr]

(2.4.30)

\displaystyle=\exp\biggl\{\e^{-t}W(h)-\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(1-\e^{-2t})\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\biggr\}

(2.4.31)

\displaystyle=\exp\biggl\{\e^{-t}W(h)-\frac{1}{2}\e^{-2t}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\biggr\}

(2.4.32)
