\displaystyle=G(\e^{-t},W(h))

(2.4.33)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{\e^{-nt}}{n!}H_{n}(W(h);\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}})

(2.4.34)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{1}{n!}\hat{I}_{n}(h^{\otimes n})\;.

(2.4.35)

This is equal to (
2.4.28
), which concludes the proof.
∎

2.4.2
Hypercontractivity

We will denote by
L^{p}(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
the Banach space of random variables

F:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\lVert F\rVert_{p}=\mathbb{E}[F^{p}]^{1/p}<\infty\;.

(2.4.36)

The following result, originally due to Nelson, says that the
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup is
hypercontractive
, meaning
that if
t>0
, then
T_{t}
maps
L^{p}(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
into

L^{q}(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
for some
q=q(t)
strictly larger than
p
.
We follow essentially the proof of
[
32
, Theorem 1.4.1]
.

{theorem}

[Hypercontractivity of the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup]
For
p>1
and
t>0
, let

q(t)=\e^{2t}(p-1)+1>p\;.

(2.4.37)

Then for any
F\in L^{p}(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
, one has

\lVert T_{t}F\rVert_{q(t)}\mathbin{\leqslant}\lVert F\rVert_{p}\;.

(2.4.38)

Proof:
.

Let
q^{\prime}
be the Hölder congujate of
q=q(t)
, that is,

\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1\;.

(2.4.39)

By duality, we have

\lVert T_{t}F\rVert_{q}=\sup_{G\in L^{q^{\prime}}}\frac{\langle T_{t}F,G\rangle}{\lVert G\rVert_{q^{\prime}}}=\sup_{G\in L^{q^{\prime}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[(T_{t}F)G]}{\lVert G\rVert_{q^{\prime}}}\;.

(2.4.40)

We will thus have proved (
2.4.38
) if we manage
to prove that

\mathbb{E}[(T_{t}F)G]\mathbin{\leqslant}\lVert F\rVert_{p}\lVert G\rVert_{q^{\prime}}

(2.4.41)

for all
G\in L^{q^{\prime}}(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
.
Since
T_{t}
is non-negative, and thus
\lvert T_{t}F\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}T_{t}(\lvert F\rvert)
,
we may assume that
F
and
G
are both non-negative. In fact, by an
approximation argument, we may assume that
a\mathbin{\leqslant}F,G\mathbin{\leqslant}b<\infty

for some
b\mathbin{\geqslant}a>0
.

The main idea is to use a kind of interpolation argument.
Recall that both
F
and
G
are functions of the random variables

W(e_{i})=X_{i}
with
i\in[\![1,N]\!]
. By Mehler’s
identity (
2.4.20
),

T_{t}F=\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\bigl[f(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N})\bigr]\;,

(2.4.42)

where

Z_{i}=\e^{-t}X_{i}+\sqrt{1-\e^{-2t}}X_{i}^{\prime}\;,

(2.4.43)

the
X_{i}^{\prime}
being independent copies of the
X_{i}
. We can represent these
variables as

X_{i}=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\dd\!W_{i}(s)\;,\qquad X_{i}^{\prime}=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\dd\!W_{i}^{\prime}(s)\;,

(2.4.44)

where
(W_{i}(s))_{0\mathbin{\leqslant}s\mathbin{\leqslant}1}
and
(W_{i}^{\prime}(s))_{0\mathbin{\leqslant}s\mathbin{\leqslant}1}

are independent Brownian motions. Then we have

Z_{i}=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}B_{i}(s)\dd\!s\;,\qquad B_{i}(s)=\e^{-t}W_{i}(s)+\sqrt{1-\e^{-2t}}W_{i}^{\prime}(s)\;.

(2.4.45)

Therefore, we can write

\mathbb{E}[(T_{t}F)G]=\mathbb{E}[PQ]\;,

(2.4.46)

where

P=f(B_{1},\dots,B_{N})\;,\qquad Q=g(W_{1},\dots,W_{N})\;.

(2.4.47)

By Ito’s formula,
P^{p}
and
Q^{p}
have integral representations of the form

P^{p}=\mathbb{E}[P^{p}]+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\varphi(s)\dd\!B(s)\;,\qquad Q^{q^{\prime}}=\mathbb{E}[Q^{q^{\prime}}]+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\psi(s)\dd\!W(s)

(2.4.48)

for some bounded, positive
\varphi
and
\psi
.
We introduce two bounded, positive martingales

M(s)=\mathbb{E}[P^{p}]+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{s}\varphi(u)\dd\!B(u)\;,\qquad N(s)=\mathbb{E}[Q^{q^{\prime}}]+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{s}\psi(u)\dd\!W(u)\;,

(2.4.49)

which satisfy

\displaystyle M(0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[P^{p}]\;,

\displaystyle N(0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Q^{q^{\prime}}]\;,

(2.4.50)

\displaystyle M(1)

\displaystyle=P^{p}\;,

\displaystyle N(1)

\displaystyle=Q^{q^{\prime}}\;.

(2.4.51)

Define
f(x,y)=x^{\alpha}y^{\beta}
, with
\alpha=\frac{1}{p}
and
\beta=\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}
.
Then
U(s)=f(M(s),N(s))
satisfies

U(0)=\lVert P\rVert_{p}\lVert Q\rVert_{q^{\prime}}\;,\qquad U(1)=PQ\;.

(2.4.52)

By Ito’s formula, we have

\dd\!U(s)=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\dd\!M(s)+\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\dd\!N(s)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}\dd\!M(s)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial y^{2}}\dd\!N(s)^{2}+\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}\dd\!M(s)\dd\!N(s)\;,

(2.4.53)

where

\displaystyle\dd\!M(s)^{2}

\displaystyle=\varphi(s)^{2}\dd\!s\;,\qquad\dd\!N(s)^{2}=\psi(s)^{2}\dd\!s\;,

(2.4.54)

\displaystyle\dd\!M(s)\dd\!N(s)

\displaystyle=\varphi(s)\psi(s)\dd\!B(s)\dd\!W^{\prime}(s)=\varphi(s)\psi(s)\e^{-s}\dd\!s\;.

(2.4.55)

Computing the partial derivatives of
f
, this leads to

\dd\!U(s)=\alpha M(s)^{\alpha-1}N(s)^{\beta}\dd\!M(s)+\beta M(s)^{\alpha}N(s)^{\beta-1}\dd\!N(s)+\frac{1}{2}M(s)^{\alpha}N(s)^{\beta}A(s)\dd\!s\;,

(2.4.56)

where

\displaystyle A(s)

\displaystyle=\alpha(\alpha-1)M(s)^{-2}\varphi(s)^{2}+\beta(\beta-1)N(s)^{-2}\psi(s)^{2}+2\alpha\beta M(s)^{-1}N(s)^{-1}\varphi(s)\psi(s)\e^{-t}

(2.4.57)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi(s)M(s)^{-1}&\psi(s)N(s)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha(\alpha-1)&\alpha\beta\e^{-t}\\
\alpha\beta\e^{-t}&\beta(\beta-1)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi(s)M(s)^{-1}\\
\psi(s)N(s)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\;.

(2.4.58)

We have

PQ=U(1)=U(0)+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\dd\!U(s)=\lVert P\rVert_{p}\lVert Q\rVert_{q^{\prime}}+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\dd\!U(s)\;.

(2.4.59)

Taking expectations, since
M(s)
and
N(s)
are martingales, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[(T_{t}F)G]=\mathbb{E}[PQ]=\lVert P\rVert_{p}\lVert Q\rVert_{q^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[M(s)^{\alpha}N(s)^{\beta}A(s)]\dd\!s\;.

(2.4.60)

The result will thus be proved if we manage to show that
A(s)\mathbin{\leqslant}0

for all
s\in[0,1]
.
Note that if
p>1
, then one finds
\alpha(1-\alpha)<0
and
\beta(1-\beta)<0
.
It thus suffices to show that the determinant of the matrix in (
2.4.58
)
is non-negative. This is equivalent to (
2.4.37
).
∎

It is now easy to prove equivalence of moments.

Proof of Theorem
2.4
:

We make the change of index
q(t)\mapsto 2p
and
p\mapsto 2
. Then (
2.4.38
)
becomes

\lVert T_{t}F\rVert_{2p}\mathbin{\leqslant}\lVert F\rVert_{2}\;,

(2.4.61)

where

\e^{2t}=2p-1\;.

(2.4.62)

In particular, for
F\in{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
, we have

\lVert T_{t}F\rVert_{2p}=\e^{-nt}\lVert F\rVert_{2p}\;.

(2.4.63)

This yields

\lVert F\rVert_{2p}=\e^{nt}\lVert T_{t}F\rVert_{2p}\mathbin{\leqslant}\e^{nt}\lVert F\rVert_{2}=(2p-1)^{n/2}\lVert F\rVert_{2}\;,

(2.4.64)

which is equivalent to (
2.4.1
).
∎

Chapter 3
Gaussian fields

In this chapter, we extend the results of the previous chapter to the
infinite-dimensional case, and discuss two particularly important cases
of Gaussian fields, namely white noise and the Gaussian free field.

3.1
Isonormal Gaussian processes

In this chapter, we will mostly be concerned with the following set-up.
Let
\Lambda=\mathbb{T}^{d}=(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{d}
be the
d
-dimensional torus, for some
d\mathbin{\geqslant}1
. Let

\mathbf{H}=L^{2}(\Lambda,\dd\!x)

(3.1.1)

be the Hilbert space of square-integrable functions
h:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Let
(e_{i})_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbf{H}
– we will typically
choose a Fourier basis. We are then interested in random fields of the
form

\phi(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}X_{i}e_{i}(x)\;,

(3.1.2)

where the
X_{i}
are independent, centred jointly Gaussian random variables of unit
variance, defined on a common probability space
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
.
We will see that this is related to the following general construction.

{definition}

[Isonormal Gaussian process]
Let
\mathbf{H}
be a separable Hilbert space.
A stochastic process
W=\{W(h)\colon h\in\mathbf{H}\}
defined on a complete
probability space
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
is an
isonormal Gaussian process

if
W
is a centred Gaussian family of random variables such that

\mathbb{E}[W(h_{1})W(h_{2})]=\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}

(3.1.3)

for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbf{H}
.

Note that the map
h\mapsto W(h)
is necessarily linear. This is because
for any
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbf{H}
, and any
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bigl(W(\lambda h_{1}+\mu h_{2})-

\displaystyle\lambda W(h_{1})-\mu W(h_{2})\bigr)^{2}]

(3.1.4)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\lVert\lambda h_{1}+\mu h_{2}\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}+\lambda^{2}\lVert h_{1}\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}+\mu^{2}\lVert h_{2}\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}

(3.1.5)

\displaystyle{}-2\lambda\langle\lambda h_{1}+\mu h_{2},h_{1}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}-2\mu\langle\lambda h_{1}+\mu h_{2},h_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}+2\lambda\mu\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=0\;.

(3.1.6)

As a consequence, if each random variable is Gaussian and centred, the set

\{W(h)\colon h\in\mathbf{H}\}
is automatically a family of jointly Gaussian random variables.
The existence of the probability space
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
follows from
Kolmogorov’s continuity theorem.

3.1.1
Wiener chaos expansion and Wiener isometry

The definition of Wiener chaoses is the same in infinite dimension
as in finite dimension.

{definition}

[Wiener chaos, infinite-dimensional version]
For any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
, we denote by
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
the subspace of

{\mathscr{H}}=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
spanned by the random variables

\{H_{n}(W(h))\colon h\in\mathbf{H},\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}=1\}\;.

(3.1.7)

For
n=0
,
{\mathscr{H}}_{0}
is the set
of constants, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Then
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
is called the

homogeneous Wiener chaos of order
n
.
The
inhomogeneous Wiener chaos of order
n
is defined as

{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathbin{\leqslant}n}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}{\mathscr{H}}_{k}\;.

(3.1.8)

We also have an analogue of Theorem
2.3.1
, with the same
proof (see also
[
32
, Theorem 1.1.1]
).

{theorem}

[Wiener chaos decomposition]

The Hilbert space
{\mathscr{H}}
can be decomposed into the infinite orthogonal sum

{\mathscr{H}}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathscr{H}}_{n}\;.

(3.1.9)

The construction of the Wiener isometry proceeds in the same way as we have
seen in Section
2.3.2
, except that one now assumes that
the multiindex
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}
has only finitely many non-zero entries.
In this way,

\Phi_{k}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0\colon k_{i}>0}H_{k_{i}}(X_{i})

(3.1.10)

is well-defined, as are

\lvert k\rvert=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0\colon k_{i}>0}\lvert k_{i}\rvert\qquad\text{and}\qquad k!=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0\colon k_{i}>0}k_{i}!\;.

(3.1.11)

The Wiener isometry, with its two normalisation conventions, is again defined as

I_{n}(e_{k})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\Phi_{k}\;,\qquad\hat{I}_{n}(e_{k})=\Phi_{k}

(3.1.12)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}
with
\lvert k\rvert=n
, where

e_{k}=\Pi\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}e_{i}^{\otimes k_{i}}\;,

(3.1.13)

\Pi
being the symmetrisation operator (
2.3.12
). Note that we have slightly
overloaded the notation for
e
, as we use the same letter for elements of
\mathbf{H}

and elements of Fock space. It should always be clear from the context which
basis vector is meant.

Let us give some simple examples for clarity.

{example}

•

Assume that
k
has only one nonzero entry
k_{i}=1
. Then
e_{k}=e_{i}

and
\hat{I}_{1}(e_{k})=H_{1}(X_{i})=X_{i}
.

•

If
k
has two nonzero entries
k_{i}=k_{j}=1
with
i\neq j
, then

e_{k}=\frac{1}{2}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}+e_{j}\otimes e_{i})
, and

\hat{I}_{2}(e_{k})=X_{i}X_{j}
.

•

If
k
has one nonzero entry
k_{i}=2
, then

e_{k}=e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
and
\hat{I}_{2}(e_{k})=H_{2}(X_{i})
.

The bound showing equivalence of moments, cf. Theorem
2.4
, can be proved in essentially
the same way as we did in finite dimension, so we just repeat it here.

{theorem}

[Equivalence of moments]

Assume
F
belongs to the
n
th Wiener chaos
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
. Then for any

p>1
, one has

\mathbb{E}[F^{2p}]^{1/2p}\mathbin{\leqslant}(2p-1)^{n/2}\mathbb{E}[F^{2}]^{1/2}\;.

(3.1.14)

{exercise}

•

Let
f,g\in\mathbf{H}
. Compute
\mathbb{E}[\hat{I}_{1}(f)\hat{I}_{1}(g)]
.

•

Do the same for
\mathbb{E}[\hat{I}_{n}(f)\hat{I}_{m}(g)]
, when

f\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
and
g\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes m}
.

3.1.2
The case of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

As mentioned above, we will mainly be concerned with the case
\mathbf{H}=L^{2}(\Lambda,\dd\!x)
,
with
\Lambda=\mathbb{T}^{d}
, endowed with a Fourier basis
(e_{i})_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}
. Elements of

\mathbf{H}
are thus functions
h:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
that can be written as a Fourier series

h(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)e_{i}(x)\;.

(3.1.15)

Recall that (
3.1.15
) defines an isometry between
\mathbf{H}
and the Hilbert
space

\widehat{\mathbf{H}}=\ell^{2}=\biggl\{\hat{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\colon\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)^{2}<\infty\biggr\}\;.

(3.1.16)

Indeed, by Parseval’s relation, we have

\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}h(x)^{2}\dd\!x=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)^{2}=\lVert\hat{h}\rVert_{\widehat{\mathbf{H}}}^{2}\;.

(3.1.17)

It is thus equivalent to work in
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}
, which is essentially the
same as what we did in the finite-dimensional case, or in
\mathbf{H}
.

Elements of
\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
can be written either as

h=h_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes h_{n}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1}\mathbin{\geqslant}0,\dots,i_{n}\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}_{1}(i_{1})\dots\hat{h}_{n}(i_{n})e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{n}}\;,

(3.1.18)

or, equivalently, as

h(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1}\mathbin{\geqslant}0,\dots,i_{n}\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})e_{i_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\dots e_{i_{n}}(x_{i_{n}})\;,

(3.1.19)

where
\hat{h}(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})=\hat{h}_{1}(i_{1})\dots\hat{h}_{n}(i_{n})
.
In this way, elements of
\smash{\mathbf{H}^{\otimes_{\text{s}}n}}
are represented by functions
h
that
are symmetric in all their arguments, that is,

h(\sigma(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))=h(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\qquad\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}\;.

(3.1.20)

The definition of contractions can then be rewritten in the following way.

{lemma}

Let
f\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes n}
and
g\in\mathbf{H}^{\otimes m}
. For any
p\mathbin{\leqslant}n\wedge m
,
all
x\in\Lambda^{n-p}
and all
y\in\Lambda^{m-p}
, one has

(f\star_{p}g)(x,y)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}(p,n)\\
\bar{\Sigma}\in{\mathfrak{S}}(p,m)\end{subarray}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}(p)}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda^{p}}f\bigl(\Sigma(z,x)\bigr)g\bigl(\bar{\Sigma}(z,\sigma(x))\bigr)\dd\!z\;.

(3.1.21)

Proof:
.

Consider the case
m=1
. Then (
2.3.45
) yields

(\hat{f}\star_{1}\hat{g})(i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\Sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}(1,n)}\sumop\displaylimits_{j\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{f}(\Sigma(j,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}))\hat{g}(j)\;.

(3.1.22)

By (
3.1.19
), we have

(f\star_{1}g)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}\mathbin{\geqslant}0}(\hat{f}\star_{1}\hat{g})(i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1})e_{i_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\dots e_{i_{n-1}}(x_{i_{n-1}})\;.

(3.1.23)

Consider the contribution of the identity permutation to (
3.1.22
),
which is

\sumop\displaylimits_{j,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{f}_{1}(j)\hat{f}_{2}(i_{1})\dots\hat{f}_{n}(i_{n-1})\hat{g}(j)e_{i_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\dots e_{i_{n-1}}(x_{i_{n-1}})\;.

(3.1.24)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle f(z,x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})g(z)\dd\!z

(3.1.25)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\sumop\displaylimits_{j,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{f}_{1}(j)\hat{f}_{2}(i_{1})\dots\hat{f}_{n}(i_{n-1})\hat{g}(k)e_{j}(z)e_{k}(z)e_{i_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\dots e_{i_{n-1}}(x_{i_{n-1}})\dd\!z\;.

(3.1.26)

Orthogonality of eigenfunctions implies

\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}e_{j}(z)e_{k}(z)\dd\!z=\delta_{jk}\;.

(3.1.27)

Therefore, (
3.1.25
) is equal to (
3.1.24
).
The argument is similar for other permutations, and for
m>1
.
∎

3.1.3
A construction of Gaussian fields

Consider now the following construction. For
h=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)e_{i}\in\mathbf{H}
, we define

\Psi(h)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)W(e_{i})e_{i}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)X_{i}e_{i}\;.

(3.1.28)

This is now an
\mathbf{H}
-valued random variable, whose value at point
x\in\Lambda
is

\Psi(h)(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)X_{i}e_{i}(x)\;.

(3.1.29)

Note furthermore that

\displaystyle\lVert\Psi(h)\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\Psi(h)(x)^{2}\dd\!x

(3.1.30)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)\hat{h}(j)X_{i}X_{j}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)\dd\!x

(3.1.31)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)^{2}X_{i}^{2}

(3.1.32)

by orthonormality of the
e_{i}
. Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert\Psi(h)\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{h}(i)^{2}=\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\;.

(3.1.33)

This shows that the map
\Psi
is an isometry from the Hilbert space
\mathbf{H}

to a subset of the Hilbert space
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}
of
\mathbf{H}
-valued random
variables that have finite variance.

In terms of Wiener chaos, since
\Psi(h)
is defined on the same
probability space
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
as the
X_{i}
, if makes sense to
decompose
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}
into Wiener chaoses, by viewing them
as random variables inded by
x\in\Lambda
. In particular,

\Psi(h)
belongs to the first Wiener chaos. This allows us to work
within the framework of separable Hilbert space. For a general construction of
Gaussian measures on separable Banach spaces, see
Chapter 3 of Martin Hairer’s lecture notes
[
27
]
.

3.2
Gaussian white noise

3.2.1
Definition and basic properties

Consider the case where

\hat{h}=(1,1,1,\dots)

(3.2.1)

is the vector all of whose components are equal to
1
. Then (
3.1.28
)
becomes

\xi(x):=\Psi(h)(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}X_{i}e_{i}(x)\;.

(3.2.2)

This random variable is called
white noise
on
\Lambda
, because
it means that every Fourier mode is random with the same variance.
The trouble is that
h
does not belong to
\mathbf{H}
, since
\hat{h}
is not
square-summable. As a result,
\xi
does not have finite variance.

One way to try to make sense of this definition is to set, for any
finite
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\hat{h}_{N}=(\underbrace{1,1,1,\dots,1}_{\text{$N$ components}},0,0,\dots)\;.

(3.2.3)

In this way, we obtain

\xi_{N}(x):=\Psi(h_{N})(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{N}X_{i}e_{i}(x)\;.

(3.2.4)

Since
h_{N}
is in
\mathbf{H}
for any finite
N
,
\xi_{N}
is a random function
with finite variance for these
N
. It is called a
mollification
with cut-off
N
of white noise. Of course, the variance of
\xi_{N}

diverges as
N
goes to infinity.

Another way to make sense of
\xi
is to view it as a random
distribution. Let
\varphi:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
be a sufficiently regular so-called

test function
. Then we have, at least formally,

\displaystyle\langle\xi,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\xi(x)\varphi(x)\dd\!x=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}X_{i}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}e_{i}(x)\varphi(x)\dd\!x=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}X_{i}\hat{\varphi}(i)\;.

(3.2.5)

This is indeed well-defined for
\varphi\in\mathbf{H}
. Furthermore,
for any
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathbf{H}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\xi,\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\sumop\displaylimits_{j\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]\hat{\varphi}_{1}(i)\hat{\varphi}_{2}(j)

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\hat{\varphi}_{1}(i)\hat{\varphi}_{2}(i)=\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}\;.

(3.2.7)

This motivates the following definition.

{definition}

[Gaussian white noise on the torus]

Gaussian white noise on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is the random distribution
\xi
on

(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
such that for any smooth test function
\varphi\in\mathbf{H}
,

\langle\xi,\varphi\rangle
is a centred Gaussian random variable of variance

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
, while the covariance is given by

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\xi,\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr]=\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}

(3.2.8)

for any two smooth test functions
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathbf{H}
.

One immediate consequence of this definition is that if
\varphi_{1}
and

\varphi_{2}
have disjoint support (meaning that they cannot be different
from zero at the same point), then
\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}=0
, and
therefore
\langle\xi,\varphi_{1}\rangle
and
\langle\xi,\varphi_{2}\rangle
are independent
by Proposition
1.1
. The relation (
3.2.8
)
is sometimes formally written

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\xi(x)\xi(y)\bigr]=\delta(x-y)\;,

(3.2.9)

where
\delta(x-y)
is the Dirac distribution, that can be formally obtained
as a limit of scaled test functions localised at
x-y
.

Another important property of white noise is related to scaling. To this end,
define for
\lambda\in(0,1]
a scaling operator
{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}
acting on

\varphi\in\mathbf{H}
by

({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}\varphi)(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{d}}\varphi\biggl(\frac{x}{\lambda}\biggr)\;.

(3.2.10)

Let
\xi_{\lambda}
be the distribution defined by

\langle\xi_{\lambda},\varphi\rangle=\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}\varphi\rangle

(3.2.11)

for any test function
\varphi
.

{exercise}

Show that if
h:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
is an integrable function, then

h_{\lambda}(x)=h(\lambda x)
.

{proposition}

[Scaling of white noise]

For any
\lambda\in(0,1]
, one has equality in law

\xi_{\lambda}\eqinlaw\frac{1}{\lambda^{d/2}}\xi\;.

(3.2.12)

Proof:
.

Both processes are Gaussian and centred. Therefore, it suffices to show that
they have the same covariance. Given two compactly supported test functions

\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
, we have
by (
3.2.8
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi_{\lambda},\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\xi_{\lambda},\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}\varphi_{1}\rangle{\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}\varphi_{2}\rangle}\bigr]

(3.2.13)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}\varphi_{1})(x)({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}\varphi_{2})(x)\dd\!x

(3.2.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{d}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\varphi_{1}\biggl(\frac{x}{\lambda}\biggr)\varphi_{2}\biggl(\frac{x}{\lambda}\biggr)\dd\!x

(3.2.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{d}}\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{\mathscr{H}}\;,

(3.2.16)

where we have not changed the domain of integration, because the

\varphi_{i}
are compactly supported.
This is indeed the covariance of
\lambda^{-d/2}\xi
.
∎

The scaling property (
3.2.12
) can be interpreted as a form of
self-similarity, in a statistical sense.

3.2.2
Regularity of white noise*

Even though white noise is very irregular, one can find function spaces to
which it belongs. Two important such function spaces are fractional Sobolev spaces
and Besov–Hölder spaces of negative regularity index.

In order to define fractional Sobolev spaces, it is more convenient to
index basis elements of
\mathbf{H}=L^{2}(\Lambda)
by their wave number.
This means that they are of the form

e_{k}(x)=\e^{2\pi\icx\langle k,x\rangle}\;,\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\;,

(3.2.17)

where
k
should not be confused with the index
k
used in the Wiener isometry.
We then write

f(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{f}(k)e_{k}(x)

(3.2.18)

for the Fourier series of a function
f\in\mathbf{H}
.

{remark}

[Complex versus real Fourier series]

When one uses complex Fourier series, because of the reality condition

\smash{\overline{\hat{f}(k)}}=\hat{f}(-k)
, one should not take independent
X_{k}
in the
underlying probability space. Instead, they should satisfy

\mathbb{E}[X_{k}X_{\ell}]=\delta_{k,-\ell}\;.

(3.2.19)

It is also possible to use real instead of complex Fourier series and independent

X_{k}
, but this makes some computation slightly more tedious.

Note that we have

(\id-\Delta)e_{k}(x)=\lambda_{k}e_{k}(x)\;,\qquad\lambda_{k}=1+(2\pi)^{d}\lVert k\rVert^{2}\;.

(3.2.20)

The eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}
of the positive operator
\id-\Delta
play
a natural role as weights in Sobolev spaces.

{definition}

[Fractional Sobolev spaces]

For
s\mathbin{\geqslant}0
, the
fractional Sobolev space

H^{s}(\Lambda)
is the
space of functions
f\in\mathbf{H}=L^{2}(\Lambda)
such that

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lambda_{k}^{s}\lvert\hat{f}(k)\rvert^{2}<\infty\;.

(3.2.21)

In particular,
H^{0}(\Lambda)=L^{2}(\Lambda)
. For
s<0
,
H^{s}(\Lambda)
is
the closure of
L^{2}(\Lambda)
under the norm (
3.2.21
).

Note that in the definition (
3.2.21
) of the fractional Sobolev
norm, one may replace the weight
\lambda_{k}^{s}
by
(1+\lVert k\rVert^{2})^{s}
.
The resulting norm is equivalent.

{proposition}

[Sobolev regularity of white noise on the torus]

White noise
\xi
belongs to
H^{s}
for any
s<-\frac{d}{2}
, in the
sense that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert\xi\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}\bigr]<\infty\qquad\text{for all $s<-\dfrac{d}{2}$}\;.

(3.2.22)

Proof:
.

It follows from the Fourier representation (
3.2.2
) of white noise
that

\lVert\xi\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lambda_{k}^{s}X_{k}^{2}\;,

(3.2.23)

where the
X_{k}
are independent standard Gaussians. Taking the expectation,
we obtain

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert\xi\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lambda_{k}^{s}\;.

(3.2.24)

The sum is comparable to the integral

\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\lVert y\rVert^{2})^{s}\dd\!y\asymp\intop\nolimits_{1}^{\infty}r^{2s}r^{d-1}\dd\!r\;,

(3.2.25)

which is convergent if and only if
s<-\frac{d}{2}
.
Here we write
x\asymp y
to indicate that
c^{-1}x\mathbin{\leqslant}y\mathbin{\leqslant}cx

for some
c\mathbin{\geqslant}1
.
∎

A second scale of function spaces that are useful when working with white noise
are so-called Hölder–Besov spaces. To define them, we first introduce a
generalisation of the scaling operator (
3.2.10
) given by

({\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{x}\varphi)(y)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{d}}\varphi\biggl(\frac{y-x}{\lambda}\biggr)\;.

(3.2.26)

For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
B_{r}
the set of smooth test functions
\varphi:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
,
supported on a ball or radius
1
, whose partial derivatives up to order
r
are
bounded by
1
.

{definition}

[Hölder–Besov spaces]
For
\alpha<0
, the space
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Lambda)
consists in all Schwartz
distributions
\zeta\in{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}(\Lambda)
such that

\lVert\zeta\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}}=\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\biggl|\frac{\langle\zeta,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{x}\varphi\rangle}{\lambda^{\alpha}}\biggr|<\infty\;,

(3.2.27)

where
r=\lceil-\alpha\rceil
.

This definition says that if
\zeta\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}
, then

\langle\zeta,{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{x}\varphi\rangle
diverges at most like
\lambda^{-\alpha}

for any
x\in\Lambda
. It is thus a measure of how far the distribution is
from admitting a finite value at
x
.

{proposition}

[Hölder–Besov regularity of white noise on the torus]

White noise
\xi
belongs to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}
for any
\alpha<-\frac{d}{2}
.

Sketch of proof.

We follow the argument outlined in
[
16
, Theorem 2.7]
.
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we introduce a dyadic lattice discretisation of

\Lambda
on scale
2^{-k}
, given by

\Lambda_{k}=(2^{-k}\mathbb{Z})^{d}\cap\Lambda\;.

(3.2.28)

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, one can show the existence of a test function

\varphi
and a constant
C
such that

\lVert\xi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}}\mathbin{\leqslant}C\sup_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\sup_{x\in\Lambda_{k}}2^{k\alpha}\bigl|\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{2^{-k}}_{x}\varphi\rangle\bigr|\;.

(3.2.29)

See for instance
[
15
, Section12]
.
Bounding the suprema by sums, and taking the
p
th power, we arrive at

(\lVert\xi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}})^{p}\mathbin{\leqslant}C^{p}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in\Lambda_{k}}2^{k\alpha p}\bigl|\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{2^{-k}}_{x}\varphi\rangle\bigr|^{p}\;.

(3.2.30)

Since
\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{2^{-k}}_{x}\varphi\rangle
belongs to the first Wiener chaos,
its
p
th power belongs to the
p
th (inhomogeneous) chaos.
Using the scaling property (
3.2.12
) and (
3.2.10
),
we get

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{2^{-k}}_{x}\varphi\rangle^{2}\bigr]=\frac{1}{\lambda^{d}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,\phi\rangle^{2}\bigr]=\frac{1}{\lambda^{d}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\;.

(3.2.31)

The equivalence of moments (
3.1.14
) implies

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\xi,{\mathscr{S}}^{2^{-k}}_{x}\varphi\rangle^{p}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{C_{p}}{\lambda^{dp/2}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{p}

(3.2.32)

for some constant
C_{p}
depending only on
p
. Plugging this
into the expectation of (
3.2.30
) with
\lambda=2^{-k}

and using the fact that
\Lambda_{k}
has
2^{kd}
points, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\lVert\xi\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}})^{p}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}C^{\prime}_{p}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\mathbin{\geqslant}0}2^{kd}2^{k\alpha p}2^{kdp/2}=C^{\prime}_{p}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\geq 0}2^{k(d+\alpha p+dp/2)}

(3.2.33)

where
C^{\prime}_{p}
depends only on
p
.
If
\alpha p<-d(1+\frac{p}{2})
, the geometric series can be summed.
It then follows by a version of Kolmogorov’s continuity theorem (see
Theorem
3.3.2
below) that
there exists a version of
\xi
with bounded
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}
norm.
Since
p
can be taken arbitrarily large, the condition reduces to

\alpha<-\smash{\frac{d}{2}}
.
∎

{remark}

[Besov spaces]
Fractional Sobolev and Hölder–Besov spaces are particular instances
of a general class of functional spaces called
Besov spaces
.
The Besov space
{\mathscr{B}}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
is a Banach space for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
and all
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, where
\alpha
measures regularity, and

p
and
q
measure integrablity. Sobolev and Hölder–Besov spaces
correspond to the particular cases

H^{s}={\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{2,2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}={\mathscr{B}}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}\;.

(3.2.34)

3.3
The Gaussian free field

3.3.1
Definition and basic properties

Consider now the case where
h
is given by

\hat{h}(k)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\;,\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\;,

(3.3.1)

where
\lambda_{k}
is defined in (
3.2.20
).
The associated Gaussian field is

\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{X_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}e_{k}(x)\;.

(3.3.2)

This time, we have

\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}\asymp\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{1+\lVert y\rVert^{2}}\dd\!y\asymp\intop\nolimits_{1}^{\infty}\frac{r^{d-1}\dd\!r}{r^{2}}=\intop\nolimits_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\!r}{r^{3-d}}\;,

(3.3.3)

which converges if
d<2
. We conclude that the Gaussian field (
3.3.2
)
has a finite variance in dimension
d=1
, but not in higher dimension. Still,
this is somewhat better than for white noise. Its covariance is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(y)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[X_{k}X_{\ell}]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}}e_{k}(x)e_{\ell}(y)

(3.3.4)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{e_{k}(x)e_{-k}(y)}{\lambda_{k}}

(3.3.5)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{e_{k}(x-y)}{\lambda_{k}}=:G(x-y)

(3.3.6)

by Remark
3.2.2
and the fact that
e_{k}(x)e_{-k}(y)=e_{k}(x-y)
.
Note that while
G(0)
is defined only for
d=1
, one can show that

G(x)
is defined for all
d
if
x\neq 0
.
The function
G
has the following property, which states that it
can be considered as the inverse of the linear operator
(\id-\Delta)
.

{lemma}

For any
g\in\mathbf{H}
, the function
f
defined by

f(x)=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}G(x-y)g(y)\dd\!y\;,

(3.3.7)

if it exists, satisfies

(\id-\Delta)f(x)=g(x)\;.

(3.3.8)

Proof:
.

Assuming the integral is well-defined, we have

\displaystyle(\id-\Delta)f(x)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{(\id-\Delta)e_{k}(x)e_{-k}(y)}{\lambda_{k}}g(y)\dd\!y

(3.3.9)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}e_{k}(x)e_{-k}(y)g(y)\dd\!y

(3.3.10)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{g}(k)e_{k}(x)=g(x)

(3.3.11)

by Dirichlet’s theorem on Fourier series.
∎

This property motivates the following definition.

{definition}

[Gaussian free field and Green function]

•

The function
G
defined by (
3.3.6
) is called
the
Green function
associated with the operator
(\id-\Delta)
.
It is also written
G=(\id-\Delta)^{-1}
.

•

The Gaussian field defined by (
3.3.2
) is called
the
Gaussian free field (GFF) with covariance
(\id-\Delta)^{-1}
.

{exercise}

Show that for any test functions
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathbf{H}
, one has

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}},\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}},\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr]=\langle\varphi_{1},(\id-\Delta)^{-1}\varphi_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}\;.

(3.3.12)

We can now easily adapt the proof of Proposition
3.2.2
to
the GFF (
3.3.2
).

{proposition}

[Sobolev regularity of the Gaussian free field]

The Gaussian free field
\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}
belongs to
H^{s}
for any
s<1-\frac{d}{2}
, in the
sense that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}\bigr]<\infty\qquad\text{for all $s<1-\dfrac{d}{2}$}\;.

(3.3.13)

Proof:
.

A similar computation as before shows that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}^{1-s}}\asymp\intop\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\dd\!y}{(1+\lVert y\rVert^{2})^{1-s}}\asymp\intop\nolimits_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\!r}{r^{3-d-2s}}\;.

(3.3.14)

This is finite if and only if
s<1-\frac{d}{2}
.
∎

For
d=1
, we obtain a bit more regularity than
L^{2}=H^{0}
, that we got in
the estimate (
3.3.3
). For dimensions
d\mathbin{\geqslant}2
, on the other hand,
we find again that the GFF is less regular than a function. We will examine
these two cases more closely in the next two sections.

3.3.2
The Gaussian free field on the circle
\mathbb{T}^{1}

Proposition
3.3.1
shows that the one-dimensional GFF
is a function that has better regularity properties than being merely
square-integable. In fact, one can show that it enjoys some Hölder
regularity. Recall the definition of classical Hölder spaces.

{definition}

[Hölder spaces of regularity
\alpha\in(0,1)
]
For
0<\alpha<1
, the space
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Lambda)
consists in all functions

f:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\lVert f\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}}=\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\lvert f(x)\rvert+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Lambda\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert}{\lVert x-y\rVert^{\alpha}}<\infty\;.

(3.3.15)

A classical result allowing the obtain Hölder regularity of a
stochastic process is due to Kolmogorov.

{theorem}

[Kolmogorov continuity criterion]

Let
(\phi(x))_{x\in[0,L]}
be a stochastic process such that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert\phi(y)-\phi(x)\rVert^{\mu}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}C\lvert y-x\rvert^{1+\nu}

(3.3.16)

for all
x,y\in[0,L]
, for some constants
\mu,\nu>0
. Then
there exists a modification of the process
(\phi(x))_{x\in[0,L]}
whose
paths belong to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\frac{\nu}{\mu}
.

We then have the following result, which shows that the one-dimensional
GFF has the same regularity as Brownian motion. Its proof can be seen
as a much easier relative of the proof of Proposition
3.2.2
.

{proposition}

[Hölder regularity of the GFF on
\mathbb{T}^{1}
]

The GFF on the circle belongs to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
for all
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof:
.

For
x,y\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(y)-\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)\bigr)^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[X_{k}X_{\ell}]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}}\bigl(e_{k}(y)-e_{k}(x)\bigr)\overline{\bigl(e_{\ell}(y)-e_{\ell}(x)\bigr)}

(3.3.17)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}\lvert e_{k}(y)-e_{k}(x)\rvert^{2}\;.

(3.3.18)

The trigonometric identity
\lvert\e^{\icx\theta}-1\rvert^{2}=4\sin^{2}\Bigl(\frac{\theta}{2}\Bigr)

yields

\lvert e_{k}(y)-e_{k}(x)\rvert^{2}=\lvert\e^{\icx k(y-x)}-1\rvert^{2}=4\sin^{2}(k(y-x))\mathbin{\leqslant}4\bigl[k^{2}(y-x)^{2}\vee 1\bigr]\;,

(3.3.19)

where
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
.
Therefore, if
\lvert y-x\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}1
, one has

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(y)-\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)\bigr)^{2}\bigr]\lesssim\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(k^{2}(y-x)^{2})\vee 1}{1+k^{2}}\asymp\intop\nolimits_{1}^{1/\lvert y-x\rvert}(y-x)^{2}\dd\!r+\intop\nolimits_{1/\lvert y-x\rvert}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\!r}{r^{2}}\asymp\lvert y-x\rvert\;.

(3.3.20)

On the other hand, if
\lvert y-x\rvert>1
, we can simply bound

\lvert e_{k}(y)-e_{k}(x)\rvert^{2}
by
4
. We conclude that there exists a constant

C_{0}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(y)-\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)\bigr)^{2}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}C_{0}\lvert y-x\rvert

(3.3.21)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
.
By the equivalence of moments bound (
3.1.14
),
it follows that for any
p>0
, there exists a constant
C(p)
such that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(y)-\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)\bigr)^{2p}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}C(p)\lvert x-y\rvert^{p}\;.

(3.3.22)

Kolmogorov’s criterion thus applies with
\mu=2p
and
\nu=p-1
, showing that

\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)
is (up to a modification) Hölder continuous with exponent

\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{p})
. Since
p
can be taken arbitrarily large, the result
follows.
∎

{remark}

[Link between
\alpha>0
and
\alpha<0
]
The definition (
3.3.15
) of the Hölder norm for
positive
\alpha
looks very different from the
definition (
3.2.27
). However, one can show
that it is equivalent to

\lVert f\rVert_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}}=\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{0}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\biggl|\frac{\langle f-f(x),{\mathscr{S}}^{\lambda}_{x}\varphi\rangle}{\lambda^{\alpha}}\biggr|\;.

(3.3.23)

We can also easily estimate moments of the GFF. By translation invariance,
these do not depend on the point
x
. The second moment is given by

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)^{2}\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}=G(0)\;,

(3.3.24)

which is finite for
d=1
, cf. (
3.3.3
). Odd moments of the GFF
are equal to zero, while even moments behave as follows.

{proposition}

[Moments of the GFF on
\mathbb{T}^{1}
]
For any
p>1
, there exists a constant
C(p)
such that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)^{2p}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}C(p)\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)^{2}\bigr]^{p}\;.

(3.3.25)

Proof:
.

A direct application of the equivalence of moments
bound (
3.1.14
) shows that (
3.3.25
)
holds with
C(p)=(2p-1)^{p}
.
We can actually get a slightly better bound by a direct computation.
Indeed, we have

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)^{2p}\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{2p}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[X_{k_{1}}\dots X_{k_{2p}}]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{2p}}}}\;.

(3.3.26)

By Isserlis’ theorem (Theorem
2.1.2
), the expectation vanishes
unless the
k_{i}
are pairwise equal, in which case it has value
1
. Since
there are
(2p-1)!!
pairwise matchings, we get

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)^{2p}\bigr]=(2p-1)!!\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{p}}}=(2p-1)!!\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)^{2}\bigr]^{p}\;.

(3.3.27)

We thus obtain (
3.3.25
) with
C(p)=(2p-1)!!
.
Using (
1.2.110
) and Stirling’s formula, we
find that
(2p-1)!!
behaves asymptotically like

\sqrt{2}(2p)^{p}\e^{-p}
, which is slightly better that

(2p-1)^{p}
.
∎

3.3.3
The Gaussian free field on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}

We have seen that the GFF on the two-dimensional torus has infinite variance,
and belongs only to Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
with
s<0
. By an argument similar
to the one used in Proposition
3.2.2
, one can also show
that it belongs to the Besov–Hölder spaces
{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}
with
\alpha<0
.
For this reason, it is not possible to define powers of
\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}
.

To circumvent this difficulty, we can use the idea introduced at the
beginning of Section
3.2.1
, which is to work with
a cut-off
N
.

{definition}

[Truncated two-dimensional Gaussian free field]
For
N\mathbin{\geqslant}1
, let
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\colon\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N\}
,
where
\lvert k\rvert=\lvert k_{1}\rvert+\lvert k_{2}\rvert
.
The
truncated two-dimensional Gaussian free field (GFF)
with covariance

(\id-\Delta_{N})^{-1}
on
\Lambda
is defined as

\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x):=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{X_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}e_{k}(x)\;.

(3.3.28)

Here
\Delta_{N}
is the restriction of
\Delta
to the subspace
E_{N}
of
\mathbf{H}

spanned by Fourier basis functions
e_{k}
with
\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N
.

The name is justified by the fact that the same computation as
in (
3.3.6
) gives

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x)\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(y)\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}e_{k}(x-y)=:G_{N}(x-y)\;,

(3.3.29)

where
G_{N}
is the Green function of
(\id-\Delta_{N})
.
In particular, the variance at any
x
is given by

C_{N}:=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x)^{2}\bigr]=G_{N}(0)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}=\Tr\bigl[(\id-\Delta_{N})^{-1}\bigr]\;.

(3.3.30)

It is not hard to see that
C_{N}
diverges like
\log(N)
as
N\to\infty
.

{exercise}

Show that

C_{N}=\dfrac{\log N}{2\pi}+{\mathcal{O}}(1)

as
N\to\infty
, by viewing (
3.3.30
) as a Riemann sum.

Since the variance
C_{N}
does not depend on
x
, we can define for any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1

the quantity

{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}={\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}:=H_{n}(\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}(x);C_{N})\;,

(3.3.31)

called
n
th Wick power
of the truncated GFF,
where
H_{n}(\phi;C_{N})
denotes the scaled Hermite polynomial
introduced in (
2.2.1
). For every
x\in\Lambda
,

{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}
is a random variable belonging to the
n
th
homogeneous Wiener chaos
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}
. The function
{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}^{n}\mathpunct{:}}
is
also an
\mathbf{H}
-valued random variable. These random variables
are independent for different
n
, while their variance is
bounded uniformly in the cut-off
N
, as shows the following
very useful result.

{proposition}

[Uniform bound on the variance of Wick powers]

For every
n\mathbin{\geqslant}1
,

\sup_{N\mathbin{\geqslant}1}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2}\biggr]<\infty\;.

(3.3.32)

To prove this result, we will need the following inequality.

{lemma}

[Young-type inequality]

Fix integers
d>n,m>0
such that
n+m>d
. Then there
exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
k
, such that

\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\\
k_{1}+k_{2}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lVert k_{1}\rVert^{n}\lVert k_{2}\rVert^{m}}\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{C}{\lVert k\rVert^{n+m-d}}

(3.3.33)

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Proof:
.

We may restrict the sum to
(k_{1},k_{2})
such that
\lVert k_{1}\rVert\mathbin{\geqslant}\lVert k_{2}\rVert
, and
multiply the end result by
2
. Since
k_{1}+k_{2}=k
, we cannot have both

\lVert k_{1}\rVert<\frac{1}{2}\lVert k\rVert
and
\lVert k_{2}\rVert<\frac{1}{2}\lVert k\rVert
.
The half sum can thus be decomposed as

S_{1}+S_{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\\
k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
\lVert k_{2}\rVert\mathbin{\leqslant}\lVert k_{1}\rVert\wedge\lVert k\rVert/2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lVert k_{1}\rVert^{n}\lVert k_{2}\rVert^{m}}+\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\\
k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
\lVert k_{1}\rVert\mathbin{\geqslant}\lVert k_{2}\rVert>\lVert k\rVert/2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lVert k_{1}\rVert^{n}\lVert k_{2}\rVert^{m}}\;.

(3.3.34)

Since
\lVert k_{2}\rVert\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{1}{2}\lVert k\rVert
implies
\lVert k_{1}\rVert\mathbin{\geqslant}\frac{1}{2}\lVert k\rVert
,
we have

S_{1}\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{2^{n}}{\lVert k\rVert^{n}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\\
\lVert k_{2}\rVert\mathbin{\leqslant}\lVert k\rVert/2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lVert k_{2}\rVert^{m}}\lesssim\frac{2^{n}}{\lVert k\rVert^{n}}\intop\nolimits_{1}^{\lVert k\rVert/2}\frac{r^{d-1}\dd\!r}{r^{m}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lVert k\rVert^{n+m-d}}\;.

(3.3.35)

As for
S_{2}
, it satisfies

S_{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\\
\lVert k_{2}\rVert>\lVert k\rVert/2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lVert k_{2}\rVert^{n+m}}\lesssim\intop\nolimits_{\lVert k\rVert/2}^{\infty}\frac{r^{d-1}\dd\!r}{r^{n+m}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lVert k\rVert^{n+m-d}}\;,

(3.3.36)

which yields the result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\mathbb{E}\bigl[{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(y)\mathpunct{:}}\bigr]\dd\!x\dd\!y

(3.3.37)

\displaystyle=n!\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(x)\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}}}(y)\bigr]^{n}\dd\!x\dd\!y

(3.3.38)

\displaystyle=n!\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}e_{k}(x-y)\biggr)^{n}\dd\!x\dd\!y

(3.3.39)

\displaystyle=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{n}}}\biggl|\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}e_{k_{1}}(x)\dots e_{k_{n}}(x)\dd\!x\biggr|^{2}

(3.3.40)

\displaystyle=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}\\
k_{1}+\dots+k_{n}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{n}}}\;,

(3.3.41)

where we have used Proposition
1.2.2
to get the second
line, and (
3.3.29
) to get the third line.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
n\mathbin{\geqslant}2
, let

S(n,k)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\\
k_{1}+\dots+k_{n}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{n}}}\;.

(3.3.42)

Then Lemma
3.3.3
, together with an index shift, shows that

S(2,k)\mathbin{\leqslant}C(2)\lambda_{k}^{-1}
for some constant
C(2)
, and by induction one gets

S(n,k)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}}S(n-1,k-k_{1})\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{C(n)}{\lambda_{k}}

(3.3.43)

for some finite
C(n)
. In particular,
S(n,0)
is bounded.
Since this provides an upper bound uniform in
N
for (
3.3.41
),
the result is proved.
∎

By the equivalence of moments bound (
3.1.14
), we also
have

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2p}\biggr]\mathbin{\leqslant}(2p-1)^{np}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{\mathchoice{\mbox{$\displaystyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\textstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\rm GFF$}}{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\rm GFF$}},N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2}\biggr]^{p}\;,

(3.3.44)

which is bounded uniformly in the cut-off
N
for all
p>1
.
We thus conclude that all moments of Wick powers of the two-dimensional
GFF are well-defined, when viewed as limits as
N\to\infty
of the
truncated GFF. Note that the same holds in dimensions
d>2
, the difference
being that the variance
C_{N}
computed in (
3.3.30
) diverges
like
N^{d-2}
instead of
\log(N)
. This faster divergence causes new
problems for non-linear fields, as we will see in Section
4.4
.

Chapter 4
The
\Phi^{4}
model

The
\Phi^{4}
model on the
d
-dimensional torus is arguably the simplest
example of non-linear field theory. It originated as a toy model in
Euclidean Quantum Field Theory, and its behaviour is very different depending
on the dimension
d
.

4.1
Definition of the model

Let
\Lambda=\mathbb{T}^{d}
be the
d
-dimensional torus for some
d\mathbin{\geqslant}1
.
Given constants
\alpha\mathbin{\geqslant}0
,
m>0
and a function
\phi:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
,
we define its energy

{\mathscr{H}}_{d,\alpha}(\phi)=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{m^{2}}{2}\phi(x)^{2}+\alpha\phi(x)^{4}\biggr]\dd\!x\;.

(4.1.1)

The name
\Phi^{4}
model
is due to the term
\phi(x)^{4}
in the integral.
It is called the
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model if we want to emphasize the value of the
dimension. The parameter
m
has the physical interpretation of a mass.
We will take it equal to
1
in what follows.

The
\Phi^{4}_{d}
measure is the probability measure
\mu_{d,\alpha}
on

\mathbf{H}=L^{2}(\Lambda,\dd\!x)
(or on a suitable a space of functions
\phi:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
),
formally defined by

\mu_{d,\alpha}\sim\frac{1}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,\alpha}}\e^{-{\mathscr{H}}_{d,\alpha}(\phi)}\dd\!\phi\;,

(4.1.2)

where
{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,\alpha}
is the normalisation. This is called a
Gibbs measure
,
and
{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,\alpha}
is known in statistical physics as the
partition function
.

As such, this definition does not make sense, since there is no such thing as
Lebesgue measure on
\mathbf{H}
. Note however that for
\alpha=0
, we can
integrate by parts, to obtain

\displaystyle{\mathscr{H}}_{d,0}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\bigl[-\Delta\phi(x)\phi(x)+\phi(x)^{2}\bigr]\dd\!x

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi,[\id-\Delta]\phi\rangle_{\mathbf{H}}\;.

(4.1.4)

In view of the expression (
2.1.1
) of the density of
a finite-dimensional Gaussian measure, we can interpret

\mu_{d,0}(\dd\!\phi)
as the law of a centred Gaussian field with
covariance
(\id-\Delta)^{-1}
that we have studied in Section
3.3
.
This means that for a random variable
F:\mathbf{H}\to\mathbb{R}
, we can try to define
its expectation under
\mu_{d,0}
as

\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{d,0}}\!\left[F\right]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[F\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{X_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}e_{k}\biggr)\biggr]\;,

(4.1.5)

where the
X_{i}
are independent, identically distributed standard
Gaussians, and the
\lambda_{k}=1+(2\pi)^{d}\lVert k\rVert^{2}
are eigenvalues
of the operator
\id-\Delta
.

{example}

•

Let
F(\phi)=\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Then

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{d,0}}\bigl[\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{X_{k}^{2}}{\lambda_{k}}\biggr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}\;,

(4.1.6)

which we have seen is the variance of the Gaussian free field.
This is finite for
d=1
(cf. (
3.3.24
)), but not for
d\mathbin{\geqslant}2
.
By translation invariance, (
4.1.6
) is also the expectation
of
\phi(x)^{2}
for any
x\in\Lambda
.

•

Let
F(\phi)=\phi(x)\phi(y)
for
x,y\in\Lambda
.
Then we have seen in (
3.3.6
) that

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{d,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)\phi(y)\bigr]=G(x-y)

(4.1.7)

is the Green function, which is defined for all
x,y
if
d=1
, and
for all
d
if
x\neq y
.

This suggests using
\mu_{d,0}
as a reference measure, instead of the
non-existent Lebesgue measure on
\mathbf{H}
, and to define expectations
under
\mu_{d,\alpha}
with
\alpha>0
by

\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{d,\alpha}}\!\left[F\right]=\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,0}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{d,0}}\biggl[F(\phi)\exp\biggl\{-\alpha\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{4}\dd\!x\biggr\}\biggr]\;.

(4.1.8)

In particular, taking
F=1
, we obtain

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,0}}={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{d,0}}\biggl[\exp\biggl\{-\alpha\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{4}\dd\!x\biggr\}\biggr]\;.

(4.1.9)

If we manage to compute this ratio, at least perturbatively for small

\alpha
, it gives us access to more general expectations of the
form (
4.1.8
).

One example of random variables
F
that are physically relevant is

F=\phi(x_{1})\dots\phi(x_{n})
, for given
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\Lambda
,
leading to so-called
n
-point functions

G_{n,d,\alpha}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{d,\alpha}}\bigl[\phi(x_{1})\dots\phi(x_{n})\bigr]\;.

(4.1.10)

But one can also consider
F
depending on an integral involving
\phi
,
such as the energy itself.

{exercise}

Compute the
n
-point function
G_{n,d,0}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
in the case

\alpha=0
.

In what follows, we will focus on the ratio (
4.1.9
) of
partition functions, since this is the first step in computing expectations
of more general random variables.

4.2
The
\Phi^{4}_{1}
model

In this section, we consider the
\Phi^{4}
model on the one-dimensional
torus
\Lambda=\mathbb{T}^{1}=\mathbb{T}
, that is, the circle. As we have seen, all moments of the GFF are
well-defined in this case. The ratio of partition functions (
4.1.9
) becomes

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\exp\biggl\{-\alpha\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{4}\dd\!x\biggr\}\biggr]\;.

(4.2.1)

One way to proceed is to expand the exponential, leading to

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}\asymp\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{4}\dd\!x\biggr)^{n}\biggr]\;,

(4.2.2)

where we use the symbol
\asymp
because we do not know if this series
is convergent (in fact, one can show that it is not!).

1
2
3
4
5
6
7
8
k_{1}
k_{3}
k_{5}
k_{8}
1
2
3
4
5
6
7
8
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{6}
k_{1}

Figure 4.1:
Two pairwise matchings contributing to the
sum (
4.2.7
). An oriented arrow from vertex

i
to vertex
j
means that
k_{j}=-k_{i}
.

The term
n=1
in the sum (
4.2.2
) can be computed
using (
3.3.26
). By Isserlis’ theorem, we have

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)^{4}\bigr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[X_{k_{1}}X_{k_{2}}X_{k_{3}}X_{k_{4}}]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{1}}\lambda_{k_{2}}\lambda_{k_{3}}\lambda_{k_{4}}}}=3\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}\lambda_{k_{2}}}=3G(0)^{2}\;,

(4.2.3)

where the factor
3
counts the number of pairwise matchings of
the four indices. Therefore, we also have

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{4}\dd\!x\biggr)\biggr]=3G(0)^{2}\;.

(4.2.4)

For the term
n=2
, we find

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{4}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{4}\dd\!x\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(y)^{4}\dd\!y\biggr]

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{8}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[X_{k_{1}}\dots X_{k_{8}}]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{8}}}}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}e_{k_{1}}(x)\dots e_{k_{4}}(x)e_{k_{5}}(y)\dots e_{k_{8}}(y)\dd\!x\dd\!y

(4.2.6)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dots,k_{8}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+k_{4}=0\\
k_{5}+k_{6}+k_{7}+k_{8}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[X_{k_{1}}\dots X_{k_{8}}]}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{8}}}}\;.

(4.2.7)

The combinatorics is now more complicated, since we have to sum over all
pairwise matchings of the
k_{i}
that satisfy the two sum constraints.
Figure
4.1
gives two examples of such pairings.

4.2.1
Wick calculus and Feynman diagrams

So far, we have not used the power of the Wiener chaos decomposition. One way
to do this is to modify the energy (
4.1.1
) to

{\mathscr{H}}_{1,\alpha}^{\text{Wick}}(\phi)=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\lVert\nabla\phi(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\phi(x)^{2}+\alpha{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\biggr]\dd\!x\;.

(4.2.8)

This is now a different model, since we have replaced the fourth
power
\phi(x)^{4}
by the fourth Wick power

{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}=H_{4}(\phi(x)^{4};C)
, where
C
should be taken equal
to the covariance
G(0)
. One could transform this into the original
model by adding a suitable multiple of the second Wick power

{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}
and a suitable constant, but we will not explore
this further here, and work with the new energy. The ratio of partition
functions (
4.2.1
) now becomes

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\exp\biggl\{-\alpha\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr\}\biggr]\asymp\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{n}\biggr]\;.

(4.2.9)

The coefficient
n=1
is simply

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr]=0\;,

(4.2.10)

since Wick powers are centred.
The coefficient
n=2
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(y)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\bigr]\dd\!x\dd\!y

(4.2.11)

\displaystyle=4!\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)\phi(y)\bigr]^{4}\dd\!x\dd\!y

(4.2.12)

\displaystyle=4!\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}G(x-y)^{4}\dd\!x\dd\!y\;,

(4.2.13)

where we have used Proposition
1.2.2

and (
4.1.7
).
Note that since
G
is translation invariant,
one can replace the double integral in (
4.2.13
) by
a simple integral of
G(x)^{4}
, but this will not be important here.
We can represent (
4.2.13
) graphically as

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2}\biggr]=4!\Pi\Bigl(\,\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox to20.77pt{\vbox to11.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-5.69046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 12.45 25.59 12.45 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -12.45 25.59 -12.45 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.57 0 C 1.57 0.87 0.87 1.57 0 1.57 C -0.87 1.57 -1.57 0.87 -1.57 0 C -1.57 -0.87 -0.87 -1.57 0 -1.57 C 0.87 -1.57 1.57 -0.87 1.57 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\Bigr)\;,

(4.2.14)

where the two vertices of the graph indicate the two integration variables

x
and
y
in (
4.2.13
), while the four edges indicate
the four factors
G(x-y)
. The map
\Pi
stands for evaluation of the integral.
This is a first example of
Feynman diagram
.

In order to generalise this computation to higher powers, we reformulate it
in terms of the Wiener isometry. For given
x\in\Lambda
, considered
as a parameter, define

h_{x}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{h}_{x}(k)e_{k}\;,\qquad\text{where }\hat{h}_{x}(k):=\frac{e_{k}(x)}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\;.

(4.2.15)

For every
x\in\Lambda
,
h_{x}
is an element of
\mathbf{H}=L^{2}(\Lambda,\dd\!x)
.
Furthermore,

\hat{I}_{1}(h_{x})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{h}_{x}(k)X_{k}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{X_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}e_{k}(x)=\phi(x)

(4.2.16)

is the Gaussian free field. It follows that

{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}=H_{4}(\phi(x);C)=\hat{I}_{4}(h_{x}^{\otimes 4})\;,

(4.2.17)

so that Proposition
2.3.3
yields

\displaystyle{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(y)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}

\displaystyle=\hat{I}_{4}(h_{x}^{\otimes 4})\hat{I}_{4}(h_{y}^{\otimes 4})

(4.2.18)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{4}\hat{I}_{8-2p}(h_{x}^{\otimes 4}\star_{p}h_{y}^{\otimes 4})\;.

(4.2.19)

Taking the expectation, we obtain

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(y)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\bigr]=\hat{I}_{0}(h_{x}^{\otimes 4}\star_{4}h_{y}^{\otimes 4})\;,

(4.2.20)

and the definition (
2.3.45
) of the contraction
f\star_{p}g
yields

\displaystyle\hat{I}_{0}(h_{x}^{\otimes 4}\star_{4}h_{y}^{\otimes 4})

\displaystyle=4!\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}}\overline{\hat{h}_{x}^{\otimes 4}(k_{1},\dots,k_{4})}\hat{h}_{y}^{\otimes 4}(k_{1},\dots,k_{4})

(4.2.21)

\displaystyle=4!\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\overline{\hat{h}_{x}(k)}\hat{h}_{y}(k)\biggr)^{4}

(4.2.22)

\displaystyle=4!\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{e_{k}(x-y)}{\lambda_{k}}\biggr)^{4}

(4.2.23)

\displaystyle=4!G(x-y)^{4}\;.

(4.2.24)

Here we have used complex conjugates in the inner products because we work with
complex Fourier series.
We thus recover (
4.2.13
) in a way that may seem more
convoluted, but allows for generalisation to higher powers.
Indeed, in the case
n=3
we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(y)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(z)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}

\displaystyle=\hat{I}_{4}(h_{x}^{\otimes 4})\hat{I}_{4}(h_{y}^{\otimes 4})\hat{I}_{4}(h_{z}^{\otimes 4})

(4.2.25)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{4}\hat{I}_{8-2p}(h_{x}^{\otimes 4}\star_{p}h_{y}^{\otimes 4})\hat{I}_{4}(h_{z}^{\otimes 4})

(4.2.26)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{p=0}^{4}\sumop\displaylimits_{q=0}^{(8-2p)\wedge 4}\hat{I}_{12-2p-2q}\bigl((h_{x}^{\otimes 4}\star_{p}h_{y}^{\otimes 4})\star_{q}h_{z}^{\otimes 4}\bigr)\;.

(4.2.27)

h_{x}^{\otimes 4}
h_{y}^{\otimes 4}
h_{z}^{\otimes 4}

Figure 4.2:
One of the pairings occurring in the
expectation (
4.2.28
).

When taking the expectation, only terms with
2p+2q=12
remain. There is
actually only one option, which is to take
p=2
and
q=4
, yielding

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(y)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(z)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\bigr]=\hat{I}_{0}\bigl((h_{x}^{\otimes 4}\star_{2}h_{y}^{\otimes 4})\star_{4}h_{z}^{\otimes 4}\bigr)\;.

(4.2.28)

The contraction operations can be represented graphically, as we did in
Section
2.3.3
. The functions
h_{x}^{\otimes 4}
,
h_{y}^{\otimes 4}

and
h_{z}^{\otimes 4}
are represented each by a vertex with four legs.
The operation
\star_{2}
corresponds to pairing two legs of
h_{x}^{\otimes 4}

with two legs of
h_{y}^{\otimes 4}
, while the operation
\star_{4}

corresponds to pairing the remaining four legs of
h_{x}^{\otimes 4}

and
h_{y}^{\otimes 4}
with the four legs of
h_{z}^{\otimes 4}
, see
Figure
4.2
. The result is

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(y)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(z)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=2!\binom{4}{2}^{2}4!\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1},\dots,k_{6}\in\mathbb{Z}}h_{x}^{\otimes 4}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})h_{y}^{\otimes 4}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{5},k_{6})h_{z}^{\otimes 4}(k_{3},k_{4},k_{5},k_{6})

(4.2.29)

\displaystyle=1728\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{e_{k_{1}}(x-y)}{\lambda_{k_{1}}}\biggr)^{2}\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{e_{k_{2}}(y-z)}{\lambda_{k_{2}}}\biggr)^{2}\biggl(\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{e_{k_{3}}(x-z)}{\lambda_{k_{3}}}\biggr)^{2}

(4.2.30)

\displaystyle=1728\,G(x-y)^{2}G(y-z)^{2}G(x-z)^{2}\;.

(4.2.31)

It follows that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{3}\biggr]

\displaystyle=1728\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda^{3}}G(x-y)^{2}G(y-z)^{2}G(x-y)^{2}\dd\!x\dd\!y\dd\!z

(4.2.32)

\displaystyle=1728\,\Pi\Bigl(\,\raisebox{-5.5pt}{\hbox to19.35pt{\vbox to18.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-2.84544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 8.66 3.15 14.97 3.15 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 20.47 C 13.42 11.37 16.54 5.94 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.57 0 C 1.57 0.87 0.87 1.57 0 1.57 C -0.87 1.57 -1.57 0.87 -1.57 0 C -1.57 -0.87 -0.87 -1.57 0 -1.57 C 0.87 -1.57 1.57 -0.87 1.57 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
