\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\Bigr)\;.

(4.2.33)

With these examples, the pattern should have become clear. The terms in
the expansion (
4.2.9
) can be written as some combinatorial
coefficients, times an integral of a product of Green functions. To
formalise this, we make the following definition.

{definition}

[Vacuum Feynman diagram]
A
vacuum diagram
is a multigraph
\Gamma=({\mathscr{V}},{\mathscr{E}})
, meaning there can
be multiple edges between vertices. Its
valuation
is defined by

\Pi(\Gamma)=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda^{\mathscr{V}}}\prodop\displaylimits_{e\in{\mathscr{E}}}G(x_{e_{+}}-x_{e_{-}})\dd\!x\;,

(4.2.34)

where
e_{\pm}
are the vertices connected by the edge
e
.

The general principle behind the above examples is as follows.

{proposition}

[Expansion of moments into Feynman diagrams]
For any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}2
,

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{n}\biggr]=\sumop\displaylimits_{k}\Pi(\Gamma_{n,k})\;,

(4.2.35)

where the sum runs over all vacuum diagrams
\Gamma_{n,k}
with
n

vertices and
2n
edges, obtained as perfect pairwise matchings of

n
vertices of arity
4
(each vertex belongs to four edges),
when matchings of different legs are counted as different terms.

Proof:
.

For any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}2
, we write

\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x_{i})^{4}\mathpunct{:}}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\hat{I}_{4}(h_{x_{i}}^{\otimes 4}))\;.

(4.2.36)

Taking the expectation, we are left with the component in the zeroth
Wiener chaos, which by a repeated application of Proposition
2.3.3

can be represented as the sum over all pairwise matchings of
n
vertices
with
4
legs each. Each pairing gives rise to a Green function, and the
result follows by integrating over all
x_{i}
.
∎

{exercise}

Show that for
g
periodic,
the unique periodic solution of
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=f(x)-g(x)
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}f(x)\\
f^{\prime}(x)\end{pmatrix}=-\bigl[U(-1)-{\mathbbm{1}}\bigr]^{-1}\intop\nolimits_{x}^{x+1}U(x-y)\begin{pmatrix}0\\
g(x+y)\end{pmatrix}\dd\!y\;,\qquad U(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh(x)&\sinh(x)\\
\sinh(x)&\cosh(x)\end{pmatrix}\;.

(4.2.37)

Deduce that the Green function
G=(\id-\Delta)^{-1}
is bounded
in dimension
d=1
.

{exercise}

Give an upper bound on the number of vacuum diagrams
\Gamma_{n,k}

occurring in (
4.2.35
) for given
n
, by allowing the
graphs to have loops (edges connecting a vertex with itself).
Assuming this bound has the right order of magnitude, what does this
say about the convergence of the series (
4.2.9
)?

4.2.2
The linked-cluster theorem

The vacuum diagrams occurring in the
n
th power of the expansion need not
be connected. For instance, the
4
th power contains terms of the form

\,\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox to20.77pt{\vbox to11.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-5.69046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 12.45 25.59 12.45 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 10.03 4.68 15.56 4.68 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 10.03 -4.68 15.56 -4.68 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -12.45 25.59 -12.45 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.57 0 C 1.57 0.87 0.87 1.57 0 1.57 C -0.87 1.57 -1.57 0.87 -1.57 0 C -1.57 -0.87 -0.87 -1.57 0 -1.57 C 0.87 -1.57 1.57 -0.87 1.57 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 25.59 0 M 27.16 0 C 27.16 0.87 26.46 1.57 25.59 1.57 C 24.72 1.57 24.02 0.87 24.02 0 C 24.02 -0.87 24.72 -1.57 25.59 -1.57 C 26.46 -1.57 27.16 -0.87 27.16 0 Z M 25.59 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}^{2}
, that arise from pairing the legs of two vertices between each
other, and of the other two vertices among themselves. However, a rather
remarkable result known in quantum field theory as

linked-cluster
theorem

[
10
,
34
,
35
]
states
that the
logarithm
of the ratio of partition functions, that is,
its cumulant expansion, contains only the connected graphs. More precisely,
it is obtained by keeping only the connected diagrams in the expansion.

{theorem}

[Linked-cluster theorem]
The cumulant expansion of the ratio of partition functions is given by

\log\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}\asymp\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\colon\Gamma_{n,k}\text{connected}}\Pi(\Gamma_{n,k})\;.

(4.2.38)

Proof:
.

This elegant proof is due to Dimitri Faure. We use the formalism of
convolution algebras introduced in Section
1.2.4
.
For an abstract variable
x
(unrelated to coordinates on
\Lambda
),
we write
\psi(x^{n})
for the coefficient of
(-\alpha)^{n}/n!
in the cumulant expansion (
4.2.38
). This means that

\log\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}\asymp\Lambda(\psi)(-\alpha)\;,

(4.2.39)

where
\Lambda
is the map introduced in (
1.2.59
).
By Proposition
1.2.4
, we have

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}\asymp\Lambda(\varphi)(-\alpha)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}\varphi(x^{n})\;,

(4.2.40)

where

\varphi(x^{n})=\exp_{\ast}(\psi)(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{k!}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\mathbin{\geqslant}1\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{k}!}\psi(x^{n_{1}})\dots\psi(x^{n_{k}})\;.

(4.2.41)

To deal with combinatorics, we assume that all vertices and edges of
the diagrams are numbered. Assuming (
4.2.38
)
is true, each
\psi(x^{n})
is a linear combination of connected graphs
with
n
vertices. Then (
4.2.41
) says that the coefficient
of
(-\alpha)^{n}/n!
in the expansion (
4.2.35
)
is obtained by all possible disjoint unions of connected graphs
such that the total number of vertices is
n
. The multinomial coefficient
accounts for the choices of vertices in the subgraphs, while the factor

1/k!
accounts for the fact that the order of the subgraphs is irrelevant.
Since this yields
all
pairings of
n
vertices, the result follows
from uniqueness of coefficients of power series.
∎

{example}

Since
\psi(x)=0
by (
4.2.10
), we obtain

\exp_{\ast}(\psi)(x^{4})=\psi(x^{4})+\binom{4}{2}\psi(x^{2})^{2}\;,

(4.2.42)

since the only allowed decompositions of
4
are
4
and
2+2
.
This means that the term of order
\alpha^{4}
in the
expansion (
4.2.35
) differs from the corresponding
term in the cumulant expansion by a term
\frac{1}{2}\binom{4}{2}\,\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox to20.77pt{\vbox to11.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-5.69046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 12.45 25.59 12.45 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 10.03 4.68 15.56 4.68 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 10.03 -4.68 15.56 -4.68 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -12.45 25.59 -12.45 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.57 0 C 1.57 0.87 0.87 1.57 0 1.57 C -0.87 1.57 -1.57 0.87 -1.57 0 C -1.57 -0.87 -0.87 -1.57 0 -1.57 C 0.87 -1.57 1.57 -0.87 1.57 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 25.59 0 M 27.16 0 C 27.16 0.87 26.46 1.57 25.59 1.57 C 24.72 1.57 24.02 0.87 24.02 0 C 24.02 -0.87 24.72 -1.57 25.59 -1.57 C 26.46 -1.57 27.16 -0.87 27.16 0 Z M 25.59 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}^{2}
.
The factor
\frac{1}{2}\binom{4}{2}=3
is precisely the number of pairwise
matchings of four vertices.

4.2.3
Asymptotic expansion

So far, we have not shown that the expansion (
4.2.35
)
is a genuine asymptotic expansion. We do this now, by proving the following
result.

{proposition}

[Asymptotic series]

For every
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
there exists a constant
M_{n}
such that the ratio of partition
functions satisfies

\biggl|\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}-\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{n}\frac{(-\alpha)^{m}}{m!}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{m}\biggr]\biggr|\mathbin{\leqslant}M_{n}\alpha^{n+1}\;.

(4.2.43)

We start by showing an a-priori bound on the Laplace transform.
To lighten notations, we will write

\mathbf{X}=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\;.

(4.2.44)

{lemma}

There exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such that for all
\alpha\in[0,\alpha_{0})
,
one has

0\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}1+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\;.

(4.2.45)

Proof:
.

In order to exploit signs, we write
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{X}_{0}-6C\mathbf{Y}_{0}+3C^{2}
, where

C=G(0)
and

\mathbf{X}_{0}=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{4}\dd\!x\mathbin{\geqslant}0\;,\qquad\mathbf{Y}_{0}=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\phi(x)^{2}\dd\!x\mathbin{\geqslant}0\;.

(4.2.46)

Therefore we have

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}}\bigr]=\e^{-3C^{2}\alpha}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{0}+6C\alpha\mathbf{Y}_{0}}\bigr]=\e^{-3C^{2}\alpha}\frac{\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}(\alpha)}{\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}(0)}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\tilde{\mu}(\alpha)}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{0}}\bigr]\;,

(4.2.47)

where
\tilde{\mu}(\alpha)
is a GFF with covariance

((1-6C\alpha)\id-\Delta)^{-1}
, and
\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}(\alpha)
denotes the normalisation
of this measure. Then we have

\displaystyle\log\frac{\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}(0)}{\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1-6\alpha+2\pi k^{2}}{1+2\pi k^{2}}

(4.2.48)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log\prodop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\biggl(1-\frac{6\alpha}{1+2\pi k^{2}}\biggr)

(4.2.49)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\log\biggl(1-\frac{6\alpha}{1+2\pi k^{2}}\biggr)\asymp-3\alpha\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{1+2\pi k^{2}}\;.

(4.2.50)

Since the sum converges, we obtain

\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}(\alpha)=\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}(0)[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha))
,
where
\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}(0)={\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}
. As a consequence,

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}}\bigr]={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\tilde{\mu}(\alpha)}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{0}}\bigr]\bigl(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\bigr)\;.

(4.2.51)

Since
\mathbf{X}_{0}
is positive, we have

0\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\tilde{\mu}(\alpha)}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{0}}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}1
,
which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.2.3
.

For
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let

D_{n}(t)=\e^{-t}-\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{n}\frac{(-t)^{m}}{m!}\;.

(4.2.52)

If
n
is even, we have

\begin{cases}0\mathbin{\leqslant}D_{n}(t)\mathbin{\leqslant}\dfrac{(-t)^{n}}{n!}&\text{if $t\mathbin{\geqslant}0$\;,}\\[10.0pt]
0\mathbin{\leqslant}D_{n}(t)\mathbin{\leqslant}\dfrac{(-t)^{n}}{n!}\e^{-t}&\text{if $t<0$\;.}\end{cases}

(4.2.53)

It follows that

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\lvert D_{n}(\alpha\mathbf{X})\rvert\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{\alpha^{n}}{n!}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{n}]\;,

(4.2.54)

while

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\lvert D_{n}(\alpha\mathbf{X})\rvert\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{X}<0}\bigr]

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{\alpha^{n}}{n!}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{n}\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{X}<0}]

(4.2.55)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}\frac{\alpha^{n}}{n!}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{2n}]\mathbb{E}[\e^{-2\alpha\mathbf{X}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{X}<0}]}\;,

(4.2.56)

by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. We know from Exercise
4.2.1

that
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{2n}]
is finite, while Lemma
4.2.3

shows that
\mathbb{E}[\e^{-2\alpha\mathbf{X}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{X}<0}]
is bounded as well.
A similar argument applies for odd
n
, with some signs reversed
in (
4.2.53
).
∎

With the computations made in Section
4.2.1
, we have thus
obtained that the ratio of partition functions satisfies

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}=1+12\alpha^{2}\Pi\Bigl(\,\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox to20.77pt{\vbox to11.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-5.69046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 12.45 25.59 12.45 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 8.66 3.15 14.97 3.15 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 20.47 C 13.42 11.37 16.54 5.94 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 0 M 25.2 0 C 25.2 0.87 24.49 1.57 23.62 1.57 C 22.75 1.57 22.05 0.87 22.05 0 C 22.05 -0.87 22.75 -1.57 23.62 -1.57 C 24.49 -1.57 25.2 -0.87 25.2 0 Z M 23.62 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\Bigr)+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{4})\;,

(4.2.57)

and more terms can be computed if needed. This expansion does not converge,
however, since the number of pairwise matchings grows like
(4n-1)!!

(cf. Exercise
4.2.1
), which by (
1.2.103
)
and Stirling’s formula behaves like
(n!)^{2}
. The constant
M_{n}

in (
4.2.43
) thus grows like
n!
. Such an expansion is called

Gevrey-
1
. The non-convergence of the expansion does not mean that
it is useless, but it means that there is an optimal value of
n
,
depending on
\alpha
, at which the expansion should be stopped to
obtain the smallest possible error bound.

{exercise}

Let
r(n)=n!\alpha^{n}
. By extending
r
to real arguments via
n!=\Gamma(n+1)
and
using Stirling’s formula, estimate the minimal value of
r(n)
for small

\alpha
. For what
n
is this minimal value reached?

4.2.4
Two-point function

x
z_{1}
z_{2}
y

Figure 4.3:
Pairing corresponding to the term
n=2
in the expansion
of the two-point function.

Let us briefly outline how the two-point function

G_{2,1,\alpha}(x,y)=\mathbb{E}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,\alpha}}\!\left[\phi(x)\phi(y)\right]

(4.2.58)

can be computed by a similar procedure. By (
4.1.8
) we have

G_{2,1,\alpha}(x,y)=\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,0}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{1,\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)\phi(y)\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}}\bigr]\;,

(4.2.59)

where
\mathbf{X}
denotes the integral of the fourth Wick power, cf. (
4.2.44
).
Expanding the exponential, we get

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)\phi(y)\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}}\bigr]\asymp\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)\phi(y)\mathbf{X}^{n}\bigr]\;.

(4.2.60)

We already know that the term
n=0
is equal to
G(x-y)
. For
n=1
, we
find

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)\phi(y)\mathbf{X}\bigr]=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)\phi(y){\mathpunct{:}\phi(z)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\bigr]\dd\!z=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\hat{I}_{1}(h_{x})\hat{I}_{1}(h_{y})\hat{I}_{4}(h_{z}^{\otimes 4})\bigr]\dd\!z\;.

(4.2.61)

This is equal to zero, because there is no perfect matching, leaving no
free legs, of two vertices with one leg each and one vertex with four legs.
For
n=2
, we obtain

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\phi(x)\phi(y)\mathbf{X}^{2}\bigr]=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{1,0}}\bigl[\hat{I}_{1}(h_{x})\hat{I}_{1}(h_{y})\hat{I}_{4}(h_{z_{1}}^{\otimes 4})\hat{I}_{4}(h_{z_{2}}^{\otimes 4})\bigr]\dd\!z_{1}\dd\!z_{2}\;.

(4.2.62)

This is now different from zero, because there exist perfect matchings, as
show in Figure
4.3
. We thus conclude that the
expectation of
\phi(x)\phi(y)\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}}
can be computed in a
similar way as for the ratio of partition functions, except that it now
involves Feynman diagrams with two free legs, labeld
x
and
y
.

{remark}

There exists a slightly different representation of the two-point function,
that avoids having to divide by the ratio of partition functions, based on the Schwinger–Dyson equations (
2.1.6
)
[
14
]
.

4.3
The
\Phi^{4}_{2}
model

In this section, we consider the
\Phi^{4}
model on the two-dimensional torus

\Lambda=\mathbb{T}^{2}
. We have seen in Section
3.3.1
that the GFF
in dimension
2
has infinite variance. In fact, one can show that the Green
function behaves like

G(x)\asymp\bigl|\log(\lVert x\rVert)\bigr|\;.

(4.3.1)

This can be seen by computing the Green function
G_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
of the full plane

\mathbb{R}^{2}
using polar coordinates, and then periodising it via

G_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}G_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(x-k)\;.

(4.3.2)

We know however that the truncated two-dimensional GFF has finite moments.
This suggests considering the modified energy

{\mathscr{H}}_{2,\alpha,N}^{\text{Wick}}(\phi_{N})=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\lVert\nabla\phi_{N}(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\phi_{N}(x)^{2}+\alpha{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}\biggr]\dd\!x\;,

(4.3.3)

defined for the truncated field

\phi_{N}(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{X_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}e_{k}(x)\;,

(4.3.4)

which has variance

C_{N}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}\asymp\log(N)\;.

(4.3.5)

We recall that
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\colon\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N\}
,
where
\lvert k\rvert=\lvert k_{1}\rvert+\lvert k_{2}\rvert
.

The situation is now similar to the one we have encountered in dimension
1
,
except for the important difference that the constant
C_{N}
occuring
in (
4.3.3
) depends on the cut-off
N
. The model thus changes
with
N
. This is an instance of what is called
renormalisation
in
quantum field theory, and
C_{N}
is known as a
counterterm
.

The computations from Section
4.2.4
can now be
repeated in the same way, and result in an expansion of the form

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{2,\alpha,N}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{2,0,N}}\asymp\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{n}\biggr]

(4.3.6)

of the ratio of partition functions, where the expectations of powers of
the fourth Wick power are given by a sum of valuations of the same Feynman
vacuum diagrams as in (
4.2.35
). The only difference
is that the diagrams involve the Green function satisfying (
4.3.1
).
It is not immediately obvous that the diagrams all have a finite valuation,
but we will show in Section
4.4.1
below that this is indeed
the case, uniformly in the cut-off
N
.

The linked-cluster theorem is also true in this case. However, the proof of
the a priori bound on the Laplace transform given in
Lemma
4.2.3
does not work here, because of the diverging
constant
C_{N}
in the energy (
4.3.3
). Fortunately, there
is an alternative proof of that bound, due to Nelson.

4.3.1
Nelson’s estimate

In order to bound the Laplace transform of the fourth Wick power
\mathbf{X}
,
we first derive the following consequence of the equivalence of moments
bound (
3.1.14
).

{lemma}

Fix two cut-offs
M>N\mathbin{\geqslant}1
. Then for any
p>1
and
n\mathbin{\geqslant}2
,
there exists a constant
K_{n}
depending only on
n
such that

{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{M}(x)^{n}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{M}}\dd\!x-\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{n}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2p}\biggr]^{1/(2p)}\mathbin{\leqslant}K_{n}(2p-1)^{n/2}\frac{(\log N)^{n-2}}{N}\;.

(4.3.7)

Proof:
.

By the same computation as in the proof of Proposition
3.3.3
,
see (
3.3.41
), we have

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{M}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{M}}\dd\!x\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}\dd\!x\biggr]=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}\\
k_{1}+\dots+k_{n}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{n}}}\;.

(4.3.8)

It follows by expanding the square that

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{M}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{M}}\dd\!x-\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}^{n}(x)\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{2}\biggr]=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{M}\setminus{\mathscr{K}}_{N}\\
k_{1}+\dots+k_{n}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{n}}}\;.

(4.3.9)

By a similar argument as in Lemma
3.3.3
, one can show by induction on
n
that

\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in{\mathscr{K}}_{M}\setminus{\mathscr{K}}_{N}\\
k_{1}+\dots+k_{n}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k_{1}}\dots\lambda_{k_{n}}}\mathbin{\leqslant}K_{n}\min\Biggl\{\frac{(\log N)^{n-2}}{N^{2}},\frac{(\log\lVert k\rVert)^{n-2}}{\lVert k\rVert^{2}}\Biggr\}\;,

(4.3.10)

where
K_{n}
does not depend on
N
or
M
. Therefore, the result follows by taking
k=0

in the above bound and using equivalence of moments
(Theorem (
3.1.14
)).
∎

{proposition}

[Nelson’s estimate]

For any
\alpha\mathbin{\geqslant}0
,
there exists a constant
K>0
, independent of
N
, such that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, one has

0\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\biggl[\exp\biggl\{-\alpha\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr\}\biggr]\mathbin{\leqslant}K\;.

(4.3.11)

Proof:
.

By the definition of scaled Hermite polynomials,

H_{4}(\phi_{N}(x);C_{N})=\bigl(\phi_{N}(x)^{2}-3C_{N}^{2}\bigr)^{2}-6C_{N}^{2}\;,

(4.3.12)

which shows that

\mathbf{X}_{N}:=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}\dd\!x\mathbin{\geqslant}-6C_{N}^{2}=:-D_{N}\;.

(4.3.13)

Since
{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{X}_{N}\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\bigr]\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{\mathbf{X}_{N}\mathbin{\geqslant}0\bigr\}\mathbin{\leqslant}1
, it is sufficient to bound

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{X}_{N}<0}\bigr]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{\mathbf{X}<0\bigr\}+\alpha\intop\nolimits_{0}^{\infty}\e^{\alpha t}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{-\mathbf{X}_{N}>t\bigr\}\dd\!t

(4.3.14)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}\e^{\alpha}+\intop\nolimits_{1}^{\infty}\e^{\alpha t}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{-\mathbf{X}_{N}>t\bigr\}\dd\!t\;.

(4.3.15)

If
t\mathbin{\geqslant}D_{N}
, then
{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{-\mathbf{X}_{N}>t\bigr\}=0
. Otherwise
we have, for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
and any choice of even
p(t)
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{-\mathbf{X}_{N}>t\bigr\}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{\mathbf{X}_{M}-\mathbf{X}_{N}>t-D_{M}\bigr\}

(4.3.16)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{\lvert\mathbf{X}_{M}-\mathbf{X}_{N}\rvert^{2p(t)}>\lvert t-D_{M}\rvert^{2p(t)}\bigr\}\;.

(4.3.17)

We now apply this inequality with
M=M(t)
satisfying

t-D_{M(t)}\mathbin{\geqslant}1\;,

(4.3.18)

which by (
4.3.5
) is possible with
\log(M(t))
of order
t^{1/2}
, and
implies
M(t)<N
. By Markov’s inequality and Lemma
4.3.1
with
n=4
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{-\mathbf{X}_{N}>t\bigr\}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl[\lvert\mathbf{X}_{M}(t)-\mathbf{X}_{N}\rvert^{p(t)}\bigr]

(4.3.19)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}K_{4}\bigl(p(t)-1\bigr)^{2p(t)}\frac{(\log N)^{4p(t)}}{N^{2p(t)}}

(4.3.20)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\leqslant}K(\eta)\frac{(p(t)-1)^{2p(t)}}{M(t)^{2(1-\eta)p(t)}}

(4.3.21)

with a finite
K(\eta)
for any
\eta>0
.
Choosing
p(t)
of order
t^{\beta}
for
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
, we obtain

\log\Bigl(\e^{\alpha t}{\mathbb{P}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\bigl\{-\mathbf{X}_{N}>t\bigr\}\Bigr)\mathbin{\leqslant}\alpha t+c_{1}\beta t^{\beta}\log(t)-c_{2}(1-\eta)t^{\beta+1/2}

(4.3.22)

for constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
. Since the term
-c_{2}(1-\eta)t^{\beta+1/2}

dominates for large
t
, this leads to a convergent integral
in (
4.3.15
).
∎

As a consequence, we obtain the following analogue of Proposition
4.2.3
,
by the same proof.

{corollary}

[Asymptotic series]

For every
n\mathbin{\geqslant}0
and
N\mathbin{\geqslant}1
, there exists a constant
M_{n}
, independent of
N
,
such that the ratio of partition functions satisfies

\biggl|\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{2,\alpha,N}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{2,0,N}}-\sumop\displaylimits_{m=0}^{n}\frac{(-\alpha)^{m}}{m!}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{2,0,N}}\biggl[\biggl(\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}\dd\!x\biggr)^{m}\biggr]\biggr|\mathbin{\leqslant}M_{n}\alpha^{n+1}\;.

(4.3.23)

As a consequence, we obtain again an expansion of the form

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{2,\alpha,N}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{2,0,N}}=1+12\alpha^{2}\Pi_{N}\Bigl(\,\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox to20.77pt{\vbox to11.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-5.69046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\Bigr)+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{4})

(4.3.24)

for the ration of partition functions, and a similar expansion holds for
the two-point function and other random variables. The main difference with
the one-dimensional case, besides the fact that the model itself depends
on
C_{N}
, is that the valuations of Feynman diagrams depend
on
N
, via the cut-off Green function
G_{N}
, given by (
3.3.29
).
One can however show that these valuations all converge to a finite
limit as
N\to\infty
.

{remark}

A non-perturbative proof of the existence of the ratio of partition
functions (
4.3.24
), based on a Girsanov formula, has been obtained
by Barashkov and Gubinelli
[
2
]
.

4.4
The
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model*

We consider now the
\Phi^{4}
model on the three-dimensional torus

\Lambda=\mathbb{T}^{3}
. One can show that the Green function now behaves as

G(x)\asymp\frac{1}{\lVert x\rVert}\;,

(4.4.1)

while the variance of the Gaussian free field satisfies

C_{N}=G_{N}(0)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}\asymp N

(4.4.2)

with
{\mathscr{K}}_{N}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\colon\lvert k\rvert\mathbin{\leqslant}N\}
. One also checks that
the truncated Green function satisfies

G_{N}(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in{\mathscr{K}}_{N}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}e_{k}(x)\asymp\frac{1}{\lVert x\rVert+N^{-1}}\;.

(4.4.3)

A natural guess would be that the energy (
4.3.3
), transposed
to the three-dimensional setting, would still lead to well-defined asymptotic
expansions of expectations. Unfortunately, this is not the case. The actual
result is as follows.

{theorem}

[Renormalisation of the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model]

Define the energy by

{\mathscr{H}}_{3,\alpha,N}^{\Phi^{4}}(\phi_{N})=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}\biggl[\lVert\nabla\phi_{N}(x)\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\phi_{N}(x)^{2}+\alpha{\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}+\beta_{N}(\alpha){\mathpunct{:}\phi_{N}(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}+\gamma_{N}(\alpha)\biggr]\dd\!x\;,

(4.4.4)

where the additional counterterms are

\displaystyle\beta_{N}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=-48\alpha^{2}\Pi_{N}\bigl(\,\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox to20.77pt{\vbox to11.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-5.69046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(4.4.6)

Then the
n
-point functions admit asymptotic expansions in
\alpha
,
all of whose terms are uniformly bounded in the cut-off
N
.

The counterterm
\beta_{N}(\alpha)
is called
mass renormalisation
,
while the counterterm
\gamma_{N}(\alpha)
is called
energy renormalisation
.
The latter is not crucial for most computations, since it will cancel out
when taking ratios of partition functions. These counterterms were not
needed in dimension
2
, because their value actually converges to finite
limits as
N\to\infty
. However, as we shall see in Section
4.4.1
,
they diverge in dimension
3
, either like
N
or like
\log N
. This is
a symptom of the fact that the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure is not absolutely
continuous with respect to the three-dimensional Gaussian free field.

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 25.59 0 M 27.16 0 C 27.16 0.87 26.46 1.57 25.59 1.57 C 24.72 1.57 24.02 0.87 24.02 0 C 24.02 -0.87 24.72 -1.57 25.59 -1.57 C 26.46 -1.57 27.16 -0.87 27.16 0 Z M 25.59 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\Bigr)
scale with
N
as
N\to\infty
,
for
d=3
, by using (
4.4.3
) and spherical coordinates.
Compare with the case
d=2
.

Theorem
4.4
is an important result in Euclidean Quantum Field theory,
which has a long history. The earliest works by Glimm and Jaffe and by Feldman
approached the problem via a detailed combinatorial analysis of Feynman
diagrams
[
23
,
24
,
21
,
25
]
. The
works
[
3
,
4
]
introduced the idea of using a
renormalisation group approach, consisting in a decomposition of the covariance
of the GFF into scales, which then allows to integrate successively over
one scale after the other. This method was further perfected
in
[
12
]
, using polymers to control error terms, an
approach based on ideas from Statistical Physics
[
26
]
.

In another direction, the approach provided
in
[
13
,
14
]
allows
to bound correlation functions without having to compute the partition
function explicitly, by using the Schwinger–Dyson equations (see also
Remark
4.2.4
). This involves the derivation of so-called skeleton
inequalities, which were obtained up to third order
in
[
13
]
, and later extended to all orders
in
[
9
]
. A relatively compact
derivation of bounds on the partition function
based on a Girsanov formula was recently
obtained in
[
2
]
.

We are not going to present a full proof of Theorem
4.4
here,
as all its versions are quite technical. However, in the next sections, we
shall outline some key ideas of a proof.

4.4.1
Hepp sectors and subdivergences*

In this section, we provide a simple way to determine whether
the value of a vacuum diagram diverges as
N\to\infty
, or not.
Given a diagram
\Gamma=({\mathscr{V}},{\mathscr{E}})
, define its degree by

\deg(\Gamma):=d(\lvert{\mathscr{V}}\rvert-1)-(d-2)\lvert{\mathscr{E}}\rvert\;.

(4.4.7)

We will call a diagram
divergent
if
\deg(\Gamma)\mathbin{\leqslant}0
.
Here we have defined the degree for a general dimension
d
, though the main
focus of this section is the case
d=3
.

{exercise}

Compute the degrees of the diagrams occurring in Theorem
4.4

for
d=2
and
d=3
.

x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
x_{4}
x_{5}
\Lambda
1
2
3
4
5
{\mathbf{n}}_{\varnothing}=0
{\mathbf{n}}_{b}=1
{\mathbf{n}}_{a}=2
{\mathbf{n}}_{c}=3
T

Figure 4.4:
A point configuration
x\in\Lambda^{5}
, its minimal spanning tree
(left), and the associated Hepp tree
{\mathbf{T}}=(T,{\mathbf{n}})
(right). The inner
nodes of
T
are labelled
\varnothing,a,b,c
. For instance,
{\mathbf{n}}_{1\wedge 2}={\mathbf{n}}_{a}=2
, so that
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are at a distance of order
2^{-2}
, while

{\mathbf{n}}_{3\wedge 5}={\mathbf{n}}_{b}=1
, so that
x_{3}
and
x_{5}
are at a distance of order

2^{-1}
.

The following theorem was obtained by Weinberg
[
37
]
.

{theorem}

[Criterion for non-divergence]

Assume
G_{N}(x)\asymp(\lVert x\rVert+N^{-1})^{d-2}
.
If
\Gamma
satisfies
\deg(\bar{\Gamma})>0
for any subgraph
\bar{\Gamma}

of
\Gamma
, then
\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)
is bounded uniformly in
N
.

This result can be proved quite easily, using an idea introduced by
Hepp
[
30
]
.

{definition}

[Hepp sector]
Fix a constant
C>1
. Let
T
be a binary tree with
m=\lvert{\mathscr{V}}\rvert
leaves, and
let
{\mathbf{n}}
be a node decoration on the vertices of
T
, which is
non-decreasing on any path from the root of
T
to a leaf of
T
. We write

{\mathbf{T}}=(T,{\mathbf{n}})
and define a subset of
\Lambda^{m}
by

D_{{\mathbf{T}}}:=\Bigl\{x\in\Lambda^{m}\colon C^{-1}2^{-{\mathbf{n}}_{i\wedge j}}\mathbin{\leqslant}\lVert x_{i}-x_{j}\rVert\mathbin{\leqslant}C2^{-{\mathbf{n}}_{i\wedge j}}\;\forall i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\Bigr\}\;,

(4.4.8)

where
i\wedge j
denotes the last common ancestor of
i
and
j
in the tree

T
. Then
D_{{\mathbf{T}}}
is called the
Hepp sector
associated with
{\mathbf{T}}
.

Given a point configuration
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\Lambda^{m}
, one can associate
with it a Hepp sector
D_{{\mathbf{T}}}
in the following way (Figure
4.4
).
One starts by finding a minimal spanning tree of
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
. Pairs of
points which are closest are children of a common node in
T
, which is
labelled by the power of
2
which is closest to the length of the edge in the spanning
tree. The construction is then iterated until all leaves of
T
are connected
to the root.

Proof of Theorem
4.4.1
.

One can check that for sufficiently large
C
,
\Lambda^{m}
is covered by the
union of all Hepp sectors
D_{{\mathbf{T}}}
. The bound on
G_{N}(x)
implies

\lvert\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)\rvert\lesssim\sumop\displaylimits_{T,{\mathbf{n}}}\intop\nolimits_{D_{T,{\mathbf{n}}}}\prodop\displaylimits_{e\in{\mathscr{E}}}\frac{1}{\bigl(\lVert x_{e_{+}}-x_{e_{-}}\rVert+N^{-1}\bigr)^{d-2}}\dd\!x\;.

(4.4.9)

It follows from the definition of Hepp sectors that the term

\lVert x_{e_{+}}-x_{e_{-}}\rVert
in (
4.4.9
) is bounded below by

\smash{C^{-1}2^{(d-2){\mathbf{n}}_{e^{\uparrow}}}}
, where
\smash{e^{\uparrow}:=e_{+}\wedge e_{-}}

denotes the last common ancestor of the two vertices incident to
e
. We
thus obtain that, uniformly in the cut-off
N
, one has

\lvert\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)\rvert\lesssim\sumop\displaylimits_{T,{\mathbf{n}}}\prodop\displaylimits_{e\in{\mathscr{E}}}2^{(d-2){\mathbf{n}}_{e^{\uparrow}}}\intop\nolimits_{D_{T,{\mathbf{n}}}}\dd\!x\;.

(4.4.10)

The volume of the Hepp sector, given by the integral over
D_{T,{\mathbf{n}}}
, is
easily seen to have order
\prodop\displaylimits_{v\in T}2^{-d{\mathbf{n}}_{v}}
, where the product runs
over all inner nodes of
T
. It follows that

\lvert\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)\rvert\lesssim\sumop\displaylimits_{T,{\mathbf{n}}}\prodop\displaylimits_{v\in T}2^{-\eta_{v}{\mathbf{n}}_{v}}\;,\quad\text{where}\quad\eta_{v}:=d-(d-2)\sumop\displaylimits_{e\in{\mathscr{E}}}\mathbbm{1}_{e^{\uparrow}}(v)\;.

(4.4.11)

Let
\mathbin{\geqslant}
be the partial order on inner vertices of
T
given by descendence:

w\mathbin{\geqslant}v
if and only if the unique path from the root of
T
to
w
contains

v
. We claim that

\sumop\displaylimits_{w\mathbin{\geqslant}v}\eta_{w}>0

(4.4.12)

holds uniformly in
v\in T
. Indeed, this expression is the degree of a
subgraph of
\Gamma
, which is positive by assumption. Using this observation,
it is not difficult to show that
\lvert\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)\rvert
is uniformly bounded, by
induction starting from the leaves of
T
.
∎

{example}

Consider the case of the tree depicted in Figure
4.4
, with the
inner vertices of
T
denoted
\varnothing,a,b,c
. We have
\eta_{a}>0
,

\eta_{c}>0
,
\eta_{b}+\eta_{c}>0
and
\eta_{\varnothing}+\eta_{a}+\eta_{b}+\eta_{c}>0
(this last sum being the degree of
\Gamma
). The sum over all node
decorations is given by

\sumop\displaylimits_{{\mathbf{n}}_{\varnothing}\mathbin{\geqslant}0}2^{-\eta_{\varnothing}}\sumop\displaylimits_{{\mathbf{n}}_{a}\mathbin{\geqslant}{\mathbf{n}}_{\varnothing}}2^{-\eta_{a}{\mathbf{n}}_{a}}\sumop\displaylimits_{{\mathbf{n}}_{b}\mathbin{\geqslant}{\mathbf{n}}_{\varnothing}}2^{-\eta_{b}{\mathbf{n}}_{b}}\sumop\displaylimits_{{\mathbf{n}}_{c}\mathbin{\geqslant}{\mathbf{n}}_{b}}2^{-\eta_{c}{\mathbf{n}}_{c}}\;.

(4.4.13)

Performing first the sums over
{\mathbf{n}}_{a}
and
{\mathbf{n}}_{c}
, then the sum over
{\mathbf{n}}_{b}
,
and finally the sum over
{\mathbf{n}}_{\varnothing}
yields indeed a finite quantity.
Since there are finally many binary trees with
5
leaves, the result follows.

The examples seen in Exercice
4.4.1
may suggest that the large-
N

behaviour of
\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)
is governed by the degree of
\Gamma
, in the
sense that
\lvert\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)\rvert
is bounded uniformly in
N
if

\deg(\Gamma)>0
, diverges like
\log(N)
if
\deg(\Gamma)=0
,
and diverges like
N^{-\deg(\Gamma)}
if
\deg(\Gamma)<0
.
Unfortunately, the reality is a bit more complex. For example, we have

\deg\Bigl(\,\raisebox{-5.5pt}{\hbox to20.77pt{\vbox to21.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-5.69046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

(4.4.14)

which is strictly positive for
d\mathbin{\leqslant}3
. However, the diagram contains the
subgraph

, which is divergent in dimension
3
. As a result, one
can show that the diagram is divergent as well. This is an instance
of a
subdivergence
, which is a serious source of complication for
the analysis. Note that this does not contradict Theorem
4.4.1
.

4.4.2
BPHZ renormalisation*

BPHZ renormalisation, named after Bogoliubov, Parasiuk, Hepp, and
Zimmermann
[
8
,
30
,
38
]
is a procedure
allowing to systematically analyse the divergent behavior of diagrams
that are either divergent, or contain sub-divergences, and to
determine the counterterms needed to obtain well-defined asymptotic
expansions. The key result is the following.

{theorem}

[BPHZ renormalisation]

There exists a linear map
{\mathscr{A}}
, acting on Feynman diagrams, such that

\Pi_{N}({\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma))\asymp\begin{cases}N^{-\deg(\Gamma)}&\text{if $\deg(\Gamma)<0$\;,}\\
\log(N)^{\zeta}&\text{if $\deg(\Gamma)=0$\;,}\end{cases}

(4.4.15)

for a finite integer
\zeta
, while
\Pi_{N}({\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma))
is bounded
uniformly in
N
if
\deg(\Gamma)>0
.

For a modern exposition, see
[
28
]
. Slightly sharper bounds
have been obtained for a different model in
[
5
]
. While the proof
is quite involved, the basic mechanism can be understood in a simple example.

{example}

Consider again the diagram in (
4.4.14
). In that case,

{\mathscr{A}}\Bigl(\,\raisebox{-5.5pt}{\hbox to20.77pt{\vbox to21.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.13791pt\lower-5.69046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 12.8 19.69 M 14.37 19.69 C 14.37 20.55 13.66 21.26 12.8 21.26 C 11.93 21.26 11.22 20.55 11.22 19.69 C 11.22 18.82 11.93 18.11 12.8 18.11 C 13.66 18.11 14.37 18.82 14.37 19.69 Z M 12.8 19.69}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope ;
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\biggr)=-\iiintop\nolimits_{\Lambda^{3}}G_{N}(x_{2}-x_{1})^{3}G_{N}(x_{3}-x_{2})\bigl[G_{N}(x_{3}-x_{2})-G_{N}(x_{3}-x_{1})\bigr]\dd\!x_{1}\dd\!x_{2}\dd\!x_{3}\;.

(4.4.18)

The crucial observation is that by Taylor’s formula,

\lvert G_{N}(x_{3}-x_{2})-G_{N}(x_{3}-x_{1})\rvert\lesssim\lvert(x_{2}-x_{1})\cdot\nabla G_{N}(x_{3}-x_{1})\rvert\lesssim\frac{\lVert x_{2}-x_{1}\rVert}{(\lVert x_{3}-x_{1}\rVert+N^{-1})^{2}}\;.

(4.4.19)

If
\lVert x_{2}-x_{1}\rVert\ll\lVert x_{3}-x_{1}\rVert
, this is smaller than the
contribution of
G_{N}(x_{3}-x_{1})
to (
4.4.17
).
This gain is enough to make the integral (
4.4.18
)
convergent.

The linear map
{\mathscr{A}}
in (
4.4.15
) has an algebraic meaning: it is actually
the so-called
antipode

of the
Connes–Kreimer extraction–contraction Hopf
algebra

[
17
,
18
]
. To explain its
construction, we introduce the following sets:

•

\mathbf{F}
is the set of all
connected
multigraphs whose vertices
have arity
2
,
3
or
4
(that is,
2
,
3
or
4
edges meet at each vertex);

•

\mathbf{F_{-}}\subset\mathbf{F}
is the subset of all divergent multigraphs in
\mathbf{F}
;

•

{\mathscr{F}}
is the algebra generated by
\mathbf{F}
with respect to the disjoint union
product
\cdot
– note that
{\mathscr{F}}
also contains
non-connected
Feynman diagrams;

•

\mathscr{F}_{-}\subset{\mathscr{F}}
is the subalgebra of
{\mathscr{F}}
generated by
\mathbf{F_{-}}
;
in particular, for any
\Gamma\in\mathscr{F}_{-}
, all connected components are divergent.

We also denote by
\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle
,
\langle\mathbf{F_{-}}\rangle
,
\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle
,
\langle\mathscr{F}_{-}\rangle
the linear spans,
respectively, of
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F_{-}}
,
{\mathscr{F}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{-}
.
The neutral element for multiplication is the empty graph, which we denote by
\mathbf{1}
.
Note that the valuation
\Pi_{N}
is multiplicative, meaning that

\Pi_{N}(\Gamma_{1}\cdot\Gamma_{2})=\Pi_{N}(\Gamma_{1})\Pi_{N}(\Gamma_{2})\qquad\text{for all $\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\in{\mathscr{F}}$\;.}

(4.4.20)

{definition}

[Connes–Kreimer extraction-contraction coproduct]
The
Connes–Kreimer extraction-contraction
coproduct

\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}:\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle\to\langle\mathscr{F}_{-}\rangle\otimes\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle
is defined by

\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)=\Gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\Gamma+\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{1}\neq\overline{\Gamma}\subsetneq\Gamma,\overline{\Gamma}\in\mathbf{F_{-}}\end{subarray}}\overline{\Gamma}\otimes(\Gamma/\overline{\Gamma})\;,

(4.4.21)

where the sum ranges over all
divergent
subgraphs
\overline{\Gamma}
, and

\Gamma/\overline{\Gamma}
denotes the graph obtained by replacing
\overline{\Gamma}

by a single vertex. The subgraphs have to be
full
, in the sense that if an edge

e
belongs to
\overline{\Gamma}
, all edges connecting the same vertices also belong
to
\overline{\Gamma}
.

The coproduct can be extended multiplicatively to a map

\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}:\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle\to\langle\mathscr{F}_{-}\rangle\otimes\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\;.

(4.4.22)

We endow
\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle
with two more linear maps. A
counit

\mathbf{1}^{\star}:\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle\to\mathbb{R}
, given by projection on the unit
\mathbf{1}
, and an antipode, defined as follows.

{definition}

[Antipode]
The
antipode

{\mathscr{A}}:\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle\to\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle
is defined inductively by
{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbf{1})=\mathbf{1}
and

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=-\Gamma-\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{1}\neq\overline{\Gamma}\subsetneq\Gamma,\overline{\Gamma}\in\mathbf{F_{-}}\end{subarray}}{\mathscr{A}}(\overline{\Gamma})\cdot(\Gamma/\overline{\Gamma})

(4.4.23)

\displaystyle=-\Gamma-{\mathscr{M}}({\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\;.

(4.4.24)

Here
\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}=\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}-\Gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}\otimes\Gamma

denotes the
reduced coproduct
, and the map
{\mathscr{M}}:\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle\otimes\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle\to\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle
denotes multiplication, defined
by
{\mathscr{M}}(\Gamma_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2})=\Gamma_{1}\cdot\Gamma_{2}
.

Both the counit
\mathbf{1}^{\star}
and the antipode
{\mathscr{A}}
can be extended multiplicatively
to the whole algebra
{\mathscr{F}}
. The space
({\mathscr{F}},\cdot,\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}},\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}^{\star},{\mathscr{A}})
constructed
in this way is a Hopf algebra, called
Connes–Kreimer extraction-contraction Hopf algebra
.
This means in particular that we have

\displaystyle(\id\otimes\Delta)\Delta\Gamma

\displaystyle=(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta\Gamma\;,

(4.4.25)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{M}}({\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\Delta\Gamma

\displaystyle={\mathscr{M}}(\id\otimes{\mathscr{A}})\Delta\Gamma=\mathbf{1}^{\star}(\Gamma)\mathbf{1}

(4.4.26)

for all
\Gamma\in{\mathscr{F}}
. We have already encountered the first property,
called
co-associativity
, in the case of polynomials, see Remark
1.2.4
.

To define BPHZ renormalisation, we first introduce the
twisted antipode
,
defined as

\tilde{\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)={\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)1_{\deg\Gamma\mathbin{\leqslant}0}\;.

(4.4.27)

Note that if
\deg(\Gamma)\mathbin{\leqslant}0
, then one has

\tilde{\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)=-\Gamma-{\mathscr{M}}(\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\;,

(4.4.28)

because
\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}
produces only divergent terms on the left of the tensor product.

A
character
on
\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle
is a linear map
g:\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle\to\mathbb{R}
which is multiplicative
in the sense that we have
g(\Gamma_{1}\cdot\Gamma_{2})=g(\Gamma_{1})g(\Gamma_{2})
for all
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\in\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle
.
With any character
g
, one can associate a linear map

M^{g}
defined by

M^{g}(\Gamma)=(g\otimes\id)\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\;,

(4.4.29)

and the set of these maps is known to form a group.
The
BPHZ character
is the linear map
g^{\text{BPHZ}}:\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle\to\mathbb{R}
given by

g^{\text{BPHZ}}(\Gamma)=\Pi_{N}\tilde{\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)\;.

(4.4.30)

The fact that
g^{\text{BPHZ}}
is indeed a character follows from multiplicativity
of
{\mathscr{A}}
and
\Pi_{N}
. The map
\smash{M^{g^{\text{BPHZ}}}}
is called

BPHZ renormalisation map
.
It defines a
renormalised valuation
given by

\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}(\Gamma)=\Pi_{N}M^{g^{\text{BPHZ}}}(\Gamma)=(g^{\text{BPHZ}}\otimes\Pi_{N})\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)=(\Pi_{N}\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\Pi_{N})\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\;.

(4.4.31)

The interest of this construction is the following result.

{lemma}

The BPHZ renormalised valuation satisfies

\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}(\Gamma)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if\/ $\deg\Gamma\mathbin{\leqslant}0$\;,}\\
-\Pi_{N}{\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)&\text{if\/ $\deg\Gamma>0$\;.}\end{cases}

(4.4.32)

Proof:
.

In the case
\deg\Gamma\mathbin{\leqslant}0
, using (
4.4.28
) we get

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}(\Gamma)={}

\displaystyle(\Pi_{N}\otimes\Pi_{N})(\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\bigl[\Gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\Gamma+\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\bigr]

(4.4.33)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(\Pi_{N}\otimes\Pi_{N})\bigl[\tilde{\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\Gamma+(\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\bigr]

(4.4.34)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(\Pi_{N}\otimes\Pi_{N})\bigl[-\Gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}-{\mathscr{M}}(\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\Gamma+(\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\bigr]\;,

(4.4.35)

which vanishes by multiplicativity of
\Pi_{N}
.
In the case
\deg\Gamma>0
, using
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)=0
in the second line of the
above computation, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=(\Pi_{N}\otimes\Pi_{N})\bigl[\mathbf{1}\otimes\Gamma+(\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)\bigr]

(4.4.36)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)+(\Pi_{N}\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\Pi_{N})\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)

(4.4.37)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{N}(\Gamma)+\Pi_{N}{\mathscr{M}}(\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\otimes\id)\mathring{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}(\Gamma)

(4.4.38)

again by multiplicativity of
\Pi_{N}
. This is equal to
-\Pi_{N}{\mathscr{A}}(\Gamma)

by (
4.4.24
).
∎

If follows from Theorem
4.4.2
that the renormalized valuation
\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}

is bounded uniformly in the cut-off
N
for any
\Gamma\in\langle\mathscr{F}\rangle
.

4.4.3
Wick map*

We now indicate how the above results allow to prove Theorem
4.4
.
We again focus on the problem of computing the ratio of partition
functions. Writing as before

\mathbf{X}=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{4}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}\dd\!x\;,\qquad\mathbf{Y}=\intop\nolimits_{\Lambda}{\mathpunct{:}\phi(x)^{2}\mathpunct{:}}_{C_{N}}\dd\!x\;,

(4.4.39)

this ratio is given by

\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{3,\alpha,N}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{3,0,N}}={\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{3,0,N}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}-\gamma}\bigr]=\e^{-\gamma}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{3,0,N}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}}\bigr]\;,

(4.4.40)

where
\beta=\beta_{N}(\alpha)
and
\gamma=\gamma_{N}(\alpha)
.
A naive approach would be to expand first the exponential, and then
use Newton’s binomial formula to get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{3,0,N}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}}\bigr]

\displaystyle\asymp\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\frac{1}{n!}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{3,0,N}}\bigl[(-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y})^{n}\bigr]

(4.4.41)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(-\alpha)^{k}}{k!}\frac{(-\beta)^{n-k}}{(n-k)!}{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{3,0,N}}\bigl[\mathbf{X}^{k}\mathbf{Y}^{n-k}\bigr]\;.

(4.4.42)

The first terms of this expansion are

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{3,0,N}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}}\bigr]\asymp 1

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 23.62 0 L 11.81 20.47 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\bigr)+\dots\;.

(4.4.45)

The problem is that some Feynman diagrams are divergent, while others
are not, and
\beta
, being of order
\alpha^{2}\log(N)
is itself
divergent. Therefore, the whole asymptotic series can have non-divergent
coefficients only if there are many cancellations between divergent
terms. The combinatorics of this is very hard to keep track of.

An alternative reformulation is as follows. We introduce a
linear map
{\mathscr{P}}:\mathbb{R}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}]\to\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle
associating with a monomial

\mathbf{X}^{n}\mathbf{Y}^{m}
all possible diagrams obtained by perfect pairwise matchings
of
n
vertices with
4
legs each and
m
vertices with
2
legs each,
and projecting on connected diagrams. In this way, by the linked-cluster
theorem,

\log{\mathbb{E}\rule{0.0pt}{9.0pt}}^{\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{3,0,N}}\bigl[\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}}\bigr]=\Pi_{N}\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}})\;.

(4.4.46)

The following theorem, inspired by ideas in
[
20
,
11
]
,
has been proved in
[
6
]
.

{theorem}

[Commutative diagram]

There exist linear maps
\W:\mathbb{R}[\mathbf{X}]\to\mathbb{R}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}]
,
and
\Theta_{\textup{F}}:\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle\to\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle
such that the following
diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}[\mathbf{X}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\mathbb{R}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Pi_{N}\tilde{{\mathscr{A}}}\otimes\id)\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}+\Theta_{\textup{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}}}

(4.4.47)

The map
\W
, called
Wick map
, satisfies

\W(\mathbf{X}^{n})=H_{n}(\mathbf{X};-\beta\mathbf{Y})\quad\forall n\mathbin{\geqslant}2\qquad\text{and}\qquad\W(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})=\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}}\;,

(4.4.48)

where
H_{n}
is the
n
th scaled Hermite polynomial,
while the map
\Theta_{\textup{F}}
satisfies

(\Pi_{N}\Theta_{\textup{F}}\circ{\mathscr{P}})(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})=-(\Pi_{N}\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\circ{\mathscr{P}})(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})=\gamma\;.

(4.4.49)

The main idea of this result is that the complicated map

(\Pi_{N}\tilde{{\mathscr{A}}}\otimes\id)\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}
, acting on Feynman diagrams, can be
replaced by the much simpler map
\W
, acting on polynomials. The Wick map
is of the form

\W=(\exp_{\ast}(-\kappa)\otimes\id)\Delta

(4.4.50)

that we have encountered in (
1.2.89
), where

\kappa(x^{n})=\begin{cases}\beta\mathbf{Y}&\text{if $n=2$\;,}\\
0&\text{otherwise\;.}\end{cases}

(4.4.51)

The intuition behind this is that the only subdivergences are graphs
containing one or several “bubbles”

as subgraphs.
The effect of the map
(\Pi_{N}\tilde{{\mathscr{A}}}\otimes\id)\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}
is to
extract bubbles and replace them by
\beta
times a vertex of arity

2
, which can be seen as replacing
\mathbf{X}^{2}
by
\beta\mathbf{Y}
. This is also
compatible with the combinatorial interpretation of Hermite polynomials
we have seen in Section
1.2.5
.
As for the relation
\W(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})=\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}}
,
it is a consequence of Proposition
1.2.4
.
One should note that we are working here with a “second
level Wick renormalisation”, the first level being associated
with using Wick powers in the energy, and the second level taking
care of the remaining subdivergences.

Together with the definition (
4.4.31
) of the BPHZ valuation,
Theorem
4.4.3
implies that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}}(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}}(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Pi_{N}\tilde{{\mathscr{A}}}\otimes\id)\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}+\Theta_{\textup{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}+\Pi_{N}\Theta_{\textup{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{N}}

(4.4.52)

This has the following consequence. On the one hand, (
4.4.40
)
and (
4.4.46
) show that

\log\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{3,\alpha,N}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{3,0,N}}=\Pi_{N}\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}})-\gamma\;.

(4.4.53)

On the other hand, by commutativity and (
4.4.49
), we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}})

\displaystyle=\bigl(\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}+\Pi_{N}\Theta_{\textup{F}}\bigr)\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})

(4.4.54)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})+\gamma\;.

(4.4.55)

It follows that

\log\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{3,\alpha,N}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{3,0,N}}=\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})\asymp\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}1}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})\;.

(4.4.56)

Now Lemma
4.4.2
shows that
\Pi_{N}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})
is different
from
0
only if
{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})
has strictly positive degree, in which case
it is equal to
-\Pi_{N}{\mathscr{A}}\circ{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})
. By Theorem
4.4.2
,
these terms are bounded uniformly in
N
. This completes the proof
of Theorem
4.4
in the case of the ratio of partition functions.

In fact, all Feynman diagrams in
{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})
have
n
vertices and
2n

edges, as they result from pairing
4n
legs, or half-edges. Therefore,

\deg({\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n}))=3(n-1)-2n=n-3\;,

(4.4.57)

which is strictly positive for all
n\mathbin{\geqslant}4
. It follows that the
sum (
4.4.56
) starts at
n=4
. On the other hand, the terms
of order
\alpha^{2}
and
\alpha^{3}
in (
4.4.45
) are
compensated by
\gamma
.

4.5
The
\Phi^{4}_{4-\varepsilon}
model*

Now that the
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model is understood in dimensions
1
,
2
and
3
,
one may wonder what happens in higher dimensions. In fact, Fröhlich has
shown
[
22
]
that the model in trivial for any
d>4
,
while Aizenmann and Duminil–Copin have shown
[
1
]

that it is trivial for
d=4
as well, for any reasonable renormalisation procedure.
Here
trivial
means that in the limit
N\to\infty
, the
n
-point functions
are the same as for the Gaussian model, as given by Isserlis’ theorem.

This leaves the question of what happens in dimensions
d\in(3,4)
. Non-integer
dimensions in that interval can be interpreted as working on the three-dimensional torus,
but changing the behaviour of the Green function to

G(x)\asymp\frac{1}{\lVert x\rVert^{d-2}}\;.

(4.5.1)

The degree of
{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})
, computed via (
4.4.7
), becomes

\deg{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})=4n-(n+1)d\;.

(4.5.2)

This means that more diagrams in
{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})
can become divergent when

d
increases. In fact, we have

\deg{\mathscr{P}}(X^{n})\mathbin{\leqslant}0\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad d\mathbin{\geqslant}d^{*}_{\textup{e}}(n):=4-\frac{4}{n+1}\;.

(4.5.3)

Note that these threshold accumulate at
d=4
as
n\to\infty
.
Furthermore, one can check that new subdivergences appear whenever
d

crosses one of the thresholds

d^{*}_{\textup{m}}(n)=d^{*}_{\textup{e}}(2n-1)=4-\frac{2}{n}\;.

(4.5.4)

Table
4.1
shows the first few of these new subdivergences.
We introduce the notations

n^{*}_{\textup{e}}(d)=\biggl\lfloor\frac{d}{4-d}\biggr\rfloor\qquad\text{and}\qquad n^{*}_{\textup{m}}(d)=\biggl\lfloor\frac{2}{4-d}\biggr\rfloor

(4.5.5)

for the inverse thresholds of
d^{*}_{\textup{m}}(n)
and
d^{*}_{\textup{e}}(n)
.

Graphs

Degree

Critical
d

Minimal
n

6-2d

3=d^{*}_{\text{m}}(2)

4

10-3d

\frac{10}{3}=d^{*}_{\text{m}}(3)

5

14-4d

\frac{7}{2}=d^{*}_{\text{m}}(4)

6

Table 4.1:
List of the first divergent subdiagrams of the
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model, with their degree,
the value of
d
for which they become divergent, and the minimal value of
n
such that they
occur in
{\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n})
.

In order to deal with these subdivergences, we have to allow for more general
replacement rules than
\mathbf{X}^{2}\mapsto\beta\mathbf{Y}
. Therefore, the relevant Wick map

\W
no longer involves Hermite polynomials, but more general so-called

Bell polynomials
.

4.5.1
Bell polynomials*

Bell polynomials can be defined by starting with cumulants

\kappa(x^{n})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $n=1$\;,}\\
y_{n}&\text{otherwise\;,}\end{cases}

(4.5.6)

where the
y_{n}
are considered for now as parameters.
The associated Wick map is

\W(t,x)=\e^{tx-K(t)}=\exp\biggl\{tx-\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}2}y_{n}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\biggr\}\;,

(4.5.7)

where
K(t)=\Lambda(\kappa)(t)
, see (
1.2.59
).

{definition}

[Bell polynomials]
The Wick map
\W(t,x)
is the generating function of Bell polynomials,
in the sense that

\W(t,x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}0}B_{n}(x,-y_{2},\dots,-y_{n})\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\;.

(4.5.8)

B_{n}
is called the
n
th complete Bell polynomial
. It can be decomposed
as

B_{n}(x,-y_{2},\dots,-y_{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=1}^{n}B_{n,k}(x,-y_{2},\dots,-y_{n-k+1})\;,

(4.5.9)

where each
B_{n,k}
is the homogeneous part of degree
k
of
B_{n}
, and
is called
incomplete Bell polynomial
of order
(n,k)
.

Using the fact (see Section
1.2.4
) that

B_{n}(x,-y_{2},\dots,-y_{n})=\W(x^{n})=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\mu^{-1}(x^{k})x^{n-k}

(4.5.10)

where
\mu^{-1}=\exp_{\ast}(-\kappa)
, one finds the explicit expression

B_{n}(x,-y_{2},\dots,-y_{n})=n!\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{n}\sumop\displaylimits_{p=1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{p}}{p!}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{p}\mathbin{\geqslant}2\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{p}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{y_{n_{1}}}{n_{1}!}\dots\frac{y_{n_{p}}}{n_{p}!}\frac{x^{n-k}}{(n-k)!}\;.

(4.5.11)

The incomplete Bell polynomial
B_{n,k}
is simply the
k
th term in this sum.
In particular, comparing with the explicit expression (
2.2.5
)
of scaled Wick polynomials, we recover

B_{n}(x,-y_{2},0,\dots,0)=H_{n}(x;y_{2})\;.

(4.5.12)

Bell polynomials also have a simple combinatorial interpretation: the coefficients
of
B_{n,k}
count the number of partitions of a set of cardinality
n
into

k
subsets, whose sizes are encoded into the monomial.

{example}

One has

B_{5,3}(x,y_{2},y_{3})=15xy_{2}^{2}+10x^{2}y_{3}\;.

(4.5.13)

Therefore, there are
15

ways of partitioning a set of
5
elements into
3
subsets of sizes
1
,
2

and
2
, and
10
ways of partitioning it into
3
subsets of sizes
1
,
1

and
3
. The polynomial
B_{5,3}(x,y_{2},y_{3})
is also obtained by applying
the substitutions
x^{2}\mapsto y_{2}
and
x^{3}\mapsto y_{3}
to the monomial

x^{5}
in all possible ways, and keeping only terms of degree
3
.

4.5.2
Wick map*

Theorem
4.4.3
admits the following generalisation, also
shown in
[
6
]
.

{theorem}

[Commutative diagram]

There exist linear maps
\W:\mathbb{R}[\mathbf{X}]\to\mathbb{R}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}]

and
\Theta_{\textup{F}}:\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle\to\langle\mathbf{F}\rangle
,
and constants
\sigma_{n}(N)
, diverging like
N^{2-(4-d)n}
,
such that the diagram (
4.4.47
) commutes.
The map Wick map
\W
satisfies

\W(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})=\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}-\beta\mathbf{Y}}

(4.5.14)

where

\beta=\beta_{N,d}(\alpha)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n=2}^{n^{*}_{\textup{m}}(d)}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}\sigma_{n}(N)\;.

(4.5.15)

Furthermore,

\W(\mathbf{X}^{n})=B_{n}(\mathbf{X},-\sigma_{2}(N)\mathbf{Y},\dots,-\sigma_{n}(N)\mathbf{Y})

(4.5.16)

holds for any
n\mathbin{\geqslant}2
, where
B_{n}
is the
n
th complete Bell polynomial,
while the map
\Theta_{\textup{F}}
satisfies

(\Pi_{N}\Theta_{\textup{F}}\circ{\mathscr{P}})(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})=-(\Pi_{N}\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\circ{\mathscr{P}})(\e^{-\alpha\mathbf{X}})=\gamma\;.

(4.5.17)

The counterterms
\sigma_{n}(N)
can again be written explicitly in terms of
valuations of divergent Feynman diagrams, the first of which are shown
in Table
4.1
. For given
n
, these diagrams
have
n
vertices, which are either two vertices of arity
3
and
n-2

vertices of arity
4
, or one vertex of arity
2
, and
n-1
vertices of
arity
4
. Using the same arguments as in Section
4.4.3
,
we arrive at the following result.

{corollary}

[Renormalisation of the
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model with
d\in(3,4)
]
Let

\gamma=\gamma_{N,d}(\alpha)=-\sumop\displaylimits_{n=2}^{n^{*}_{\textup{e}}(d)}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}\Pi_{N}\tilde{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n}))\;.

(4.5.18)

Then the
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model with
d\in(3,4)
, mass counterterm
\beta

and energy counterterm
\gamma
satisfies

\log\frac{{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,\alpha,N}}{{\mathscr{Z}}_{d,0,N}}\asymp-\sumop\displaylimits_{n\mathbin{\geqslant}n^{*}_{\textup{e}}(d)}\frac{(-\alpha)^{n}}{n!}\Pi_{N}{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{P}}(\mathbf{X}^{n}))\;,

(4.5.19)

where the coefficients are bounded uniformly in the cut-off
N
.
